Progress summary Quality report

The Bridge of One

Eight levels and 140 problems that take you from “if 6 pens cost ₹72…” to Olympiad problems where nobody tells you which method to use. Each problem has a full explanation, and every explanation stays hidden until you decide to open it.

How to use this course

  1. Choose a level. Each level opens with its learning objective, the key ideas and the traps to watch for. Read these before the problems: they are short on purpose.
  2. Open a problem and work on it on paper, or in the squared box under the problem. Aim to understand, not to be fast.
  3. If you get stuck, open “Stuck? Show a nudge”. It gives one hint written for that problem and a short list of questions that often unlock problems. It never gives the answer away.
  4. When you have an answer, or have genuinely tried and cannot move, press “Reveal answer and explanation”. Read all of it, not just the final number. Most explanations include a check and a second method.
  5. Mark your own progress. “Mark as attempted” means you have worked on the problem. “Mark as completed” means you could now explain the solution to a friend without looking. Opening the answer is recorded separately and never counts as completed.
  6. Move with “Previous problem” and “Next problem”, or press P and N when you are not typing. Your marks and working stay in this browser on this device.

How the levels fit together

  • Levels 1 and 2 build the core idea: when every unit is worth the same amount, you can cross from what you know to what you need through “one” (or any convenient chunk), even when the numbers are fractions or decimals.
  • Level 3 turns the idea around. When a total is fixed (a job, a distance, a store of food), more of one thing means less of the other.
  • Level 4 asks the most important question of all: does the relationship actually hold? Fixed charges, gaps between events, ages and areas all break simple proportion.
  • Level 5 combines rates. When things work at the same time, what they do in one unit of time adds up (or subtracts, for leaks and drains).
  • Level 6 handles several quantities at once, one at a time, using combined units such as worker-hours.
  • Level 7 gives Olympiad problems without telling you the method. You must discover the model.
  • Level 8 mixes everything, with traps placed where a familiar method stops working.

Difficulty marks

Every problem shows a difficulty from 1 to 4: Foundation, Core, Challenge and Olympiad. Difficulty describes the thinking needed, not the size of the numbers.

What an explanation contains

Most explanations follow the same path: the final answer, what is happening, what stays the same, the reasoning step by step, a way to picture it, a check, another method and the general lesson to keep. Problems that ask you to judge, explain or invent something use headings that suit them instead.

For parents and teachers

  • The page works offline as a single file. It makes no network requests and sends nothing anywhere.
  • Progress and typed working are saved only in this browser on this device. Clearing browser data or opening the file on another device starts fresh. “Reset” clears progress after asking for confirmation.
  • The page never judges written work. The student decides when a problem is complete, and the progress page shows separately whether the answer was opened first.
  • Conventions: money is in rupees (₹), units are metric, and buildings are numbered ground floor, 1st floor, 2nd floor and so on. Whenever a problem needs an assumption such as equal workers or a steady rate, the problem states it. Large numbers use international commas: 1,200,000 is 12,00,000 in the Indian system.

Level 1 of 8

Direct proportion and the bridge of one

15 problems

Learning objective

Understand why a constant amount per unit lets you scale from one situation to another, and use the bridge of one to cross from a quantity you know to the quantity you need.

Key ideas

  • Direct proportion means the amount per unit never changes: every notebook costs the same, the printer prints the same number of pages every minute.
  • The bridge of one. Find the value for one unit first, then multiply. If 6 notebooks cost ₹138, one costs ₹138 ÷ 6 = ₹23, so 11 cost 11 × ₹23.
  • Why it works. If every unit adds the same amount, the total is (amount per unit) × (number of units). Double the units and you double the total.
  • The “one” can be any convenient chunk. If 24 bricks weigh 66 kg, then 8 bricks (a third of them) weigh 22 kg, so 16 bricks weigh 44 kg.
  • You can build the bridge from either side: kilometres per litre or litres per kilometre. Choose whichever makes the next step easy.
  • A table is proportional exactly when (second quantity ÷ first quantity) gives the same number in every row.

Watch out for

  • Adding instead of multiplying: going from 12 pancakes to 20 pancakes does not mean “8 more eggs”.
  • Assuming the amount per unit is constant when it is not (bulk prices, delivery charges, whole packs).
  • Forgetting to ask whether the answer should be bigger or smaller than the number you started with.

Level 2 of 8

Fractions, decimals, convenient units and rate comparison

15 problems

Learning objective

Use the bridge of one when the quantities are fractions or decimals, choose convenient units, convert between units, compare rates fairly and round in the way the situation demands.

Key ideas

  • Dividing by a fraction still means “how much for one whole unit?”. If 3 over 4 kg costs ₹540, then 1 over 4 kg costs ₹180 and 1 kg costs ₹720.
  • A rate is an amount per one unit of something else: rupees per kilogram, kilometres per hour, pages per minute.
  • To compare offers, put them on the same footing: the same unit price, or the price of the same quantity. Both methods must agree.
  • “Cost per gram” and “grams per rupee” carry the same information. A lower cost per gram always means more grams per rupee.
  • Converting units is the bridge of one again: 1 m/s means 3600 m in an hour, which is 3.6 km/h.
  • Rounding depends on the question. Buses and paint tins are rounded up, full boxes are rounded down, and answers built on rough data should not show false precision.

Watch out for

  • Multiplying when you should divide: if 2 over 5 of a tank is 48 L, the whole tank must be more than 48 L.
  • Comparing numbers that are in different units, such as m/s against km/h.
  • Rounding to the nearest whole number when the situation needs “at least” or “at most”.

Level 3 of 8

Inverse proportion and fixed totals

15 problems

Learning objective

Recognise when a total is fixed, identify exactly what that total is, and explain why increasing one quantity must decrease the other.

Key ideas

  • In inverse proportion two quantities multiply to a fixed total: workers × days = total work, speed × time = distance, students × days = food supply.
  • The fixed total is the bridge. 12 workers taking 15 days means the job is 180 worker-days of work. Any team must supply those 180 worker-days.
  • Doubling one quantity halves the other, because the product must stay the same.
  • Worker-days, student-days and similar units measure an amount of work or food in a way that does not depend on how it is shared out.
  • Inverse proportion depends on assumptions: equal workers, constant speed, the same daily ration. Say them out loud.

Watch out for

  • “Half the pumps, half the time.” With a fixed amount of water, fewer pumps take longer.
  • Calling any decreasing relationship “inverse proportion”. It is inverse only when the product stays constant.
  • Forgetting real limits: tiles must fit, and a hole has room for only so many diggers.

Level 4 of 8

Proportionality tests, hidden units and false assumptions

20 problems

Learning objective

Test whether a relationship is really proportional before using it, spot hidden units such as gaps, cuts and floors, and recognise when the information given is not enough.

Key ideas

  • A relationship is directly proportional only if doubling one quantity always doubles the other. A fixed charge, a starting amount or a fixed difference breaks this.
  • Hidden units. Clocks strike in gaps, logs are cut in cuts, stairs climb in flights. Count the thing that actually takes the time.
  • Simultaneous events do not add up: six shirts drying side by side take as long as one.
  • Area and volume scale faster than length. Double every length and the area becomes 4 times as large, the volume 8 times.
  • Ages keep a constant difference, not a constant ratio.
  • Two data points can fit more than one rule. When the rule is not stated, the honest answer may be “cannot be determined”.

Watch out for

  • Using a correct calculation inside a wrong model, for example running a marathon at sprinting speed.
  • Ignoring a fixed part, such as a call-out fee or a starting height.
  • Using every number in the question. Some information is irrelevant, and you should be able to say why.

Level 5 of 8

Combined rates: work, pipes, leaks, speed and average speed

20 problems

Learning objective

Explain why rates add when agents work at the same time, handle leaks and drains as negative rates, solve relative-speed and catching-up problems, and compute average speed correctly.

Key ideas

  • When agents work at the same time, what each does in one unit of time adds up. Their times do not add: two painters together are faster than either alone. (Times add only for stages done one after another.)
  • If A finishes a job in 6 hours, A does 1 over 6 of the job per hour. Rates in “jobs per hour” add; then time = 1 job ÷ combined rate.
  • A leak or drain is a negative rate. If the combined rate is zero the level never changes; if it is negative the tank empties instead of filling.
  • Relative speed. Moving towards each other, speeds add; moving the same way, the gap changes at the difference of the speeds.
  • Average speed is total distance ÷ total time, never the average of the speeds unless the times are equal.
  • A useful check: the combined time must be shorter than the fastest worker's time alone, and no shorter than that time divided by the number of workers (equal to it only when everyone is equally fast).

Watch out for

  • Adding or averaging times.
  • Forgetting a train's own length, or a head start.
  • Averaging two speeds over equal distances.

Level 6 of 8

Compound and mixed proportion

20 problems

Learning objective

Solve problems where three or four quantities affect the answer, by reasoning about one quantity at a time and by using combined units such as worker-hours and machine-hours.

Key ideas

  • One at a time. Change one quantity, hold the others fixed, and ask whether the answer goes up or down. Then multiply by the matching factor.
  • Combined units. 8 workers working 6 hours a day for 5 days supply 8 × 6 × 5 = 240 worker-hours. If that lays 480 m of cable, each metre needs 1 over 2 worker-hour.
  • Effects can cancel: fewer workers but longer days can leave the number of days unchanged.
  • Unequal workers or machines are converted into a common unit first (for example, if one large pump does the work of two small pumps, count it as two small pumps).
  • Every compound model rests on assumptions: equal efficiency, steady rates, no bottlenecks. A shortage of stoves or space can make extra workers useless.

Watch out for

  • Using a factor upside down. Predict the direction before writing the fraction.
  • Forgetting a quantity, such as hours per day.
  • Giving a fractional number of machines or workers when a whole number is needed.

Level 7 of 8

Olympiad modelling, changing conditions and hidden relationships

25 problems

Learning objective

Discover the model for yourself. Nobody will tell you whether a problem is direct, inverse, combined or something new. Build the model from what stays the same, test it on the data, and defend it.

Key ideas

  • Begin with what stays the same: total work, total distance, a difference, or a fixed amount plus a steady flow.
  • Draw a time line for problems in stages. Each stage has its own rate; the work done in all stages adds up to the whole job.
  • Work backwards from the end when the end is better known than the start.
  • When a resource grows while it is used (grass in a field, a well fed by a spring), split it into “what was there at the start” and “what arrives per day”. Two pieces of data let you find both.
  • Choose the point of view that makes the problem simple, such as watching from the moving water of a river or from the other runner.
  • An apparent contradiction is a signal that a hidden assumption is false. Find it.

Watch out for

  • Applying the net rate right up to the end when the last stage behaves differently.
  • Assuming the data are enough. Sometimes the correct answer is “not determined, and here is what is missing”.
  • Assuming the data are consistent. Sometimes they reveal a fixed time or cost that must be modelled.

Level 8 of 8

Cumulative challenge

10 problems

Learning objective

Choose the right model in unfamiliar situations where several ideas from the course meet and where one small change can make a familiar method fail.

How to approach these

  • Read the whole problem and decide what kind of relationship is present before calculating anything.
  • Expect more than one stage, more than one idea and at least one trap.
  • After solving, ask which single change to the story would break your method. That question is how Olympiad setters think.

Watch out for

  • Recognising a familiar shape and applying its method without checking the assumptions.
  • Stopping at a number without checking units, direction and whether the result is possible.

    Level 1, question 1 of 15 (problem 1 of 140)

    Question 1.1Eleven notebooks

    Difficulty 1 of 4: Foundation

    Six identical notebooks cost ₹222 altogether. How much do 11 of the same notebooks cost?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Every notebook costs the same. Can you find what just one notebook costs?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    Eleven notebooks cost ₹407.

    Understand the situation

    We know the total price of 6 notebooks, and we want the total price of 11 notebooks of the same kind. The word identical matters: it tells us that every notebook has the same price.

    Find the mathematical structure

    • What stays the same: the price of one notebook.
    • What changes: how many notebooks we buy, and so the total cost.
    • How they are connected: each notebook adds the same amount to the bill, so total cost = (number of notebooks) × (price of one notebook).

    When one quantity is always a fixed number of times the other, we say the two quantities are in direct proportion. Here we assume there is no discount for buying more.

    Build the reasoning

    Step 1: cross the bridge to one. The ₹222 is shared equally among 6 notebooks, so we divide.

    222 ÷ 6 = 37, meaning: rupees for one notebook

    Step 2: build up to eleven. Eleven notebooks are eleven lots of ₹37, so we multiply.

    11 × 37 = 407, meaning: rupees for 11 notebooks

    Dividing answers the question "how much for each one?". Multiplying answers "how much for this many?". Going through one is why this is called the unitary method: unit means one.

    Develop intuition

    Picture the ₹222 laid out as 6 equal piles of coins, one pile for each notebook. Each pile is ₹37. To pay for 11 notebooks, you lay down 11 such piles.

    Check the answer

    • Direction: more notebooks should cost more, and ₹407 is more than ₹222.
    • Size: 12 notebooks would be exactly double 6 notebooks, ₹444. Eleven is one notebook fewer than 12, so the answer should be a little less than ₹444. Indeed 444 − 407 = 37, exactly one notebook.
    • Reverse: ₹407 ÷ 11 = ₹37 per notebook, which matches.

    Alternative reasoning

    Use a convenient number instead of one. Twelve notebooks are two lots of six:

    2 × 222 = 444, meaning: rupees for 12 notebooks

    444 − 37 = 407, meaning: remove one notebook to get the price of 11

    General lesson

    When every unit is worth the same amount, go through one: divide to find the value of one unit, then multiply to reach the number you need. Before calculating, predict roughly where the answer should land.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Direct application, Estimation and bounds.

    Level 1, question 2 of 15 (problem 2 of 140)

    Question 1.2Label printer

    Difficulty 1 of 4: Foundation

    A machine prints labels at a steady rate. It prints 1,350 labels in 9 minutes. How many labels does it print in 25 minutes?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    "Steady rate" means every minute is just like every other minute. How many labels come out in one minute?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    3,750 labels in 25 minutes.

    Understand the situation

    We know how many labels are printed in 9 minutes. We need the number printed in 25 minutes by the same machine working the same way.

    Find the mathematical structure

    • Constant: the number of labels printed each minute. This is the machine's rate.
    • Changing: the time, and so the total number of labels.
    • Connection: labels printed = (labels per minute) × (number of minutes).

    The phrase steady rate is exactly the condition that lets us scale. If the machine sped up, slowed down or jammed, the method would not be valid.

    Build the reasoning

    Step 1: find the rate. The 1,350 labels came out evenly over 9 minutes.

    1,350 ÷ 9 = 150, meaning: labels printed in each minute

    Step 2: use the rate for 25 minutes. Each of the 25 minutes adds another 150 labels.

    25 × 150 = 3,750, meaning: labels printed in 25 minutes

    Develop intuition

    A rate is a promise: "every minute, 150 more labels". Over 25 minutes, the promise is kept 25 times.

    Check the answer

    27 minutes would be exactly 3 × 9 minutes, giving 3 × 1,350 = 4,050 labels. We want 2 minutes less than that, which is 2 × 150 = 300 labels fewer:

    4,050 − 300 = 3,750, meaning: same answer by a different route

    Alternative reasoning

    Use 5 minutes as the convenient unit. One minute gives 150 labels, so 5 minutes give 750, and 25 minutes are 5 lots of 5 minutes:

    5 × 750 = 3,750, meaning: labels in 25 minutes

    General lesson

    A steady rate is what makes scaling legitimate. Find the amount per one unit of time, then multiply by the time you need.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Direct application.

    Level 1, question 3 of 15 (problem 3 of 140)

    Question 1.3Filling bottles

    Difficulty 2 of 4: Core

    8 identical bottles hold 12 litres of water altogether when full. How many of these bottles are needed to hold exactly 39 litres?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Find how much one bottle holds. Then ask how many of those amounts fit into 39 litres.

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    26 bottles.

    Understand the situation

    This time we know the total amount of water, 39 litres, and must find how many bottles are needed. That is the reverse of the usual "find the total" question.

    Find the mathematical structure

    • Constant: the amount one bottle holds.
    • Connection: total water = (number of bottles) × (amount in one bottle).
    • So the number of bottles = total water ÷ amount in one bottle.

    Build the reasoning

    Step 1: one bottle. 12 litres are shared equally among 8 bottles.

    12 ÷ 8 = 1.5, meaning: litres held by one bottle

    Step 2: how many bottles? We are asking how many portions of 1.5 litres make 39 litres. That is division by grouping.

    39 ÷ 1.5 = 26, meaning: bottles needed

    Develop intuition

    Think of pouring the 39 litres into bottles one at a time, 1.5 litres in each, and counting the bottles. Division is just a quick way of doing that counting.

    Check the answer

    • 26 × 1.5 = 39 litres, so 26 bottles hold exactly 39 litres.
    • Size: 39 litres is a little more than 3 × 12 = 36 litres, so we need a little more than 3 × 8 = 24 bottles. 26 fits.

    Alternative reasoning

    Bridge through one litre instead of one bottle. If 12 litres need 8 bottles, then 1 litre needs 8 over 12 = 2 over 3 of a bottle.

    39 × 2 over 3 = 26, meaning: bottles for 39 litres

    Or use a convenient chunk. 12 litres fill 8 bottles, so 3 litres fill 2 bottles. 39 litres is 13 lots of 3 litres, so it fills 13 × 2 = 26 bottles.

    What if the amount were not exact?

    If the question asked for 40 litres, we would get:

    40 ÷ 1.5 = 26 and 2 over 3, meaning: bottles' worth of water

    You cannot use two-thirds of a bottle as a container, so you would need 27 bottles, with the last one only partly filled (26 full bottles hold 39 litres, and the 27th holds the last 1 litre). Rounding up is right here because every litre must go somewhere.

    General lesson

    The bridge of one works in both directions: litres per bottle, or bottles per litre. Choose whichever makes the arithmetic easy, and always ask whether a non-whole answer must be rounded up or down.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Reverse reasoning, Alternative solution.

    Level 1, question 4 of 15 (problem 4 of 140)

    Question 1.4Chairs for the hall

    Difficulty 2 of 4: Core

    A school is buying identical chairs. 14 chairs cost ₹9,800. The principal asks, "How much will it cost to put chairs in the whole new hall?"

    1. What extra information do you need before you can answer? Explain why.
    2. You are then told that the hall will have 9 rows with 12 chairs in each row. Find the cost.

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    A price for each chair is not a total. What must you multiply it by?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. You need the number of chairs for the hall. You also need the price per chair to stay the same for a bigger order (no bulk discount).
    2. The hall needs 108 chairs, costing ₹75,600.

    Understand the situation

    We can find the price of one chair from the information given. The principal's question is about a total, and a total depends on how many chairs there are, which nobody has told us yet.

    Find the mathematical structure

    Total cost = (number of chairs) × (price of one chair). We know a way to find the second factor but not the first. A good problem solver notices a missing quantity before starting to calculate, instead of grabbing the numbers that happen to be in the question.

    Build the reasoning

    Price of one chair:

    9,800 ÷ 14 = 700, meaning: rupees for each chair

    Number of chairs in the hall: 9 rows with 12 chairs in each row.

    9 × 12 = 108, meaning: chairs needed

    Total cost:

    108 × 700 = 75,600, meaning: rupees for all the chairs

    Check the answer

    108 chairs is a bit less than 8 × 14 = 112 chairs, which would cost 8 × ₹9,800 = ₹78,400. Our answer is 4 chairs (₹2,800) less than that:

    78,400 − 2,800 = 75,600, meaning: matches

    Alternative reasoning

    Cost one row first. A row of 12 chairs costs 12 × ₹700 = ₹8,400. Nine rows cost:

    9 × 8,400 = 75,600, meaning: rupees

    General lesson

    Before calculating, ask: "What exactly do I need to know?" A rate (rupees per chair) only becomes a total when you know how many units there are.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Missing quantity, Direct application.

    Level 1, question 5 of 15 (problem 5 of 140)

    Question 1.5Two packs of pencils

    Difficulty 2 of 4: Core

    Pack A has 12 identical pencils for ₹96. Pack B has 15 identical pencils of the same quality for ₹105. Kiran says, "Pack A is the better deal because ₹96 is less than ₹105."

    1. Is Kiran's reasoning correct? Explain.
    2. Which pack gives the cheaper pencil, and by how much per pencil?
    3. How much would you save by buying 60 pencils in the cheaper packs instead of the more expensive packs?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    A fair comparison needs the same number of pencils on both sides. Try one pencil, or a number that both pack sizes divide into.

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. No. Kiran compares the prices of different numbers of pencils, which is not a fair comparison.
    2. Pack B is cheaper: ₹7 per pencil against ₹8, so it is ₹1 cheaper per pencil.
    3. 60 pencils cost ₹480 in packs A and ₹420 in packs B, a saving of ₹60.

    Understand the situation

    "Better deal" means you pay less for the same amount of pencils. Pack A costs less money, but it also gives you fewer pencils, so its lower price might simply be because you are getting less.

    Find the mathematical structure

    To compare value we need a common basis: the price of the same number of pencils. The simplest basis is one pencil. Another is a number of pencils that both pack sizes fit into exactly.

    Build the reasoning

    Price of one pencil in each pack:

    96 ÷ 12 = 8, meaning: rupees per pencil in pack A

    105 ÷ 15 = 7, meaning: rupees per pencil in pack B

    Pack B's pencils cost ₹1 less each, even though the pack itself costs more.

    60 pencils: 60 is a multiple of both 12 and 15.

    60 ÷ 12 = 5, meaning: packs of A needed

    5 × 96 = 480, meaning: rupees using pack A

    60 ÷ 15 = 4, meaning: packs of B needed

    4 × 105 = 420, meaning: rupees using pack B

    480 − 420 = 60, meaning: rupees saved with pack B

    Develop intuition

    Kiran's comparison is like saying a small bag of rice is cheaper than a big bag. Of course it costs less: it holds less. Value is about what you get for each unit.

    Check the answer

    The saving of ₹60 on 60 pencils is exactly ₹1 per pencil, matching part (b).

    General lesson

    Compare like with like. Either compare the price of one unit, or compare the prices of the same total quantity. Comparing totals for different quantities tells you nothing about value.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Comparison, Error diagnosis.

    Level 1, question 6 of 15 (problem 6 of 140)

    Question 1.6Meera's printer prediction

    Difficulty 2 of 4: Core

    A printer prints 18 pages in 3 minutes, working at a steady rate. Meera says, "In 5 minutes it will print 20 pages. 5 minutes is 2 minutes more than 3 minutes, so it prints 2 pages more than 18."

    1. Explain exactly what is wrong with Meera's reasoning.
    2. Find the correct number of pages printed in 5 minutes.
    3. Use Meera's method to predict how many pages are printed in 1 minute. Show that her prediction contradicts the information in the question.

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    How many pages does the printer add in each extra minute? Is it 1?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. Meera adds 1 page for each extra minute, but the printer actually prints 6 pages every minute. She treats the change as adding a fixed difference instead of multiplying by a steady rate.
    2. 30 pages.
    3. Her method gives 16 pages in 1 minute. At that steady rate, 3 minutes would give 48 pages, not 18, so her method contradicts the facts.

    Understand the situation

    The printer's speed is fixed. Meera's argument links "2 more minutes" to "2 more pages", as though one minute were worth one page.

    Find the mathematical structure

    In a steady-rate situation, pages = (pages per minute) × minutes. Increasing the time by 2 minutes increases the pages by 2 × (pages per minute), not by 2. Meera's reasoning keeps the difference between the two numbers the same; proportional reasoning keeps the ratio (pages per minute) the same.

    Build the reasoning

    The real rate:

    18 ÷ 3 = 6, meaning: pages each minute

    Five minutes:

    5 × 6 = 30, meaning: pages in 5 minutes

    Testing Meera's method. For 1 minute she would say: "1 is 2 less than 3, so 2 pages fewer than 18", which gives 16 pages in 1 minute. But if the printer printed 16 pages every minute, then in 3 minutes it would print:

    3 × 16 = 48, meaning: pages, but the question says 18

    So her method cannot be right. Her method also predicts that in 0 minutes the printer prints 18 − 3 = 15 pages, which is impossible.

    Develop intuition

    Imagine the printer dropping 6 pages into the tray every minute. Two extra minutes bring two extra drops of 6 pages, so 12 more pages, not 2.

    Check the answer

    30 pages in 5 minutes is 6 pages per minute, the same rate as 18 pages in 3 minutes.

    General lesson

    In a steady-rate situation, a change in one quantity produces a proportional change in the other, not an equal change. A powerful way to test any method is to try it on a case where you already know the answer.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Error diagnosis, Counterexample.

    Level 1, question 7 of 15 (problem 7 of 140)

    Question 1.7Why scaling works

    Difficulty 2 of 4: Core

    4 identical pens cost ₹60, so Arjun says that 12 pens cost ₹180.

    1. Explain, without using any memorised rule such as "cross-multiply", why Arjun is right. Say clearly what stays the same.
    2. Describe two different real situations in which 12 pens would not cost ₹180, even though 4 pens still cost ₹60.
    3. For one of your situations, give a possible cost for 12 pens and say which step of the explanation in (a) breaks.

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    What is Arjun quietly assuming about the price of each extra pen?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. Each pen costs ₹60 ÷ 4 = ₹15, and every pen costs the same, so 12 pens cost 12 × ₹15 = ₹180. Equivalently, 12 pens are 3 groups of 4 pens, and each group costs ₹60.
    2. Examples: a bulk discount; a fixed delivery charge for each order; a "buy 10, get 2 free" offer; only a limited number of pens on sale at ₹15.
    3. Example: an online shop charges ₹40 delivery plus ₹5 per pen. Then 4 pens cost ₹60 but 12 pens cost ₹100. The step that breaks is "every pen adds the same ₹15".

    Understand the situation

    Arjun's answer is correct only because of an assumption hidden in the word identical: the price of each pen is the same no matter how many you buy. Part (a) asks us to say that assumption out loud; parts (b) and (c) ask what happens when it fails.

    Building the argument

    Why ₹180. If every pen has the same price, the ₹60 is split equally among 4 pens:

    60 ÷ 4 = 15, meaning: rupees for each pen

    Twelve pens are twelve such prices:

    12 × 15 = 180, meaning: rupees for 12 pens

    Another way to say the same thing: 12 pens can be arranged as 3 groups of 4 pens. Each group costs ₹60, so 3 groups cost 3 × ₹60 = ₹180.

    What stays the same: the price of one pen. That single fact is what lets us scale. It guarantees that every group of 4 pens costs ₹60, and that the cost grows by the same ₹15 for every extra pen.

    When it fails.

    • Bulk discount: "₹15 each, or a box of 12 for ₹150". Then 12 pens cost ₹150.
    • Fixed delivery charge: "₹40 delivery on every order, plus ₹5 per pen". Check 4 pens: 40 + 4 × 5 = 60 rupees, exactly as given. But 12 pens cost:

    40 + 12 × 5 = 100, meaning: rupees for 12 pens with this pricing

    • Free pens: "buy 10, get 2 free". Then 12 pens cost the price of 10 pens, ₹150.
    • Limited stock: only 10 pens are left at ₹15, and after that the price is ₹20 each. Then 12 pens cost 10 × 15 + 2 × 20 = ₹190.

    In the delivery example, the step "every pen costs ₹15" breaks. The first pen costs ₹45 (₹40 delivery and ₹5 for the pen), and every further pen costs only ₹5. The cost is no longer a fixed number of rupees per pen.

    Develop intuition

    Notice something surprising: the fact "4 pens cost ₹60" fits both pricing schemes. One piece of data cannot tell you whether a relationship is proportional. You need to know how the price is built up.

    General lesson

    Scaling works because the amount per unit stays the same. Whenever you use the unitary method, say what you are assuming stays the same, and ask whether that is really true in the situation.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Proof and explanation, Counterexample, Changed conditions.

    Level 1, question 8 of 15 (problem 8 of 140)

    Question 1.8Bag of marbles

    Difficulty 1 of 4: Foundation

    35 identical marbles weigh 140 g altogether.

    1. Find the weight of 105 marbles by first finding the weight of one marble.
    2. Find the weight of 105 marbles again, this time without finding the weight of one marble.
    3. Which method was quicker here? Give a different number of marbles for which method (a) would be quicker, and find its weight.

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    For (b), how is 105 related to 35?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. One marble weighs 4 g, so 105 marbles weigh 420 g.
    2. 105 = 3 × 35, so 105 marbles weigh 3 × 140 g = 420 g.
    3. Method (b) was quicker because 105 is an exact multiple of 35. For a number such as 37 marbles, going through one marble is easier: 37 × 4 = 148 g.

    Find the mathematical structure

    The weight of one marble is fixed, so total weight = (number of marbles) × (weight of one marble). Because of this, multiplying the number of marbles by any factor multiplies the weight by the same factor.

    Build the reasoning

    (a) Through one marble:

    140 ÷ 35 = 4, meaning: grams for one marble

    105 × 4 = 420, meaning: grams for 105 marbles

    (b) Through a scale factor:

    105 ÷ 35 = 3, meaning: 105 marbles are 3 times as many as 35 marbles

    3 × 140 = 420, meaning: three times the weight

    (c) For 37 marbles the scale factor is 37 ÷ 35 = 37 over 35, which is awkward. Going through one marble is simple:

    37 × 4 = 148, meaning: grams for 37 marbles

    Develop intuition

    Both methods use the same fact in different clothes. "One marble weighs 4 g" and "three times the marbles weigh three times as much" are both consequences of every marble weighing the same.

    Check the answer

    Both methods give 420 g. And 420 ÷ 105 = 4 g per marble, as expected.

    General lesson

    The bridge of one always works, but it is not always the shortest road. When the numbers are exact multiples of each other, scale directly; when they are not, go through one.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Alternative solution, Direct application.

    Level 1, question 9 of 15 (problem 9 of 140)

    Question 1.9Reading a map

    Difficulty 2 of 4: Core

    On a map, 3 cm represents a real distance of 12 km.

    1. Two villages are 7.5 cm apart on the map. How far apart are they really?
    2. A straight road is 50 km long. How long will it be on the map?
    3. The map's scale is also printed as 1 : 400,000, which means 1 cm on the map stands for 400,000 cm in real life. Check that this agrees with "3 cm represents 12 km".
    4. Your answer to (a) is a straight-line distance. Why might the distance you walk between the villages be longer?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    What real distance does 1 cm on the map stand for?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. 30 km. (b) 12.5 cm. (c) 12 km = 1,200,000 cm and 1,200,000 ÷ 3 = 400,000, so 1 cm represents 400,000 cm. The two descriptions agree. (d) Paths bend around hills, rivers and fields, so a walking route is usually longer than the straight line.

    Understand the situation

    A map is a shrunken copy of the land. Every length on it is shrunk by the same factor (divided by the same number), so map lengths and real lengths are in direct proportion.

    Find the mathematical structure

    Constant: the number of kilometres represented by each centimetre of the map. This is the map's scale.

    Build the reasoning

    12 ÷ 3 = 4, meaning: kilometres shown by 1 cm of map

    (a)

    7.5 × 4 = 30, meaning: kilometres between the villages

    (b) Reverse: how many centimetres, each worth 4 km, make 50 km?

    50 ÷ 4 = 12.5, meaning: centimetres on the map

    (c) 1 km = 1,000 m and 1 m = 100 cm, so 1 km = 100,000 cm.

    12 × 100,000 = 1,200,000, meaning: centimetres in 12 km

    1,200,000 ÷ 3 = 400,000, meaning: real centimetres for each map centimetre

    Develop intuition

    The scale "1 : 400,000" says that a real distance is 400,000 times as long as its picture on the map. As long as the map length and the real length are measured in the same unit, the multiplier is always 400,000.

    Check the answer

    • (a) 7.5 cm is 2.5 times 3 cm, so the real distance should be 2.5 × 12 km = 30 km.
    • (b) 12.5 cm is a little more than 4 lots of 3 cm, which would be 48 km. 50 km is a little more than 48 km.

    General lesson

    A map scale is a rate (kilometres per centimetre), and it works exactly like a price per item. The same answer comes out whatever units you use, provided you convert carefully.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Transfer, Reverse reasoning.

    Level 1, question 10 of 15 (problem 10 of 140)

    Question 1.10Cooking for seven

    Difficulty 2 of 4: Core

    A recipe for 4 people uses 300 g of pasta and 2 tins of tomatoes. Hamid is making the same recipe for 7 people.

    1. How much pasta does he need?
    2. How many tins of tomatoes does the recipe need for 7 people? Tins are sold whole, so how many must he buy?
    3. The recipe also says, "Cook the pasta for 10 minutes." Should Hamid change the cooking time for 7 people? Explain.

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Find the amounts for one person. For (c), ask whether cooking time depends on how many people will eat.

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. 525 g of pasta.
    2. The recipe needs 3.5 tins, so Hamid must buy 4 tins.
    3. No. Cooking time depends on how long pasta takes to soften in boiling water, not on how many people will eat it. (This assumes his pot is big enough to cook it all at once.)

    Understand the situation

    The ingredients are needed per person: each extra person needs the same extra amount of pasta and tomato. The cooking time is different: all the pasta cooks at the same time in one pot.

    Find the mathematical structure

    Pasta and tomatoes are in direct proportion to the number of people. Cooking time is not: it stays the same whatever the number of people. Recognising which quantities scale is part of the problem.

    Build the reasoning

    (a)

    300 ÷ 4 = 75, meaning: grams of pasta for one person

    7 × 75 = 525, meaning: grams for 7 people

    (b)

    2 ÷ 4 = 0.5, meaning: tins for one person

    7 × 0.5 = 3.5, meaning: tins needed for 7 people

    He cannot buy half a tin. Three tins are not enough, so he must buy 4 and use three and a half of them (or use all 4 if he likes extra sauce).

    Check the answer

    7 people is a little less than double 4 people, so the amounts should be a little less than double: a bit less than 600 g of pasta and a bit less than 4 tins. 525 g and 3.5 tins fit.

    General lesson

    Before scaling, ask which quantities really grow with the number of units. Ingredients scale; cooking time usually does not. And when items come whole, decide whether the situation needs you to round up or down.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Direct application, Model selection.

    Level 1, question 11 of 15 (problem 11 of 140)

    Question 1.11Make a whole-rupee answer

    Difficulty 2 of 4: Core

    Invent a shopping problem of this form:

    "___ identical items cost ₹___. How much do ___ of these items cost?"

    Your numbers must satisfy both of these conditions:

    • the price of one item is not a whole number of rupees, and
    • the final answer is a whole number of rupees.

    Solve your problem, explain why the final answer came out as a whole number, and describe a way to make as many such problems as you like.

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Pick a small group of items that costs a whole number of rupees, but which cannot be split into whole-rupee prices. Then ask for a number of items that is made of whole groups.

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    One example: 4 items cost ₹30. How much do 6 items cost? One item costs ₹7.50, which is not a whole number of rupees, but 6 items cost ₹45, which is. Many other answers are possible.

    One possible solution

    30 ÷ 4 = 7.5, meaning: rupees for one item, so ₹7.50, not whole

    6 × 7.5 = 45, meaning: rupees for 6 items, a whole number

    Why it worked. Two items cost 2 × ₹7.50 = ₹15, a whole number. Six items are 3 pairs, so they cost 3 × ₹15 = ₹45. Any even number of these items costs a whole number of rupees; any odd number does not (for example 5 items cost ₹37.50).

    A recipe for making more

    1. Choose a group size and a whole-rupee price for the group that does not divide evenly, such as 3 items for ₹10. One item then costs ₹3.33 and a bit, which is not whole.
    2. Ask for a number of items that is a multiple of the group size: 12 items are 4 groups, so they cost 4 × ₹10 = ₹40.

    More precisely: if k items cost C rupees, then n items cost n × C ÷ k rupees. This is a whole number exactly when n × C is divisible by k.

    Check the answer

    In the example, check both conditions: ₹7.50 is not whole, and ₹45 is whole. Also check the problem makes sense: 6 items should cost 1.5 times as much as 4 items, and 1.5 × 30 = 45.

    General lesson

    Making up a problem with a required property forces you to understand why answers come out the way they do. Here the key insight is that a whole-rupee price for a group survives when you buy whole groups.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Problem construction.

    Level 1, question 12 of 15 (problem 12 of 140)

    Question 1.12A faster bottling machine

    Difficulty 2 of 4: Core

    A bottling machine fills 240 bottles in 8 minutes, working at a steady rate.

    1. How many bottles does it fill in 30 minutes?
    2. It is replaced by a new machine that fills 240 bottles in 6 minutes, also at a steady rate. How many bottles does the new machine fill in 30 minutes?
    3. How much time does the new machine save on an order of 900 bottles?
    4. When the machine was replaced, which quantity in the problem changed, and which stayed the same? How did this change your "bridge of one"?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Find bottles per minute for each machine. For (c), work out how long each machine needs for 900 bottles.

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. 900 bottles. (b) 1,200 bottles. (c) The old machine takes 30 minutes and the new one 22.5 minutes, so it saves 7.5 minutes (7 minutes 30 seconds). (d) The rate changed, while the time (30 minutes in both (a) and (b)) stayed the same. "One minute" used to be worth 30 bottles and is now worth 40 bottles.

    Understand the situation

    The same 240 bottles now take less time. That means the machine fills more bottles in each minute. Every later answer depends on this new rate.

    Build the reasoning

    Old machine:

    240 ÷ 8 = 30, meaning: bottles per minute

    30 × 30 = 900, meaning: bottles in 30 minutes

    New machine:

    240 ÷ 6 = 40, meaning: bottles per minute

    30 × 40 = 1,200, meaning: bottles in 30 minutes

    (c) Time for 900 bottles. Now we know the number of bottles and want the time. Divide by the rate:

    900 ÷ 30 = 30, meaning: minutes for the old machine

    900 ÷ 40 = 22.5, meaning: minutes for the new machine

    30 − 22.5 = 7.5, meaning: minutes saved

    Develop intuition

    Think of each minute as a box that holds a certain number of bottles. The new machine has bigger boxes (40 bottles instead of 30), so it needs fewer boxes for the same order.

    Check the answer

    The new machine is 40 over 30 = 4 over 3 times as fast, so in 30 minutes it should fill 4 over 3 × 900 = 1,200 bottles. That matches (b). For the same order it should need 3 over 4 of the time: 3 over 4 × 30 = 22.5 minutes, which matches (c).

    General lesson

    When a condition changes, find out which quantity it changes. Here the time stayed the same in (b), but the rate changed, so the value of "one minute" had to be recalculated before scaling.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Changed conditions, Direct application.

    Level 1, question 13 of 15 (problem 13 of 140)

    Question 1.13Mixing a paint shade

    Difficulty 2 of 4: Core

    A paint shade is made by mixing blue paint and white paint in the ratio 3 : 5. That means 3 parts of blue for every 5 parts of white, measured in litres.

    1. Isha has 12 litres of blue. How much white does she need?
    2. Another time, she wants exactly 64 litres of the mixed paint. How much blue and how much white should she use?
    3. One day Isha mixes 12 litres of blue with 18 litres of white by mistake. Is the shade darker or lighter than intended? Explain how you know. What is the least amount of paint she could add to correct it, and of which colour?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Treat "one part" as the bridge. How many litres is one part in each question?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. 20 litres of white.
    2. 24 litres of blue and 40 litres of white.
    3. The shade is darker (too much blue for the amount of white). She should add 2 litres of white.

    Understand the situation

    A ratio like 3 : 5 describes a recipe. It does not tell us the amounts, only how they compare: whatever "one part" is, blue is 3 of them and white is 5.

    Find the mathematical structure

    Constant: the size of one part within a single batch. Once we know how many litres make one part, we know everything. This is the bridge of one again, with "one part" as the unit.

    Build the reasoning

    (a) The 12 litres of blue are 3 parts.

    12 ÷ 3 = 4, meaning: litres in one part

    5 × 4 = 20, meaning: litres of white (5 parts)

    (b) A full batch has 3 + 5 = 8 parts, and these 8 parts make 64 litres.

    64 ÷ 8 = 8, meaning: litres in one part

    3 × 8 = 24, meaning: litres of blue

    5 × 8 = 40, meaning: litres of white

    (c) With 12 litres of blue she should have used 20 litres of white, as in (a). She used only 18 litres, so there is too little white for the blue. The mixture is darker than intended. She cannot take blue out, but she can add white:

    20 − 18 = 2, meaning: litres of white to add

    This is the smallest correction. Adding more blue would only make things worse: every extra litre of blue would need another 5 over 3 litres of white on top of these 2 litres.

    Develop intuition

    Imagine the paint poured from 1-litre jugs: for every 3 jugs of blue, 5 jugs of white. In (c), Isha has poured 4 lots of 3 jugs of blue, so she needs 4 lots of 5 jugs of white, which is 20 jugs, but she has poured only 18. Two jugs of white are missing.

    Check the answer

    • (b) 24 + 40 = 64 litres, and 24 : 40 simplifies to 3 : 5 (divide both by 8).
    • (c) After adding, 12 : 20 simplifies to 3 : 5, the intended shade.

    Alternative reasoning

    In (c), compare the amount of white for each litre of blue. The recipe gives 5 over 3 = 1.67 litres of white per litre of blue (to 2 decimal places). Isha's mixture gives 18 ÷ 12 = 1.5 litres. Less white per litre of blue means a darker shade.

    General lesson

    A ratio is a rate in disguise. Find the size of one part, and the ratio becomes an ordinary unitary-method problem. To correct a mixture, add the ingredient that is short, since you cannot remove what is already mixed in.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Transfer, Error diagnosis.

    Level 1, question 14 of 15 (problem 14 of 140)

    Question 1.14Petrol for a long drive

    Difficulty 2 of 4: Core

    A car travels 54 km on 3 litres of petrol. Petrol costs ₹104 per litre. Assuming the car uses petrol at the same rate for the whole journey, how much will the petrol cost for a journey of 270 km?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    There are two rates here: kilometres per litre, and rupees per litre. Use the first to find the litres, then the second to find the cost.

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    The petrol costs ₹1,560.

    Understand the situation

    Two connections are chained together: distance depends on petrol used, and cost depends on petrol bought. The quantity that links them is the number of litres.

    Find the mathematical structure

    • Distance is in direct proportion to petrol used (the car uses petrol at a steady rate).
    • Cost is in direct proportion to petrol bought (a fixed price per litre).
    • So cost is also in direct proportion to distance. We can go distance → litres → rupees.

    Build the reasoning

    Distance per litre:

    54 ÷ 3 = 18, meaning: kilometres per litre

    Petrol needed: how many portions of 18 km make 270 km?

    270 ÷ 18 = 15, meaning: litres needed

    Cost:

    15 × 104 = 1,560, meaning: rupees

    Alternative reasoning

    Spot the scale factor. 270 km is 5 times 54 km, so the car needs 5 times 3 litres = 15 litres, and the cost is 15 × ₹104 = ₹1,560.

    Cost per kilometre. You could also find the cost of one kilometre, but the number is awkward:

    104 ÷ 18 = 52 over 9, meaning: rupees per kilometre, exactly

    104 ÷ 18 ≈ 5.78, meaning: rupees per kilometre, rounded

    270 × 52 over 9 = 1,560, meaning: rupees, using the exact value

    270 × 5.78 = 1,560.6, meaning: rupees, using the rounded value

    Using the rounded value gives ₹1,560.60, which is 60 paise wrong. Rounding in the middle of a calculation lets small errors grow. Keep exact values (or the friendlier route through litres) until the end.

    Check the answer

    15 litres at 18 km per litre is 270 km. And ₹1,560 is a little more than 15 × ₹100 = ₹1,500, as expected.

    General lesson

    Rates can be chained: if A depends proportionally on B, and B on C, then A depends proportionally on C. Choose the bridge that keeps the numbers friendly, and round only at the very end.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Direct application, Alternative solution.

    Level 1, question 15 of 15 (problem 15 of 140)

    Question 1.15A day of heartbeats

    Difficulty 2 of 4: Core

    Suppose a person's heart beats at a steady 72 beats per minute.

    1. Before calculating exactly, decide whether the heart beats more than or fewer than 100,000 times in one day of 24 hours. Explain your estimate.
    2. Calculate the exact number of beats in a day under this assumption.
    3. Real hearts do not beat at a perfectly steady rate. Name one thing that would make the true number different, and say whether it would push the number up or down.

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Use a friendly rate first, such as 70 beats per minute. How many minutes are there in a day?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. More than 100,000. Even at only 70 beats per minute, a day gives 70 × 60 × 24 = 100,800 beats, and 72 is more than 70.
    2. 103,680 beats (in Indian notation, 1,03,680).
    3. For example, the heart beats more slowly during sleep, which would make the true number lower; exercise speeds it up, which would make it higher.

    Understand the situation

    We have a rate per minute and want a total for a day, so we must convert a day into minutes. Part (a) asks for a quick judgement, part (b) for an exact value, and part (c) asks us to question the model itself.

    Find the mathematical structure

    If the rate is steady, beats = (beats per minute) × (number of minutes). A day has 24 hours of 60 minutes each.

    Build the reasoning

    (a) Estimate with friendly numbers.

    70 × 60 = 4,200, meaning: beats per hour at 70 per minute

    4,200 × 24 = 100,800, meaning: beats per day at 70 per minute

    That is already more than 100,000, and the real rate of 72 is higher, so the answer is "more".

    (b) Exact.

    60 × 24 = 1,440, meaning: minutes in a day

    72 × 1,440 = 103,680, meaning: beats in a day

    Check the answer

    Two extra beats per minute add 2 × 1,440 = 2,880 beats per day, and 100,800 + 2,880 = 103,680. The estimate and the exact answer agree.

    Questioning the model

    The calculation is only as good as its assumption of a steady rate. A resting heart rate of about 72 is typical for some adults, but during sleep the rate usually drops, and during running it can more than double. The value 103,680 is the answer under the stated assumption, not a fact about any real person.

    General lesson

    Estimate first with friendly numbers: it tells you what size of answer to expect and often settles a "more or less" question without exact work. Then remember that any unitary-method answer rests on the assumption that the rate is constant.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Estimation and bounds, Assumption check.

    Level 2, question 1 of 15 (problem 16 of 140)

    Question 2.1Three-quarters of a kilogram

    Difficulty 1 of 4: Foundation

    3 over 4 kg of cheese costs ₹360.

    1. How much does 1 kg of the same cheese cost?
    2. How much does 2 and 1 over 2 kg cost?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Three quarters of a kilogram cost ₹360. What does one quarter cost?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. 1 kg costs ₹480. (b) 2 and 1 over 2 kg costs ₹1,200.

    Understand the situation

    We know the price of less than a kilogram and want the price of exactly one kilogram, then of two and a half kilograms. The price per kilogram is fixed.

    Find the mathematical structure

    Cost is in direct proportion to weight. The bridge does not have to be "1 kg" straight away: here a quarter of a kilogram is the natural stepping stone, because we already have three of them.

    Build the reasoning

    Step 1: one quarter. 3 over 4 kg is three quarter-kilograms, and they cost ₹360 together.

    360 ÷ 3 = 120, meaning: rupees for 1 over 4 kg

    Step 2: one kilogram. A kilogram is four quarters.

    4 × 120 = 480, meaning: rupees for 1 kg

    Step 3: two and a half kilograms. 2 and 1 over 2 kg is 10 quarter-kilograms (4 + 4 + 2).

    10 × 120 = 1,200, meaning: rupees for 2 and 1 over 2 kg

    Steps 1 and 2 together are the same as dividing by 3 over 4:

    360 ÷ 3 over 4 = 480, meaning: dividing by a fraction asks "how much for one whole kilogram?"

    Develop intuition

    Draw a bar for one kilogram, cut into 4 equal pieces. Three of the pieces are labelled ₹360, so each piece is ₹120, and the whole bar is ₹480.

    Check the answer

    • Direction: 1 kg is more cheese than 3 over 4 kg, so it must cost more than ₹360. It does.
    • A tempting error: 360 × 3 over 4 = 270. That would make a whole kilogram cheaper than three quarters of a kilogram, which is impossible. When you meet "3 over 4 kg costs ₹360", you divide by 3 over 4, not multiply.
    • (b) 2 and 1 over 2 × 480 = 1,200, the same answer.

    General lesson

    To find the value of one whole unit from a fraction of a unit, divide by the fraction. If that feels slippery, go through the unit fraction first: from three quarters to one quarter, then to four quarters.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Direct application, Error diagnosis.

    Level 2, question 2 of 15 (problem 17 of 140)

    Question 2.2Ribbon by the metre

    Difficulty 1 of 4: Foundation

    2.5 m of ribbon costs ₹45. How much does 4.2 m of the same ribbon cost?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    What does 1 m cost? Or, if decimals worry you, what does 10 cm cost?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    4.2 m costs ₹75.60.

    Understand the situation

    The price is charged by length, at a fixed price per metre. We know the cost of one length and want the cost of another.

    Find the mathematical structure

    Cost = (length in metres) × (price per metre). Decimals change nothing about the reasoning: they only change the arithmetic.

    Build the reasoning

    45 ÷ 2.5 = 18, meaning: rupees per metre

    4.2 × 18 = 75.6, meaning: rupees for 4.2 m

    In money we write ₹75.60.

    Alternative reasoning

    Use smaller units, so that every number is whole. 2.5 m is 25 lots of 10 cm, 4.2 m is 42 lots of 10 cm, and ₹45 is 4,500 paise.

    4,500 ÷ 25 = 180, meaning: paise for 10 cm of ribbon

    42 × 180 = 7,560, meaning: paise for 4.2 m, which is ₹75.60

    Check the answer

    • Bounds: 4.2 m lies between 4 m (₹72) and 5 m (₹90, which is double 2.5 m). ₹75.60 is between them, and closer to ₹72, as it should be since 4.2 is closer to 4.
    • Reverse: 75.6 ÷ 4.2 = 18 rupees per metre.

    General lesson

    The bridge of one works the same way with decimals. If decimals make the arithmetic awkward, change to a smaller unit (centimetres, paise, grams) so that the numbers become whole.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Direct application, Estimation and bounds.

    Level 2, question 3 of 15 (problem 18 of 140)

    Question 2.3Chocolates through a convenient group

    Difficulty 2 of 4: Core

    24 identical chocolates cost ₹180. How much do 40 of these chocolates cost?

    Solve it in two ways: once by finding the cost of one chocolate, and once by using a group size that divides exactly into both 24 and 40. Which way do you prefer, and why?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Which number divides exactly into both 24 and 40? Use the biggest one you can find.

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    40 chocolates cost ₹300. One chocolate costs ₹7.50; a group of 8 chocolates costs ₹60. Many people prefer the group of 8 here because it avoids decimals, but both methods are correct.

    Understand the situation

    Every chocolate has the same price, so cost is in direct proportion to the number of chocolates. The question is about choosing a convenient bridge.

    Build the reasoning

    Method 1: through one chocolate.

    180 ÷ 24 = 7.5, meaning: rupees for one chocolate

    40 × 7.5 = 300, meaning: rupees for 40 chocolates

    Method 2: through a group of 8. 8 is the largest number that divides exactly into both 24 and 40 (their highest common factor).

    24 ÷ 8 = 3, meaning: 24 chocolates are 3 groups of 8

    180 ÷ 3 = 60, meaning: rupees for a group of 8

    40 ÷ 8 = 5, meaning: 40 chocolates are 5 groups of 8

    5 × 60 = 300, meaning: rupees for 40 chocolates

    Develop intuition

    Imagine the chocolates packed in bags of 8. You bought 3 bags for ₹180, so each bag costs ₹60. Now you want 5 bags.

    Why both methods must agree

    Both methods are the same calculation in different order. The cost of 40 chocolates is ₹180 × 40 over 24. The fraction 40 over 24 can be simplified by cancelling the common factor 8:

    40 over 24 = 5 over 3, meaning: cancel the common factor 8

    180 × 5 over 3 = 300, meaning: rupees

    Cancelling the 8 is the "group of 8" method. Cancelling is not a trick; it is the bridge through a convenient group, written as a fraction.

    Check the answer

    40 chocolates are 5 over 3 times as many as 24, so they should cost a bit less than double ₹180 (₹360). ₹300 fits.

    General lesson

    The "one" in the unitary method can be any convenient chunk. A common factor of the two quantities often gives a bridge with whole numbers, and it is the same as cancelling in a fraction.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Alternative solution, Direct application.

    Level 2, question 4 of 15 (problem 19 of 140)

    Question 2.4Wrapping sweets for hours

    Difficulty 2 of 4: Core

    A machine wraps sweets at a steady rate. It wraps 0.6 kg of sweets in 1.5 minutes. How many kilograms of sweets does it wrap in 2 and 1 over 4 hours?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    The rate is given per minute, but the time is in hours. Put them in the same unit first.

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    The machine wraps 54 kg in 2 and 1 over 4 hours.

    Understand the situation

    The rate uses minutes and the time uses hours. Before any scaling, both must be in the same unit, or the answer will be wildly wrong.

    Find the mathematical structure

    Mass wrapped = (kilograms per minute) × (number of minutes). The rate is steady, so direct proportion holds.

    Build the reasoning

    Step 1: convert the time. 2 and 1 over 4 hours is 2 hours and 15 minutes.

    2 × 60 + 15 = 135, meaning: minutes in 2 and 1 over 4 hours

    Step 2: the rate per minute.

    0.6 ÷ 1.5 = 0.4, meaning: kilograms wrapped per minute

    Step 3: scale.

    135 × 0.4 = 54, meaning: kilograms in 135 minutes

    Alternative reasoning

    Work per hour instead.

    0.4 × 60 = 24, meaning: kilograms per hour

    2 and 1 over 4 × 24 = 54, meaning: kilograms

    Or double up. 0.6 kg in 1.5 minutes means 1.2 kg in 3 minutes, so 6 kg in 15 minutes. 135 minutes are 9 lots of 15 minutes, giving 9 × 6 = 54 kg.

    Check the answer

    Two tempting errors.

    • Using the per-minute rate with the time in hours: 0.4 × 2.25 = 0.9 kg. That would mean less than one kilogram in over two hours, although the machine wraps 0.6 kg in just 1.5 minutes. Impossible.
    • Reading 2 and 1 over 4 hours as "2.15 hours" or "215 minutes". A quarter of an hour is 15 minutes, so 2 and 1 over 4 hours is 2.25 hours, which is 135 minutes.

    Sense check: 24 kg per hour for a little over 2 hours gives a little over 48 kg. 54 kg fits.

    General lesson

    A rate always carries two units, such as "kilograms per minute". Before scaling, make sure the time (or other quantity) is measured in the same unit as the rate's "per" part.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Direct application, Error diagnosis.

    Level 2, question 5 of 15 (problem 20 of 140)

    Question 2.5Three brands of rice

    Difficulty 2 of 4: Core

    Three brands of rice of the same quality are sold in these packs:

    • Brand A: 450 g for ₹81
    • Brand B: 700 g for ₹119
    • Brand C: 1.2 kg for ₹210
    1. Rank the three brands from best value to worst value. Show your method.
    2. Rank them again using a different method, and explain why both methods must give the same ranking.
    3. Give one sensible reason why a shopper might still choose a brand that is not the best value.

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Find the price of the same amount of rice for each brand, such as 1 kg or 100 g.

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. B, then C, then A. Per kilogram: B costs ₹170, C costs ₹175, A costs ₹180.
    2. Grams per rupee: B gives about 5.88 g, C about 5.71 g, A about 5.56 g. More grams per rupee means better value, so the order is again B, C, A.
    3. For example: the shopper only needs a small amount, has no room to store a big pack, or the rice would go stale before it is used.

    Understand the situation

    The packs contain different amounts and have different prices, so neither the prices nor the weights can be compared directly. We need a common basis.

    Build the reasoning

    (a) Price of 1 kg. Convert grams to kilograms: 450 g = 0.45 kg and 700 g = 0.7 kg.

    81 ÷ 0.45 = 180, meaning: rupees per kilogram for A

    119 ÷ 0.7 = 170, meaning: rupees per kilogram for B

    210 ÷ 1.2 = 175, meaning: rupees per kilogram for C

    The lowest price per kilogram is the best value: B (₹170), then C (₹175), then A (₹180).

    (b) Grams for one rupee.

    450 ÷ 81 ≈ 5.56, meaning: grams per rupee for A

    700 ÷ 119 ≈ 5.88, meaning: grams per rupee for B

    1,200 ÷ 210 ≈ 5.71, meaning: grams per rupee for C

    Now the largest number is the best value: B, then C, then A.

    Why the methods agree. "Rupees per gram" and "grams per rupee" are the same rate turned upside down. If one brand charges less for each gram, then each rupee must buy more grams. Turning a set of numbers upside down reverses their order, so the ranking is the same, but "best" moves from the smallest number to the largest.

    Alternative reasoning

    Price of 100 g (a handy unit when the weights are in grams): A costs 81 ÷ 4.5 = ₹18, B costs 119 ÷ 7 = ₹17, and C costs 210 ÷ 12 = ₹17.50.

    Check the answer

    B is cheapest per kilogram even though its pack is not the largest. "The biggest pack is always the best value" is a tempting rule, and here it is false: C is bigger than B but costs more per kilogram.

    General lesson

    To compare offers, compare like with like: the same quantity of goods, or the same amount of money. Both ways must agree, and if they do not, there is an arithmetic mistake.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Comparison, Alternative solution.

    Level 2, question 6 of 15 (problem 21 of 140)

    Question 2.6Cheetah against car

    Difficulty 3 of 4: Challenge

    A cheetah sprints 250 m in 9 seconds (treat its speed as steady during the sprint). A car travels at a steady 90 km/h.

    1. Which is faster during the cheetah's sprint? Compare them in metres per second, then in kilometres per hour.
    2. How far does the car travel in those 9 seconds?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    How many metres are in 90 km, and how many seconds are in an hour?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. The cheetah is faster: about 27.78 m/s against the car's 25 m/s, which is 100 km/h against 90 km/h.
    2. The car travels 225 m, which is 25 m less than the cheetah covers in the same 9 seconds.

    Understand the situation

    The two speeds are given in different units, metres per second and kilometres per hour. Before comparing, we must convert them to the same unit.

    Find the mathematical structure

    A speed is a rate: distance per unit of time. Converting units is the bridge of one twice over: change the distance unit and the time unit, keeping the same motion.

    Build the reasoning

    Car in metres per second. 90 km is 90,000 m, and 1 hour is 3,600 s.

    90,000 ÷ 3,600 = 25, meaning: metres per second for the car

    Cheetah in metres per second.

    250 ÷ 9 ≈ 27.78, meaning: metres per second for the cheetah

    So the cheetah is faster.

    Cheetah in kilometres per hour. An hour is 3,600 seconds, which is 400 lots of 9 seconds.

    3,600 ÷ 9 = 400, meaning: nine-second periods in one hour

    400 × 250 = 100,000, meaning: metres the cheetah would cover in an hour at this speed

    That is 100 km, so the cheetah's speed is 100 km/h, faster than the car's 90 km/h.

    (b)

    25 × 9 = 225, meaning: metres travelled by the car in 9 seconds

    Develop intuition

    Every 1 m/s is the same as 3.6 km/h, because 1 m each second becomes 3,600 m, or 3.6 km, each hour. So 25 m/s is 25 × 3.6 = 90 km/h, as expected.

    Check the answer

    Real cheetahs reach roughly 100 km/h, but only for a short sprint of a few hundred metres. Saying "the cheetah is faster than the car" is true for this sprint, not for a long journey. A rate describes a situation only while it lasts.

    General lesson

    Never compare rates in different units. Convert both, carefully, using the bridge of one: 1 km = 1,000 m, 1 hour = 3,600 seconds, so 1 m/s = 3.6 km/h.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Comparison, Transfer.

    Level 2, question 7 of 15 (problem 22 of 140)

    Question 2.7Tins of paint

    Difficulty 2 of 4: Core

    One litre of paint covers 12 m² of wall. A room's walls have a total area of 130 m², and they need two coats of paint. Paint is sold only in 4-litre tins.

    1. How many litres of paint are needed? Give the exact amount.
    2. How many tins must be bought?
    3. How much paint will be left over?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Two coats means the paint has to cover the walls twice. And a shop will not sell you part of a tin.

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. 21 and 2 over 3 litres (about 21.67 litres). (b) 6 tins. (c) 2 and 1 over 3 litres (about 2.33 litres) left over.

    Understand the situation

    The paint must cover the walls twice, so the area to be painted is double the wall area. Tins are whole, so the number of tins must be a whole number that gives at least enough paint.

    Build the reasoning

    Area to cover:

    2 × 130 = 260, meaning: square metres of painting (two coats)

    Paint needed: each litre covers 12 m².

    260 ÷ 12 = 21 and 2 over 3, meaning: litres of paint

    Tins:

    21 and 2 over 3 ÷ 4 = 5 and 5 over 12, meaning: tins' worth of paint

    5 tins hold only 20 litres, which is not enough. So we must round up to 6 tins.

    Left over:

    6 × 4 = 24, meaning: litres bought

    24 − 21 and 2 over 3 = 2 and 1 over 3, meaning: litres left over

    Check the answer

    5 tins (20 litres) would cover 20 × 12 = 240 m², short of the 260 m² needed. 6 tins (24 litres) cover 288 m², which is enough.

    Why not round to the nearest whole number?

    5 and 5 over 12 rounds to 5 to the nearest whole number. But with 5 tins, 20 m² of wall would be left with only one coat. When the answer counts things you must have enough of, round up, even if the decimal part is small.

    General lesson

    Rounding is a decision about the situation, not a rule about digits. Ask: must I have at least this much (round up), can I use only complete items (round down), or do I just want a sensible approximate value (round to the nearest)?

    Thinking skills in this problem: Direct application, Model selection.

    Level 2, question 8 of 15 (problem 23 of 140)

    Question 2.8Bows from a ribbon

    Difficulty 2 of 4: Core

    Sana has a 10 m ribbon. Each bow she makes needs 0.35 m of ribbon.

    1. How many complete bows can she make?
    2. How much ribbon is left over?
    3. Suppose instead that her ribbon had been longer. What is the shortest length of ribbon that would let her make 30 bows from that one ribbon?
    4. Instead, Sana keeps her 10 m ribbon and buys a separate new piece of the same ribbon for the extra bows. Pieces of ribbon cannot be joined together. What is the shortest new piece that lets her end up with 30 bows? Why is the answer not 30 × 0.35 − 10 = 0.5 m?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Try working in centimetres. How many 35 cm pieces fit into 1,000 cm?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. 28 bows. (b) 0.2 m (20 cm) left over.
    2. 10.5 m, which is 0.5 m longer than her ribbon.
    3. 0.7 m (70 cm). After 28 bows her leftover 20 cm is too short for a bow and cannot be joined to the new piece, so the new piece must hold 2 whole bows. The answer 0.5 m would be right only if the 20 cm scrap could be joined on.

    Understand the situation

    We must find how many 0.35 m pieces fit into 10 m. A bow needs the full 0.35 m, so a shorter leftover piece is useless for a bow.

    Find the mathematical structure

    Number of bows = total length ÷ length per bow, rounded down to a whole number, because only complete pieces count.

    Build the reasoning

    Change to centimetres to make the numbers whole: 10 m = 1,000 cm and 0.35 m = 35 cm.

    1,000 ÷ 35 = 28 and 4 over 7, meaning: pieces of 35 cm that would fit

    28 × 35 = 980, meaning: centimetres used by 28 bows

    1,000 − 980 = 20, meaning: centimetres left over

    So Sana can make 28 complete bows, with 20 cm (0.2 m) left.

    (c) One unbroken ribbon long enough for 30 bows:

    30 × 0.35 = 10.5, meaning: metres of ribbon for 30 bows

    10.5 − 10 = 0.5, meaning: metres longer than her ribbon

    (d) With a separate piece, the 20 cm scrap from the first ribbon is wasted, because a bow needs 35 cm in one piece.

    30 − 28 = 2, meaning: bows still to make

    2 × 35 = 70, meaning: centimetres, the shortest new piece that holds 2 whole bows

    Check the answer

    • A 29th bow would need 29 × 0.35 = 10.15 m of ribbon, which is more than 10 m. So 28 really is the maximum.
    • (d) A new 0.5 m piece would give only one more bow (35 cm), leaving a 15 cm scrap. The two scraps, 20 cm and 15 cm, add up to 35 cm but cannot be joined, so Sana would have only 29 bows.

    Develop intuition

    Rounding down is right here for the opposite reason to rounding up in the paint question: the leftover 20 cm is real ribbon, but it is not enough to make a complete bow.

    General lesson

    When you divide to find "how many fit", the remainder matters. Keep it: it tells you what is left over, and whether a leftover can be used depends on the situation (here, pieces of ribbon cannot be joined).

    Thinking skills in this problem: Reverse reasoning, Direct application.

    Level 2, question 9 of 15 (problem 24 of 140)

    Question 2.9Two hours thirty

    Difficulty 2 of 4: Core

    A car travels 150 km in 2 hours 30 minutes. Sam finds its average speed like this: "150 ÷ 2.30 is about 65.2, so the speed is 65.2 km/h."

    1. What is wrong with Sam's working?
    2. Find the correct average speed.
    3. How long a time does "2.30 hours" really stand for?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    How many hours is 30 minutes? Is it 0.30 of an hour?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. Sam treated "2 hours 30 minutes" as the decimal 2.30 hours. But 30 minutes is half an hour, so the time is 2.5 hours.
    2. 60 km/h.
    3. 2.30 hours is 2 hours and 0.3 × 60 = 18 minutes, which is 2 hours 18 minutes.

    Understand the situation

    We do not count time in tens and hundreds: an hour has 60 minutes, not 100, so the digits after the decimal point of an hour are not minutes.

    Build the reasoning

    Convert the time correctly. 30 minutes is 30 over 60 = 1 over 2 of an hour.

    2 + 30 over 60 = 2.5, meaning: hours

    Average speed:

    150 ÷ 2.5 = 60, meaning: kilometres per hour

    What 2.30 hours means:

    0.3 × 60 = 18, meaning: minutes in 0.3 of an hour

    Alternative reasoning

    Work in minutes: 2 hours 30 minutes is 150 minutes. The car covers 150 km in 150 minutes, so 1 km each minute. There are 60 minutes in an hour, so 60 km in an hour.

    Check the answer

    At 60 km/h, in 2.5 hours the car covers 60 × 2.5 = 150 km. Sam's 65.2 km/h would cover the 150 km in about 2 hours 18 minutes, which is exactly the time his mistaken "2.30 hours" stands for.

    General lesson

    Before using a time in a calculation, convert it fully into one unit: hours as a fraction or decimal, or minutes. "2 h 30 min" is 2.5 h, not 2.30 h.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Error diagnosis.

    Level 2, question 10 of 15 (problem 25 of 140)

    Question 2.10Two-fifths of a tank

    Difficulty 3 of 4: Challenge

    A tap fills 2 over 5 of a tank in 3 over 4 of an hour, at a steady rate.

    1. How long does it take to fill the whole tank? Give your answer in hours and minutes (and seconds if needed).
    2. What fraction of the tank does it fill in one hour?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    If 2 over 5 takes 3 over 4 hour, how long does 1 over 5 take?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. 1 and 7 over 8 hours, which is 1 hour 52 minutes 30 seconds.
    2. 8 over 15 of the tank.

    Understand the situation

    Both the amount (a fraction of the tank) and the time (a fraction of an hour) are fractions. The structure is unchanged: a steady rate means equal parts of the tank take equal times.

    Build the reasoning

    (a) Bridge through one fifth. 2 over 5 of the tank is two fifths. Each fifth takes half of 3 over 4 hour.

    3 over 4 ÷ 2 = 3 over 8, meaning: hours to fill 1 over 5 of the tank

    5 × 3 over 8 = 15 over 8, meaning: hours to fill all 5 fifths

    15 over 8 = 1 and 7 over 8, meaning: hours

    Now convert 7 over 8 of an hour into minutes and seconds:

    7 over 8 × 60 = 52.5, meaning: minutes

    That is 52 minutes and 30 seconds, so the whole tank takes 1 hour 52 minutes 30 seconds.

    (b) Fraction per hour. Divide the amount by the time:

    2 over 5 ÷ 3 over 4 = 8 over 15, meaning: of the tank each hour

    Check the answer

    • 8 over 15 of the tank per hour for 15 over 8 hours fills 8 over 15 × 15 over 8 = 1 whole tank.
    • 2 over 5 is less than half the tank and takes 3 over 4 hour, so the whole tank should take more than 2 × 3 over 4 = 1 and 1 over 2 hours. 1 and 7 over 8 hours fits.
    • 8 over 15 is a bit more than half, which agrees: in one hour the tap fills a bit more than half the tank.

    Develop intuition

    Draw the tank as 5 equal slices. Each slice takes 3 over 8 of an hour, which is 22.5 minutes. Five slices take 5 × 22.5 = 112.5 minutes, which is 1 hour 52.5 minutes.

    General lesson

    Fractions do not change the method. Find what one simple piece (one fifth of the tank) needs, then build up. Dividing amount by time gives the rate; dividing the whole job by the rate gives the time.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Direct application, Reverse reasoning.

    Level 2, question 11 of 15 (problem 26 of 140)

    Question 2.11Two ways to measure fuel use

    Difficulty 3 of 4: Challenge

    Car A travels 18 km on each litre of petrol. Car B uses 6 litres of petrol for every 100 km.

    1. Which car is more fuel-efficient? Show your working.
    2. For one of these two ways of measuring, a bigger number is better; for the other, a smaller number is better. Explain which is which, and why.
    3. How much petrol would each car use on a 450 km trip?

    Not marked yet.
