    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Put both cars into "kilometres per litre", or both into "litres per 100 km".

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. Car A. Car B manages only 16 and 2 over 3 km per litre (about 16.67), less than Car A's 18 km per litre. Equivalently, Car A uses about 5.56 litres per 100 km, less than Car B's 6.
    2. In kilometres per litre, bigger is better (each litre takes you further). In litres per 100 km, smaller is better (each trip needs less fuel).
    3. Car A uses 25 litres; Car B uses 27 litres.

    Understand the situation

    The two cars' fuel use is described in opposite ways: one as distance for each unit of fuel, the other as fuel for each unit of distance. We need the same description for both.

    Build the reasoning

    Car B in kilometres per litre. 6 litres take it 100 km.

    100 ÷ 6 = 16 and 2 over 3, meaning: kilometres per litre for B

    Car A in litres per 100 km. Each litre takes it 18 km.

    100 ÷ 18 = 5 and 5 over 9, meaning: litres per 100 km for A (about 5.56)

    Either way, Car A is more efficient.

    (c) A 450 km trip.

    450 ÷ 18 = 25, meaning: litres for Car A

    450 ÷ 100 × 6 = 27, meaning: litres for Car B (4.5 lots of 100 km)

    Why "bigger is better" flips

    "Kilometres per litre" measures how far a litre takes you; an efficient car goes further, so bigger is better. "Litres per 100 km" measures how much fuel a fixed distance costs; an efficient car needs less, so smaller is better. The two measures are the same rate turned upside down, so improving the car pushes one number up and the other down.

    Check the answer

    In (c), the difference of 2 litres over 450 km agrees with the difference in "litres per 100 km": (6 − 5 and 5 over 9) × 4.5 = 4 over 9 × 4.5 = 2 litres.

    General lesson

    A rate can be written either way up: "per litre" or "per kilometre". Before comparing, decide which way up you are using, and remember what a bigger number means in that form.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Comparison, Model selection.

    Level 2, question 12 of 15 (problem 27 of 140)

    Question 2.12Price per kilogram, roughly first

    Difficulty 2 of 4: Core

    A 1.75 kg pack of dried fruit costs ₹389.

    1. Without dividing exactly, decide whether the price per kilogram is more or less than ₹200. Then find two whole-number amounts of rupees, at most ₹10 apart, between which the price per kilogram must lie.
    2. Calculate the price per kilogram exactly, and round it to the nearest rupee.

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Try multiplying instead of dividing: what would 1.75 kg cost at ₹200 per kilogram? At ₹250? At ₹220?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. More than ₹200, because 1.75 kg at ₹200 per kilogram would cost only ₹350. The price lies between, for example, ₹220 and ₹225, because 1.75 × 220 = 385 and 1.75 × 225 = 393.75.
    2. Exactly ₹222 and 2 over 7 per kilogram (about ₹222.29), which is ₹222 to the nearest rupee.

    Understand the situation

    Dividing by 1.75 is awkward to do in your head, but multiplying by 1.75 is easy: 1.75 is 1 + 3 over 4. So we test guesses by multiplying.

    Build the reasoning

    (a) Test some prices.

    1.75 × 200 = 350, meaning: too little, so the price per kg is above ₹200

    1.75 × 250 = 437.5, meaning: too much, so it is below ₹250

    1.75 × 220 = 385, meaning: just under ₹389, so above ₹220

    1.75 × 225 = 393.75, meaning: just over ₹389, so below ₹225

    (b) Exact. 1.75 kg is 7 over 4 kg, and dividing by 7 over 4 is the same as multiplying by 4 over 7.

    389 × 4 over 7 = 1556 over 7, meaning: rupees per kilogram

    1556 over 7 = 222 and 2 over 7, meaning: rupees per kilogram

    222 and 2 over 7 ≈ 222.29, meaning: to the nearest paisa

    To the nearest rupee: ₹222, because 2 over 7 is less than one half.

    Check the answer

    1.75 × 222 = 388.50, which is just under ₹389, consistent with a price slightly more than ₹222.

    General lesson

    To estimate a division, turn it into a multiplication and test friendly guesses. Two guesses, one too small and one too big, trap the answer between them and protect you from slips with the decimal point.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Estimation and bounds.

    Level 2, question 13 of 15 (problem 28 of 140)

    Question 2.13Litres for the money

    Difficulty 1 of 4: Foundation

    Petrol costs ₹104.50 per litre. Riya pays ₹1,672 for petrol.

    1. How many litres does she buy?
    2. The next week the price rises to ₹106.40 per litre. How many litres does ₹1,672 buy now? Give your answer to two decimal places.
    3. Explain why the answer to (b) is smaller than the answer to (a).

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    How many lots of ₹104.50 make ₹1,672?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. 16 litres. (b) 15.71 litres (to two decimal places). (c) The money is fixed; when each litre costs more, the same money buys fewer litres.

    Understand the situation

    In (a) we know the cost and the price per litre, and want the number of litres: a reverse question. In (b) the amount of money stays the same but the price changes.

    Build the reasoning

    (a) The number of litres is the number of ₹104.50 portions in ₹1,672.

    1,672 ÷ 104.5 = 16, meaning: litres

    (b)

    1,672 ÷ 106.4 ≈ 15.71, meaning: litres, to two decimal places

    (c) Litres × price per litre = money spent. With the money fixed at ₹1,672, a larger price per litre must be balanced by a smaller number of litres.

    Check the answer

    • (a) 16 × 104.50 = 1,672.
    • (b) 15.71 × 106.40 = 1,671.544, so the amount is just under ₹1,672, as expected after rounding the litres down slightly.

    General lesson

    Dividing money by price per unit gives the number of units. When the total money is fixed, price and quantity move in opposite directions. You will study this kind of relationship properly in Level 3.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Reverse reasoning, Changed conditions.

    Level 2, question 14 of 15 (problem 29 of 140)

    Question 2.14When the big pack is worse

    Difficulty 2 of 4: Core

    Many shoppers believe that "the bigger pack is always better value".

    1. Invent two offers for the same product, with different pack sizes and prices, for which the bigger pack is worse value. Prove it using the price of the same quantity.
    2. Describe a simple rule for making as many such examples as you like.

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Choose the small pack first and work out its price per kilogram. Now price the big pack slightly above that rate.

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. One example: 500 g of oats for ₹60 and 1 kg of oats for ₹130. The small pack costs ₹120 per kilogram and the big pack costs ₹130 per kilogram, so the big pack is worse value. (Many other examples work.)
    2. Find the small pack's price per unit, multiply it by the big pack's quantity, and price the big pack at more than that amount.

    Proving the example

    Compare the price of 1 kg in each pack. The small pack holds 0.5 kg:

    60 ÷ 0.5 = 120, meaning: rupees per kilogram, small pack

    130 ÷ 1 = 130, meaning: rupees per kilogram, big pack

    Another way: two small packs give you the same 1 kg for 2 × ₹60 = ₹120, which is ₹10 less than the big pack.

    Making more examples

    Suppose the small pack holds s units and costs p rupees. Its price per unit is p ÷ s. A big pack holding b units would cost b × p ÷ s rupees at the same rate. Price it at anything above that amount and the big pack is worse value.

    For instance: small pack 200 g for ₹50 (₹25 per 100 g). A 1 kg pack at the same rate would cost ₹250, so a 1 kg pack priced at ₹260 or ₹299 is worse value.

    Why the belief exists

    Shops often do give lower prices per unit on big packs, because packing and handling cost less per kilogram. But "often" is not "always". A single counterexample is enough to show that a general rule is false.

    General lesson

    Never trust a rule of thumb about value: compute the price of the same quantity. To disprove a general claim, one clear counterexample is enough.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Problem construction, Counterexample.

    Level 2, question 15 of 15 (problem 30 of 140)

    Question 2.15Juice machine at the fair

    Difficulty 3 of 4: Challenge

    A juice machine fills cups one at a time, at a steady rate of 3 cups every 8 seconds. Assume no time is lost changing cups.

    1. How long does it take to fill 250 cups? Give the time in minutes and seconds.
    2. How many cups are completely filled in 5 minutes?
    3. Can one machine fill 400 cups in 15 minutes? Explain, and find how long it takes to fill 400 cups.

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    How long does one cup take? How many cups per second does the machine fill?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. 666 and 2 over 3 seconds, which is 11 minutes 6 and 2 over 3 seconds (about 11 minutes 7 seconds).
    2. 112 cups (the 113th cup is only half full).
    3. No. In 15 minutes it fills only 337 complete cups. 400 cups need 17 minutes 46 and 2 over 3 seconds (about 17 minutes 47 seconds).

    Understand the situation

    The rate is given in an unusual way: 3 cups per 8 seconds. We can turn it into "seconds per cup" (useful for finding times) or "cups per second" (useful for finding numbers of cups).

    Find the mathematical structure

    • Time = (seconds per cup) × (number of cups).
    • Cups = (cups per second) × (number of seconds), rounded down if we count only full cups.

    Build the reasoning

    Seconds for one cup:

    8 ÷ 3 = 8 over 3, meaning: seconds for each cup (that is 2 and 2 over 3 seconds)

    (a)

    250 × 8 over 3 = 666 and 2 over 3, meaning: seconds for 250 cups

    11 × 60 = 660, meaning: seconds in 11 minutes

    666 and 2 over 3 − 660 = 6 and 2 over 3, meaning: seconds more

    So 250 cups take 11 minutes 6 and 2 over 3 seconds.

    (b) 5 minutes is 300 seconds.

    300 × 3 over 8 = 112.5, meaning: cups' worth of juice in 300 seconds

    Only 112 cups are complete; the 113th is half full.

    (c) 15 minutes is 900 seconds.

    900 × 3 over 8 = 337.5, meaning: cups' worth in 15 minutes

    That is only 337 complete cups, so one machine cannot fill 400 cups in 15 minutes.

    400 × 8 over 3 = 1066 and 2 over 3, meaning: seconds needed for 400 cups

    1066 and 2 over 3 − 17 × 60 = 46 and 2 over 3, meaning: seconds after 17 minutes

    So it needs 17 minutes 46 and 2 over 3 seconds.

    Check the answer

    • (a) A rough check: 3 cups per 8 seconds is a bit more than 1 cup every 3 seconds, so 250 cups take a bit less than 750 seconds (12.5 minutes). 666 seconds fits.
    • (b) 112 cups take 112 × 8 over 3 = 298 and 2 over 3 seconds, just under 300. The 113th cup would finish at 301 and 1 over 3 seconds, just after 5 minutes.

    General lesson

    A rate like "3 cups per 8 seconds" can be turned either way up. Choose the form that matches the question: "seconds per cup" to find a time, "cups per second" to find a number. Then round according to what the question counts.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Direct application, Estimation and bounds.

    Level 3, question 1 of 15 (problem 31 of 140)

    Question 3.1Building a wall

    Difficulty 1 of 4: Foundation

    12 workers, all working at the same rate, can build a wall in 10 days. How many days would 15 such workers take to build the same wall?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    How many days would it take one worker on their own?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    8 days.

    Understand the situation

    The wall is the same, so the amount of work is the same. What changes is how many people share that work. More workers should finish sooner.

    Find the mathematical structure

    • What stays the same: the total amount of work needed for the wall.
    • Measuring that work: 12 workers working for 10 days put in 12 × 10 = 120 worker-days of work. (One worker-day is the work one worker does in one day.) Any team that builds this wall must put in these same 120 worker-days.
    • Connection: (number of workers) × (number of days) = 120. When the number of workers goes up, the number of days must come down so that the product stays 120.

    Two quantities whose product stays fixed are in inverse proportion. This model assumes every worker works at the same rate, the same hours each day, and that extra workers do not get in each other's way.

    Build the reasoning

    Step 1: the fixed total.

    12 × 10 = 120, meaning: worker-days needed for the wall

    Step 2: share it among 15 workers. Each day the team supplies 15 worker-days of work.

    120 ÷ 15 = 8, meaning: days for 15 workers

    The bridge of one appears here too: one worker alone would need 120 days, so 15 workers need 120 ÷ 15 = 8 days.

    Develop intuition

    Think of the wall as needing 120 tickets of work, and each worker hands in one ticket per day. With 15 workers, 15 tickets come in each day, so it takes 8 days to collect all 120.

    Check the answer

    • Direction: more workers, fewer days. 8 is less than 10.
    • Size: 15 workers is 5 over 4 times as many as 12, so the time should be 4 over 5 of 10 days, which is 8 days.
    • Tempting error: 10 × 15 ÷ 12 = 12.5 days. That says more workers take longer, which fails the direction check.

    General lesson

    When the total amount of work is fixed, find it first, in worker-days. Then the number of days is that total divided by the number of workers. Always check the direction: more workers must mean less time.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Direct application.

    Level 3, question 2 of 15 (problem 32 of 140)

    Question 3.2Faster on the highway

    Difficulty 2 of 4: Core

    At a steady 60 km/h, a journey takes 3 hours 20 minutes. How long does the same journey take at a steady 80 km/h?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    What stays the same between the two journeys? Find it first.

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    2 hours 30 minutes.

    Understand the situation

    The road and the destination do not change, only the speed. A faster car should take less time.

    Find the mathematical structure

    The fixed quantity is the distance. Distance = speed × time, so speed and time are in inverse proportion when the distance is fixed.

    Build the reasoning

    Step 1: the distance. 3 hours 20 minutes is 3 and 1 over 3 hours, because 20 minutes is 20 over 60 = 1 over 3 of an hour.

    60 × 3 and 1 over 3 = 200, meaning: kilometres in the journey

    Step 2: the new time.

    200 ÷ 80 = 2.5, meaning: hours at 80 km/h

    2.5 hours is 2 hours 30 minutes.

    Alternative reasoning

    Scale the time directly. The speed is multiplied by 80 over 60 = 4 over 3, so the time is multiplied by 3 over 4. In minutes, 3 hours 20 minutes is 200 minutes.

    200 × 3 over 4 = 150, meaning: minutes at the faster speed

    150 minutes is 2 hours 30 minutes. This route never needs the distance at all.

    Check the answer

    • At 80 km/h for 2.5 hours the car covers 80 × 2.5 = 200 km, the same distance.
    • Faster speed, less time: 2 h 30 min is less than 3 h 20 min.

    General lesson

    In inverse proportion, if one quantity is multiplied by a fraction, the other is multiplied by that fraction turned upside down. Find the fixed quantity, or use the upside-down factor directly.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Direct application, Alternative solution.

    Level 3, question 3 of 15 (problem 33 of 140)

    Question 3.3Food at the camp

    Difficulty 1 of 4: Foundation

    A camp has enough food to feed 40 children for 15 days. Each child eats the same amount every day.

    1. How long would the same food last 50 children?
    2. How many children could the food feed for 20 days?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Measure the food in "child-days": how many days the food would last just one child.

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. 12 days. (b) 30 children.

    Find the mathematical structure

    The total food is fixed. Measure it in child-days: one child-day is the food one child eats in one day. The food is 40 × 15 = 600 child-days. Then (number of children) × (number of days) = 600.

    Build the reasoning

    40 × 15 = 600, meaning: child-days of food

    (a)

    600 ÷ 50 = 12, meaning: days the food lasts 50 children

    (b) Now the number of days is given and we want the number of children.

    600 ÷ 20 = 30, meaning: children it can feed for 20 days

    Develop intuition

    600 child-days means that one child could eat from this store for 600 days. Share the store among more children and it runs out sooner; make it last longer and fewer children can share it.

    Check the answer

    • (a) More children, fewer days: 12 is less than 15. And 50 × 12 = 600.
    • (b) More days, fewer children: 30 is less than 40. And 30 × 20 = 600.

    General lesson

    Inverse proportion problems can be asked in either direction. The fixed total (here, child-days of food) answers both.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Direct application, Reverse reasoning.

    Level 3, question 4 of 15 (problem 34 of 140)

    Question 3.4Rectangles with the same area

    Difficulty 2 of 4: Core

    A rectangle is 24 cm long and 15 cm wide.

    1. A second rectangle has the same area and is 30 cm long. How wide is it?
    2. For rectangles with this area, what happens to the width when the length is multiplied by 4 over 3?
    3. Find three more rectangles with this area whose length and width are both whole numbers of centimetres.

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    What stays the same for all these rectangles? Write it as "length × width = ?".

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. 12 cm. (b) The width is multiplied by 3 over 4. (c) For example: 36 cm by 10 cm, 40 cm by 9 cm and 45 cm by 8 cm (any other pair of whole numbers that multiply to 360 works).

    Find the mathematical structure

    The area is fixed at 24 × 15 = 360 cm². For every rectangle with this area, length × width = 360. So length and width are in inverse proportion.

    Build the reasoning

    24 × 15 = 360, meaning: square centimetres of area

    (a)

    360 ÷ 30 = 12, meaning: centimetres wide

    (b) If the length is multiplied by 4 over 3, the product would be multiplied by 4 over 3 as well, unless the width is multiplied by 3 over 4 to undo it, because 4 over 3 × 3 over 4 = 1. Example: from 24 cm long and 15 cm wide to 32 cm long and 15 × 3 over 4 = 45 over 4 = 11.25 cm wide. Check: 32 × 11.25 = 360.

    (c) We need pairs of whole numbers that multiply to 360 (factor pairs of 360). Some examples:

    Length (cm)Width (cm)Area (cm²)
    3610360
    409360
    458360
    606360
    2018360

    Develop intuition

    Imagine 360 unit squares of paper, each 1 cm by 1 cm. You can arrange them in rows to make many different rectangles. Longer rows mean fewer rows, but the number of squares never changes.

    Check the answer

    1. The new rectangle is longer, so it must be narrower: 12 cm is less than 15 cm. And 30 × 12 = 360.

    General lesson

    "Same area" is a fixed total just like "same job" or "same distance". When a product is fixed, multiplying one factor by a fraction multiplies the other by the same fraction turned upside down. Whole-number versions of an inverse relationship are factor pairs.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Transfer, Direct application.

    Level 3, question 5 of 15 (problem 35 of 140)

    Question 3.5Notebooks on a budget

    Difficulty 2 of 4: Core

    Neha's money is exactly enough to buy 18 notebooks at ₹45 each, with nothing left over.

    1. How many notebooks costing ₹54 each could she buy with the same money?
    2. How many notebooks costing ₹50 each could she buy, and how much money would be left over?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    How much money does Neha have?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. 15 notebooks. (b) 16 notebooks, with ₹10 left over.

    Find the mathematical structure

    The fixed total is Neha's money: 18 × ₹45 = ₹810. Price × number of notebooks = money spent. With a fixed amount of money, a higher price means fewer notebooks.

    Build the reasoning

    18 × 45 = 810, meaning: rupees Neha has

    (a)

    810 ÷ 54 = 15, meaning: notebooks at ₹54

    (b)

    810 ÷ 50 = 16.2, meaning: notebooks' worth of money at ₹50

    She cannot buy 0.2 of a notebook, so she buys 16.

    16 × 50 = 800, meaning: rupees spent

    810 − 800 = 10, meaning: rupees left over

    Why (b) does not come out exactly

    In true inverse proportion the product stays exactly fixed. But notebooks come whole, so the number must be rounded down, and some money is left unspent. Real problems often follow an inverse pattern only approximately, because of whole-number limits.

    Check the answer

    • (a) 15 × ₹54 = ₹810 exactly.
    • (b) A 17th notebook would cost 17 × ₹50 = ₹850, which is more than ₹810.

    General lesson

    With a fixed budget, price and quantity are in inverse proportion. Find the budget first. When the items must be whole, round down and state the leftover money.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Direct application, Changed conditions.

    Level 3, question 6 of 15 (problem 36 of 140)

    Question 3.6Sharing a prize

    Difficulty 2 of 4: Core

    A prize of money is shared equally. When 8 people share it, each person gets ₹750.

    1. How many people would have to share it for each to get ₹500?
    2. Could the prize be shared equally so that each person gets exactly ₹700, with nothing left over? Explain.
    3. If 2 more people join the original 8, by how much does each person's share fall?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Find the total prize first. Then ask how many equal shares fit into it.

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. 12 people. (b) No. The prize is ₹6,000, and 6,000 ÷ 700 is not a whole number. (c) Each share falls from ₹750 to ₹600, a fall of ₹150.

    Find the mathematical structure

    The total prize is fixed. Number of people × share per person = total prize, so the number of people and the share are in inverse proportion. But the number of people must be a whole number.

    Build the reasoning

    8 × 750 = 6,000, meaning: rupees in the prize

    (a)

    6,000 ÷ 500 = 12, meaning: people

    (b)

    6,000 ÷ 700 = 8 and 4 over 7, meaning: people needed, which is not a whole number

    With 8 people each gets ₹750; with 9 people each gets ₹666 and 2 over 3. No whole number of people gives exactly ₹700.

    (c)

    6,000 ÷ 10 = 600, meaning: rupees each for 10 people

    750 − 600 = 150, meaning: rupees less for each person

    A surprise in (c)

    The number of people went up by a quarter (from 8 to 10), but each share went down by only a fifth (₹150 is 1 over 5 of ₹750). In inverse proportion, "up by a quarter" does not mean "down by a quarter". Multiplying by 5 over 4 on one side means multiplying by 4 over 5 on the other.

    Check the answer

    12 × ₹500 = ₹6,000 and 10 × ₹600 = ₹6,000, so both answers keep the total fixed.

    General lesson

    Fixed totals give inverse proportion, but whole-number conditions can make some values impossible. A share is possible exactly when it divides the total a whole number of times.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Reverse reasoning, Changed conditions.

    Level 3, question 7 of 15 (problem 37 of 140)

    Question 3.7Pumps against the clock

    Difficulty 2 of 4: Core

    6 identical pumps, working together, empty a tank in 50 minutes.

    1. How many such pumps are needed to empty the tank in 20 minutes?
    2. The tank must be emptied in 35 minutes or less. What is the smallest number of pumps that will do it, and how long will they take?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Measure the job in "pump-minutes": how long would one pump take on its own?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. 15 pumps. (b) 9 pumps, taking 33 and 1 over 3 minutes (33 minutes 20 seconds).

    Find the mathematical structure

    The job is fixed: 6 pumps × 50 minutes = 300 pump-minutes. (Number of pumps) × (minutes) = 300. Assumption: each pump removes water at the same steady rate, however many pumps are running.

    Build the reasoning

    6 × 50 = 300, meaning: pump-minutes to empty the tank

    (a)

    300 ÷ 20 = 15, meaning: pumps needed for 20 minutes

    (b)

    300 ÷ 35 = 8 and 4 over 7, meaning: pumps needed for exactly 35 minutes

    We cannot use part of a pump. 8 pumps are not enough:

    300 ÷ 8 = 37.5, meaning: minutes with 8 pumps, too slow

    So we need 9 pumps:

    300 ÷ 9 = 33 and 1 over 3, meaning: minutes with 9 pumps

    1 over 3 of a minute is 20 seconds, so 9 pumps take 33 minutes 20 seconds, within the limit.

    Check the answer

    • Fewer minutes need more pumps: 20 minutes needs more than 6 pumps.
    • (b) "35 minutes or less" is a deadline, so we round the number of pumps up. Rounding 8 and 4 over 7 to the nearest whole number would give 9 here, but for the right reason we must ask "is 8 enough?" and find that it is not.

    General lesson

    In inverse proportion, a deadline means "at least this many workers or machines": round up, then calculate the actual time with the whole number you chose.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Reverse reasoning, Model selection.

    Level 3, question 8 of 15 (problem 38 of 140)

    Question 3.8Soldiers in a fort

    Difficulty 3 of 4: Challenge

    A fort has enough food for 120 soldiers for 30 days. Each soldier eats the same amount every day. After 10 days, 40 soldiers leave the fort. For how many more days will the remaining food last the soldiers who stay?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Ignore the first 10 days once they are over. How much food is left, measured in soldier-days?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    The remaining food lasts 30 more days (so 40 days in total from the start).

    Understand the situation

    The story has two stages. During the first 10 days, 120 soldiers eat. Then the number of soldiers changes, and the food that is left must be shared by the soldiers who stay.

    Find the mathematical structure

    Measure food in soldier-days. The fixed total applies to the food remaining at the moment the change happens, not to the original store.

    Build the reasoning

    Food at the start:

    120 × 30 = 3,600, meaning: soldier-days of food

    Food eaten in the first 10 days:

    120 × 10 = 1,200, meaning: soldier-days eaten

    3,600 − 1,200 = 2,400, meaning: soldier-days left

    Soldiers who stay:

    120 − 40 = 80, meaning: soldiers remaining

    2,400 ÷ 80 = 30, meaning: more days

    Develop intuition

    After 10 days, the food left would last the original 120 soldiers for 20 more days. Only 80 soldiers remain, which is 2 over 3 of 120. Two-thirds as many mouths make the food last 3 over 2 times as long: 3 over 2 × 20 = 30 days.

    Check the answer

    • 80 soldiers × 30 days = 2,400 soldier-days, exactly the food left.
    • Tempting errors: using the full 30 days of food again ("120 × 30 ÷ 80 = 45 days"), which forgets that food was eaten in the first 10 days; or answering 20 days, which forgets that soldiers left.

    General lesson

    When conditions change part-way through, split the story into stages. Subtract what was used before the change, then apply inverse proportion to what remains.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Changed conditions.

    Level 3, question 9 of 15 (problem 39 of 140)

    Question 3.9Two upside-down answers

    Difficulty 2 of 4: Core

    Here are two pieces of working. Both are wrong.

    Riya: "4 identical taps fill a tank in 6 hours. So 8 taps take 12 hours, because twice the taps means twice the time."

    Arun: "9 workers take 16 days to finish a job. So 12 workers take 16 × 12 ÷ 9 = 21 and 1 over 3 days."

    1. Explain each mistake.
    2. Give the correct answer to each problem. Assume all taps are identical and all workers work at the same rate.
    3. Describe one quick check that would have caught both mistakes before any careful calculation.

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    With more taps or more workers, should the job take more time or less?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. Both treated an inverse relationship as if it were direct. More taps or workers share the same job, so the time should go down, not up.
    2. 8 taps take 3 hours; 12 workers take 12 days.
    3. A direction check: more workers or taps on the same job must mean less time.

    Build the reasoning

    Riya's problem. The fixed job is 4 × 6 = 24 tap-hours.

    4 × 6 = 24, meaning: tap-hours to fill the tank

    24 ÷ 8 = 3, meaning: hours for 8 taps

    Twice the taps share the same filling, so each hour twice as much water flows in, and the time halves.

    Arun's problem. The fixed job is 9 × 16 = 144 worker-days.

    9 × 16 = 144, meaning: worker-days in the job

    144 ÷ 12 = 12, meaning: days for 12 workers

    Arun multiplied by 12 over 9, which makes the time bigger. With more workers the time must shrink, so the factor must be the upside-down 9 over 12:

    16 × 9 over 12 = 12, meaning: days, the same answer

    The quick check

    Before calculating, ask: "Should the answer be bigger or smaller than the number I started with?" Both mistaken answers (12 hours and 21 and 1 over 3 days) are bigger than the starting times, although the teams got bigger. That alone shows something went wrong.

    General lesson

    In inverse proportion, the factor is the upside-down ratio. If you write the factor as a fraction, choose the one that makes the answer move in the right direction.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Error diagnosis, Estimation and bounds.

    Level 3, question 10 of 15 (problem 40 of 140)

    Question 3.10Direct, inverse or neither?

    Difficulty 2 of 4: Core

    For each pair of quantities, decide whether they are in direct proportion, inverse proportion, or neither. For direct and inverse proportion, say exactly what stays constant. For "neither", give a reason.

    1. The number of equal slices a cake is cut into, and the mass of each slice.
    2. The number of identical stamps bought, and their total cost.
    3. The speed of a car, and the time it takes to travel a fixed 100 km.
    4. The number of friends sharing a fixed taxi fare equally, and the amount each pays.
    5. The side length of a square, and its perimeter.
    6. The side length of a square, and its area.
    7. A child's age, and the child's height.
    8. The number of pages read each day, and the number of days needed to finish a 300-page book (count part of a day as a fraction of a day).

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    For each pair, test what happens when one quantity doubles. Does the other double, halve, or do something else?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    PairRelationshipWhat stays constant, or why neither
    (a) slices and mass of each sliceInversethe mass of the whole cake
    (b) stamps and total costDirectthe price of one stamp
    (c) speed and time for 100 kmInversethe distance, 100 km
    (d) friends and each shareInversethe total fare
    (e) side and perimeterDirectperimeter ÷ side, which is always 4
    (f) side and areaNeitherdoubling the side makes the area 4 times as large
    (g) age and heightNeithergrowth is not steady, and height at age 0 is not 0
    (h) pages per day and daysInversethe total number of pages, 300

    The test behind each verdict

    • Direct proportion: one quantity ÷ the other stays the same. Doubling one doubles the other. In (b), 2 stamps cost twice as much as 1. In (e), a square of side 3 cm has perimeter 12 cm, and a square of side 6 cm has perimeter 24 cm.
    • Inverse proportion: one quantity × the other stays the same. Doubling one halves the other. In (c), 50 km/h takes 2 hours and 100 km/h takes 1 hour: 50 × 2 = 100 × 1 = 100.
    • Neither: no ratio and no product stays constant. In (f), a square of side 1 cm has area 1 cm² and one of side 2 cm has area 4 cm². The ratio of area to side changes from 1 to 2, so it is not direct. The area increases as the side increases, so it cannot be inverse either.
    • In (g), a baby is about 50 cm long at birth, a 5-year-old might be about 110 cm, and a 10-year-old about 140 cm. Doubling the age from 5 to 10 does not double the height, and growth slows and stops altogether in adulthood.

    A detail in (h)

    The question counts part of a day as a fraction of a day, so pages per day × days is always exactly 300. For example, reading 40 pages a day takes 7 and 1 over 2 days, and 40 × 7 and 1 over 2 = 300. If only whole days counted, 40 pages a day would need 8 days (the book is finished on the 8th day), and the relationship would be only roughly inverse.

    General lesson

    Do not decide by feel. Test: does the ratio stay fixed (direct), does the product stay fixed (inverse), or neither? "One goes up when the other goes up" is not enough for direct proportion, and "one goes down when the other goes up" is not enough for inverse proportion.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Model selection.

    Level 3, question 11 of 15 (problem 41 of 140)

    Question 3.11Faster by a quarter

    Difficulty 3 of 4: Challenge

    A car drives a 120 km route at a steady 60 km/h.

    1. If it drives twice as fast, how does the time change? Explain using what stays the same.
    2. Priya says: "If the car's speed goes up by a quarter (that is, by 25%), the time will go down by a quarter." Test her claim with the numbers, and explain the result.
    3. By what fraction must the speed go up so that the time goes down by a quarter?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Work out the actual times at 60 km/h and at 75 km/h. Compare them.

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. The time halves, from 2 hours to 1 hour, because the distance stays 120 km.
    2. Priya is wrong. The time goes from 120 minutes to 96 minutes, which is down by a fifth (20%), not a quarter.
    3. The speed must go up by a third (from 60 km/h to 80 km/h).

    Find the mathematical structure

    Distance = speed × time, and the distance is fixed at 120 km. So if the speed is multiplied by some number, the time must be multiplied by that number turned upside down.

    Build the reasoning

    (a)

    120 ÷ 60 = 2, meaning: hours at 60 km/h

    120 ÷ 120 = 1, meaning: hour at 120 km/h

    (b) Up by a quarter means multiplying the speed by 5 over 4:

    60 × 5 over 4 = 75, meaning: km/h

    120 ÷ 75 = 1.6, meaning: hours, which is 96 minutes

    120 − 96 = 24, meaning: minutes saved

    24 ÷ 120 = 1 over 5, meaning: of the original time saved

    Multiplying the speed by 5 over 4 multiplies the time by 4 over 5, which is a decrease of 1 over 5, not 1 over 4.

    (c) To cut the time by a quarter, the time is multiplied by 3 over 4. The speed must therefore be multiplied by 4 over 3, an increase of a third:

    60 × 4 over 3 = 80, meaning: km/h

    120 ÷ 80 = 1.5, meaning: hours, which is 3 over 4 of 2 hours

    Develop intuition

    Speed × time must stay at 120, so whatever multiplies the speed must be undone by the time. Multiplying the speed by 5 over 4 forces the time to be multiplied by 4 over 5, which removes 1 over 5 of it, not 1 over 4. "Up a quarter, then down a quarter" cannot keep a product fixed: 5 over 4 × 3 over 4 = 15 over 16, which is not 1. (In direct proportion it is different: both quantities are multiplied by the same factor, so a rise of a quarter in one gives a rise of a quarter in the other.)

    Check the answer

    75 km/h × 1.6 h = 120 km and 80 km/h × 1.5 h = 120 km, so the distance is the same in every case.

    General lesson

    In inverse proportion, equal factors cancel (×2 and ÷2), but equal percentage changes do not. Always change one quantity by a factor, then turn the factor upside down for the other.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Counterexample, Proof and explanation.

    Level 3, question 12 of 15 (problem 42 of 140)

    Question 3.12Which team works harder?

    Difficulty 3 of 4: Challenge

    Two teams were each given an identical job.

    • Team A: 6 people finished their job in 8 days.
    • Team B: 5 people finished their job in 9 days.

    Both teams started on the same day. Within each team everyone works at the same rate, but the two teams' rates may differ.

    1. Team A finished first. Does that mean Team A's members work faster? Explain.
    2. Whose members work faster, and how many times as fast?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    How many person-days did each team need for the same job?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. No. Team A finished first because it had more people, not because its members were faster.
    2. Team B's members work faster: each does 16 over 15 times as much per day as a Team A member.

    Understand the situation

    Finishing time depends on two things: how many people there are and how fast each works. To compare individual speed, we must remove the effect of team size.

    Find the mathematical structure

    Count the job in person-days for each team. Since the jobs are identical, a team whose members are faster needs fewer person-days for the same job.

    Build the reasoning

    6 × 8 = 48, meaning: person-days Team A needed

    5 × 9 = 45, meaning: person-days Team B needed

    Team B did the same job with fewer person-days, so each Team B person-day achieved more.

    How many times as fast? A Team A member does 1 over 48 of the job per day; a Team B member does 1 over 45 of it.

    1 over 45 ÷ 1 over 48 = 48 over 45, meaning: Team B rate compared with Team A rate

    48 over 45 = 16 over 15, meaning: in lowest terms

    So a Team B member works 16 over 15 times as fast, or 1 over 15 faster.

    Develop intuition

    Imagine one person from each team working alone on the job. The Team A person would need 48 days; the Team B person only 45 days.

    Check the answer

    If Team B's 5 people worked at Team A's rate, they would need 48 ÷ 5 = 9.6 days. They actually took only 9 days, so they must be faster than Team A's rate.

    General lesson

    The time a team takes mixes "how many" with "how fast". To compare efficiency, convert to a common unit such as person-days, and compare the amount of work done per person per day.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Comparison, Assumption check.

    Level 3, question 13 of 15 (problem 43 of 140)

    Question 3.13Meshing gears

    Difficulty 2 of 4: Core

    Two gears mesh, so that their teeth interlock and they turn together. Gear A has 36 teeth and gear B has 24 teeth.

    1. When A makes 10 complete turns, how many turns does B make?
    2. How many turns must A make so that B makes 45 turns?
    3. Explain why (number of teeth) × (number of turns) is the same for both gears.

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Watch the point where the gears touch. How many teeth of A pass that point in 10 turns? How many teeth of B must pass it too?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. 15 turns. (b) 30 turns. (c) At the point where the gears touch, every tooth of A that passes is matched by one tooth of B, so both gears pass the same number of teeth. That number is teeth × turns for each gear.

    Find the mathematical structure

    The fixed quantity is the number of teeth that pass the meeting point. For each gear it equals (teeth on the gear) × (turns). So teeth and turns are in inverse proportion: the gear with fewer teeth must turn more times.

    Build the reasoning

    (a)

    36 × 10 = 360, meaning: teeth of A that pass the meeting point

    360 ÷ 24 = 15, meaning: turns of B

    (b)

    24 × 45 = 1,080, meaning: teeth of B that pass the meeting point

    1,080 ÷ 36 = 30, meaning: turns of A

    Why the product is the same

    The teeth interlock, so they cannot slip. Each time one tooth of A moves past the meeting point, exactly one tooth of B moves past it too. So in any period of time the same number of teeth pass for both gears. A gear that makes t turns with n teeth passes n × t teeth, and this number must be equal for the two gears.

    Develop intuition

    A bicycle uses the same idea with a chain: a big front gear and a small back gear make the back wheel spin several times for each turn of the pedals.

    Check the answer

    B is smaller, so it must turn more often than A. Turns of A : turns of B = 10 : 15 = 2 : 3, which is the teeth ratio A : B = 36 : 24 = 3 : 2 turned upside down.

    General lesson

    Inverse proportion appears whenever two things share a fixed total. Here the shared total is the number of teeth passing a point. Finding the quantity that stays the same is the key in unfamiliar situations.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Transfer, Proof and explanation.

    Level 3, question 14 of 15 (problem 44 of 140)

    Question 3.14Inventing inverse situations

    Difficulty 3 of 4: Challenge

    1. Invent a real situation in which, when one quantity doubles, another halves. State exactly what stays constant, and use numbers to show that the product of the two quantities stays the same.
    2. Invent a real situation in which one quantity goes up while another goes down, but they are not in inverse proportion. Use numbers to show that their product changes.

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    For (b), think of something that decreases by the same amount each hour or each year.

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    Many answers are possible. For example:

    1. Sharing 24 sweets equally. 2 children get 12 each, 4 children get 6 each, 8 children get 3 each. The total, 24 sweets, stays constant: 2 × 12 = 4 × 6 = 8 × 3 = 24.
    2. A burning candle. A 20 cm candle burns 2 cm every hour. After 1 hour it is 18 cm long; after 2 hours it is 16 cm long. Time goes up and length goes down, but 1 × 18 = 18 while 2 × 16 = 32, so the product is not constant.

    Why (a) is inverse proportion

    In the sweets example, (number of children) × (sweets each) is always the total number of sweets. When the children double, each child's share must halve to keep that product at 24. Any situation where "something fixed is shared out" works the same way: a fixed distance shared between speed and time, a fixed job shared among workers, a fixed budget shared between price and number of items.

    Why (b) is not inverse proportion

    The candle loses a fixed amount each hour, rather than keeping a fixed product. Check what inverse proportion would predict: if length × time were constant at 18, then after 2 hours the length would be 9 cm, and after 3 hours 6 cm. In fact it is 16 cm and 14 cm. The candle also burns out completely after 10 hours, while an inverse relationship would never reach zero.

    1 × 18 = 18, meaning: product after 1 hour

    2 × 16 = 32, meaning: product after 2 hours, different

    Other good examples for (b): the money left in a purse as you spend ₹20 a day; the value of a phone that loses the same amount each year; the temperature of a cup of tea as it cools.

    General lesson

    "As one goes up, the other goes down" describes many relationships, but inverse proportion is a special one: the product stays fixed, so doubling one always halves the other. Test the product before using the inverse method.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Problem construction, Counterexample.

    Level 3, question 15 of 15 (problem 45 of 140)

    Question 3.15Hiring workers by the day

    Difficulty 2 of 4: Core

    7 workers can finish a job in 18 days, all working at the same rate.

    1. Before calculating exactly, decide between which two consecutive whole numbers the number of days for 11 workers must lie.
    2. Find the exact time for 11 workers.
    3. The workers can only be hired for whole days. For how many days must 11 workers be hired?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Find the job size in worker-days. Then ask: how many worker-days do 11 workers supply in 11 days, and in 12 days?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. Between 11 and 12 days. (b) 11 and 5 over 11 days. (c) 12 days.

    Find the mathematical structure

    The fixed total is 7 × 18 = 126 worker-days. Eleven workers supply 11 worker-days each day.

    Build the reasoning

    7 × 18 = 126, meaning: worker-days in the job

    (a) Test whole numbers of days:

    11 × 11 = 121, meaning: worker-days in 11 days, not quite enough

    11 × 12 = 132, meaning: worker-days in 12 days, more than enough

    So the time lies between 11 and 12 days.

    (b)

    126 ÷ 11 = 11 and 5 over 11, meaning: days

    (c) The job finishes during the 12th day, so the workers must be hired for 12 days.

    Alternative reasoning

    Estimate by scaling. 11 workers is a little more than 3 over 2 × 7 = 10.5 workers. With 3 over 2 times as many workers, the time would be 2 over 3 × 18 = 12 days. Slightly more workers means slightly less than 12 days. This agrees with (a).

    Check the answer

    11 workers for 11 and 5 over 11 days supply 11 × 11 and 5 over 11 = 126 worker-days, exactly the job.

    General lesson

    A quick multiplication test (workers × days) traps the answer between two whole numbers before you divide. Then let the situation decide how to round: if work cannot stop half-way through a hired day, round up.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Estimation and bounds, Direct application.

    Level 4, question 1 of 20 (problem 46 of 140)

    Question 4.1The taxi fare

    Difficulty 2 of 4: Core

    A taxi company charges a fixed ₹50 when you get in, plus a fixed amount for every kilometre travelled. A 6 km ride costs ₹170.

    1. Meera says a 12 km ride costs ₹340, because 12 km is twice 6 km. Is she right? Explain.
    2. Find the correct cost of a 12 km ride.
    3. What does a 1 km ride cost? Why is the answer not ₹170 ÷ 6?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Separate the ₹170 into the part you pay just for getting in and the part you pay for the kilometres.

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. No. Doubling the distance doubles only the distance charge, not the ₹50 starting charge.
    2. ₹290.
    3. ₹70. Dividing ₹170 by 6 spreads the ₹50 starting charge over the 6 km, as if it were part of the price of each kilometre. It is not: you pay it once per ride.

    Understand the situation

    The fare has two parts: a fixed charge of ₹50 that every ride pays, and a distance charge that grows with the kilometres.

    Find the mathematical structure

    Fare = 50 + (rate per km) × (distance). The distance charge is directly proportional to the distance, but the whole fare is not, because of the fixed ₹50. A quick test: in direct proportion, a ride of 0 km would cost ₹0. Here it would cost ₹50.

    Build the reasoning

    Find the rate per kilometre.

    170 − 50 = 120, meaning: rupees paid for the 6 km themselves

    120 ÷ 6 = 20, meaning: rupees per kilometre

    (b)

    50 + 12 × 20 = 290, meaning: rupees for 12 km

    (c)

    50 + 1 × 20 = 70, meaning: rupees for 1 km

    Develop intuition

    Meera's ₹340 would be the price of two separate 6 km rides, where you pay the ₹50 twice. In one 12 km ride you pay it only once, which is why the true fare is exactly ₹50 less than her answer.

    Check the answer

    • 12 km costs 50 + 240 = ₹290, and 6 km costs 50 + 120 = ₹170, as the question says.
    • The difference between Meera's answer and the correct one is 340 − 290 = 50, exactly the starting charge she counted twice.

    General lesson

    A fixed charge breaks direct proportion. Remove the fixed part first, use proportion on the part that really grows, then add the fixed part back.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Error diagnosis, Model selection.

    Level 4, question 2 of 20 (problem 47 of 140)

    Question 4.2Fence posts

    Difficulty 2 of 4: Core

    Posts are placed every 3 m along a straight fence 30 m long, with a post at each end.

    1. How many posts are needed?
    2. How many posts are needed for a straight fence 60 m long, set up in the same way?
    3. Rohan says, "Double the length, double the posts", and answers 22 for part (b). Explain his mistake.
    4. Posts are placed every 3 m, measured along the edge, around a circular pond whose edge is 30 m long. How many posts are needed?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Draw a short fence, say 9 m long, and count the posts and the gaps.

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. 11 posts. (b) 21 posts. (c) The number of gaps doubles (from 10 to 20), but the number of posts is always one more than the number of gaps on a straight fence. (d) 10 posts.

    Understand the situation

    The 3 m measures the gap between neighbouring posts, not the posts themselves. So the length tells us directly how many gaps there are, and we must then count the posts carefully.

    Find the mathematical structure

    • On a straight fence with a post at each end: posts = gaps + 1.
    • On a closed loop: posts = gaps, because the last gap ends at the first post.
    • The number of gaps is proportional to the length. The number of posts is not, because of the "+ 1".

    Build the reasoning

    (a)

    30 ÷ 3 = 10, meaning: gaps of 3 m

    10 + 1 = 11, meaning: posts on the straight fence

    (b)

    60 ÷ 3 = 20, meaning: gaps

    20 + 1 = 21, meaning: posts

    (d) Around the pond there are again 10 gaps, but walking all the way round you come back to the first post. Each post now has a gap after it, so the number of posts equals the number of gaps: 10.

    Develop intuition

    Picture a 9 m fence: posts at 0 m, 3 m, 6 m and 9 m. That is 4 posts for 3 gaps. Doubling to 18 m adds 3 more gaps, and each new gap needs only one new post at its far end, giving 7 posts, not 8.

    Check the answer

    Rohan's rule would give 2 × 11 = 22 posts. Joining two 30 m fences end to end needs only 21 posts, because the post where they meet is shared.

    General lesson

    Count the thing that is actually proportional. Gaps grow in proportion to length; posts do not, and the correction depends on whether the line is open or closed.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Counterexample, Transfer.

    Level 4, question 3 of 20 (problem 48 of 140)

    Question 4.3Cutting a log

    Difficulty 2 of 4: Core

    It takes 12 minutes to cut a log into 4 pieces. How long will it take to cut an identical log into 8 pieces? Each cut goes straight across the log, cuts through only one piece at a time, and takes the same time as every other cut.

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    How many cuts make 4 pieces?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    28 minutes.

    Understand the situation

    The time is spent on cuts, not on pieces. The question tells us about pieces, so we must translate.

    Find the mathematical structure

    Cutting a log straight across: pieces = cuts + 1. The time is proportional to the number of cuts.

    Build the reasoning

    4 − 1 = 3, meaning: cuts to make 4 pieces

    12 ÷ 3 = 4, meaning: minutes for each cut

    8 − 1 = 7, meaning: cuts to make 8 pieces

    7 × 4 = 28, meaning: minutes for 8 pieces

    Develop intuition

    Draw a log with 3 lines across it: you see 4 pieces. Every new cut adds just one piece.

    Check the answer

    The tempting answer, 24 minutes, comes from "twice the pieces, twice the time". But 8 pieces need 7 cuts, which is more than twice 3 cuts, so the time must be more than 24 minutes.

    General lesson

    Before using proportion, ask what the time (or cost) is really spent on. Here it is cuts, and cuts are one fewer than pieces.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Model selection, Error diagnosis.

    Level 4, question 4 of 20 (problem 49 of 140)

    Question 4.4Climbing the stairs

    Difficulty 2 of 4: Core

    In a building, each flight of stairs from one floor to the next has the same number of steps. Walking at a steady pace, Kabir takes 60 seconds to climb from the 1st floor to the 4th floor. How long does he take to climb from the 2nd floor to the 9th floor?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    How many flights of stairs are there between the 1st floor and the 4th floor?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    140 seconds (2 minutes 20 seconds).

    Understand the situation

    A floor number is a label for a position, like a house number, not an amount. The amount of climbing is the number of flights between the two floors.

    Find the mathematical structure

    Flights climbed = (top floor number) − (bottom floor number). The time is proportional to the number of flights. This works whatever the building calls its lowest floor, because only the difference matters.

    Build the reasoning

    4 − 1 = 3, meaning: flights from the 1st floor to the 4th floor

    60 ÷ 3 = 20, meaning: seconds per flight

    9 − 2 = 7, meaning: flights from the 2nd floor to the 9th floor

    7 × 20 = 140, meaning: seconds

    Check the answer

    Two tempting errors:

    • Treating floor numbers as amounts: "9 is 9 over 4 of 4, so 9 over 4 × 60 = 135 seconds".
    • Counting floors instead of flights: "the 1st to the 4th is 4 floors, so 15 seconds per floor, and the 2nd to the 9th is 8 floors, so 120 seconds".

    Both give the wrong answer because they do not count flights.

    Develop intuition

    Climbing from the 1st floor to the 4th floor, you walk up from 1 to 2, from 2 to 3 and from 3 to 4: three flights. You are already standing on the 1st floor, so it does not count.

    General lesson

    Labels such as floor numbers, page numbers, dates and times of day are positions. The amount between two positions is their difference, and that difference is what may be proportional.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Model selection, Transfer.

    Level 4, question 5 of 20 (problem 50 of 140)

    Question 4.5Shirts on the line

    Difficulty 2 of 4: Core

    On a sunny day, 3 shirts hung on a long washing line dry in 2 hours.

    1. How long would 6 shirts take to dry if they were all hung on the line at the same time?
    2. Now suppose the line has room for only 3 shirts at a time, and a shirt is taken down only when it is completely dry. How long would it take to dry 7 shirts? (Ignore the time taken to swap shirts.)

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Does one shirt wait for another before it starts drying?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. 2 hours. (b) 6 hours.

    Understand the situation

    Drying is something that happens to every shirt at the same time. A shirt does not dry faster or slower because other shirts are on the line, as long as there is room for it in the sun.

    Find the mathematical structure

    • When all items are processed simultaneously, the time does not depend on how many there are.
    • When there is limited room, items are processed in batches, and the time depends on the number of batches.

    Build the reasoning

    (a) All 6 shirts dry side by side, each taking 2 hours, so the whole batch takes 2 hours.

    (b) Only 3 shirts can hang at once, so the shirts dry in batches:

    7 ÷ 3 = 2 and 1 over 3, meaning: batches' worth of shirts

    A third of a batch still needs a full drying time, so there are 3 batches (3 shirts, then 3 shirts, then 1 shirt).

    3 × 2 = 6, meaning: hours

    Check the answer

    The tempting answers come from proportional thinking: "6 shirts take 4 hours" in (a) or "7 shirts take 4 and 2 over 3 hours" in (b). They would be right only if the shirts had to dry one after another at a rate of so many shirts per hour.

    General lesson

    Ask whether things happen at the same time or one after another. Simultaneous processes do not add up; limited room creates batches, and batches must be rounded up.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Model selection, Changed conditions.

    Level 4, question 6 of 20 (problem 51 of 140)

    Question 4.6Lilies on a pond

    Difficulty 2 of 4: Core

    A patch of water lilies on a pond doubles in area every day. On day 30 the lilies first cover the whole pond.

    1. On which day did they cover half the pond?
    2. On which day did they cover a quarter of the pond?
    3. Ali says, "They covered half the pond on day 15, because 15 is half of 30." Explain his mistake.
    4. What fraction of the pond did they cover on day 25?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Work backwards from day 30. If the area doubles each day, what was it the day before?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. Day 29. (b) Day 28. (c) Ali assumes the area grows by the same amount each day, but it grows by the same factor (×2). (d) 1 over 32 of the pond.

    Understand the situation

    "Doubles every day" means that each day's area is twice the previous day's area. Going backwards, each day's area is half the next day's.

    Find the mathematical structure

    This is not proportional growth. In proportional growth, the area would increase by the same amount each day, so half the time would give half the area. Here the area is multiplied by 2 each day, so most of the growth happens near the end.

    Build the reasoning

    Work backwards from the whole pond:

    DayFraction of pond covered
    301
    291 over 2
    281 over 4
    271 over 8
    261 over 16
    251 over 32

    Develop intuition

    On day 29, half the pond is still open water. Someone looking at the pond would think there is plenty of time left. Yet one day later the pond is full. Doubling looks slow at first and then becomes explosive.

    Check the answer

    On day 15 the lilies covered 1 over 2 multiplied by itself 15 times, which is 1 over 32768 of the pond, nowhere near half.

    General lesson

    Quantities that multiply by a fixed factor in each time step do not grow in proportion to time. Test any "half the time gives half the amount" claim by working step by step.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Error diagnosis, Counterexample.

    Level 4, question 7 of 20 (problem 52 of 140)

    Question 4.7Pizza sizes

    Difficulty 3 of 4: Challenge

    A pizzeria sells round pizzas, all of the same thickness, with the toppings spread just as thickly on each. A pizza 20 cm across costs ₹240, and a pizza 30 cm across costs ₹480.

    1. The bigger pizza is 1.5 times as wide but costs 2 times as much. Does that make it worse value? Explain carefully.
    2. Which pizza is better value?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    You eat the top surface of a pizza, not its width. What happens to the area of a shape when every length is multiplied by 1.5?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. No. Making a shape 1.5 times as wide (and 1.5 times as long) makes its area 1.5 × 1.5 = 2.25 times as large. The bigger pizza has 2.25 times as much pizza for 2 times the price.
    2. The 30 cm pizza is better value.

    Understand the situation

    The amount of pizza depends on its area, not on its width. Since the thickness and toppings are the same, the amount you get is in proportion to the area.

    Find the mathematical structure

    When a shape is enlarged so that every length is multiplied by a number k, its area is multiplied by k × k. This is true for any shape: squares, rectangles, circles, even a drawing of a cat.

    Build the reasoning

    Why the area grows by 2.25. Compare squares first, because their areas are easy:

    20 × 20 = 400, meaning: square centimetres, square 20 cm wide

    30 × 30 = 900, meaning: square centimetres, square 30 cm wide

    900 ÷ 400 = 2.25, meaning: times as much area

    A circle 30 cm across is an exact enlargement of a circle 20 cm across, with every length multiplied by 1.5, so its area is also 2.25 times as large.

    Compare value. At the small pizza's rate, 2.25 small pizzas' worth of area would cost:

    2.25 × 240 = 540, meaning: rupees at the small pizza's rate

    The big pizza gives that much pizza for ₹480, so it is ₹60 cheaper than buying the same amount of pizza at the small pizza's rate.

    Alternative reasoning

    If you know that a circle's area is π × radius × radius (with π about 3.14):

    • small pizza: radius 10 cm, area about 314 cm², so about ₹0.76 per cm²;
    • big pizza: radius 15 cm, area about 707 cm², so about ₹0.68 per cm².

    The big pizza costs less per square centimetre.

    Check the answer

    The comparison does not depend on π at all: for both pizzas the area is π times the radius times the radius, so π cancels when we compare. That is why the square comparison gives the right ratio.

    General lesson

    Area does not scale like length. Multiply every length by k and the area is multiplied by k × k. Before comparing value, decide which quantity you are actually paying for.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Comparison, Model selection.

    Level 4, question 8 of 20 (problem 53 of 140)

    Question 4.8Brother and sister

    Difficulty 2 of 4: Core

    Arjun's sister was born exactly 4 years before him. When Arjun was 4 years old, his sister was 8, twice his age.

    1. When Arjun is 20, will his sister be 40? Explain, and find how old she will be.
    2. How old will Arjun be when his sister's age is exactly 1.5 times his age?
    3. Will his sister ever again be exactly twice his age? Explain.

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    What stays the same about their ages as the years go by?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. No. Both of them get one year older every year, so the 4-year gap never changes. She will be 24. (b) Arjun will be 8 (and his sister 12). (c) No. She is twice his age only at the moment when Arjun is 4.

    Understand the situation

    Both children get older at exactly the same rate: one year per year. So what stays the same is the difference between their ages, not the ratio.

    Find the mathematical structure

    Sister's age = Arjun's age + 4, always. Ages differ by a constant amount, so they are not in direct proportion: if they were, doubling Arjun's age would double his sister's.

    Build the reasoning

    (a)

    20 + 4 = 24, meaning: sister's age when Arjun is 20

    (b) We need the sister's age (Arjun's age + 4) to equal 1.5 times Arjun's age. The extra 0.5 of Arjun's age must therefore be exactly the 4-year difference.

    4 ÷ 0.5 = 8, meaning: Arjun's age

    8 + 4 = 12, meaning: sister's age, and 12 = 1.5 × 8

    (c) For her to be twice his age, the extra amount, one whole Arjun's age, must equal the 4-year difference. That happens only when Arjun is 4 years old. Once he is older, his age is more than 4, so she can never be twice his age again.

    Develop intuition

    As both children grow older, the fixed 4-year gap becomes a smaller and smaller part of their ages. Their ages look more and more alike: at 40 and 44, the sister is only 1.1 times as old.

    Check the answer

    1. The ratio 24 : 20 is 1.2, not 2. As predicted, the ratio shrinks over time while the difference stays at 4.

    General lesson

    Before scaling, ask what really stays constant. For ages, it is the difference. Quantities that keep a fixed difference are not in proportion.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Counterexample, Reverse reasoning.

    Level 4, question 9 of 20 (problem 54 of 140)

    Question 4.9Stretching a spring

    Difficulty 3 of 4: Challenge

    A spring is 20 cm long when nothing hangs from it. When a 150 g mass hangs from it, it is 26 cm long. Within its safe range (which includes every mass in this question), the amount the spring stretches is directly proportional to the mass hung on it.

    1. How long is the spring when a 400 g mass hangs from it?
    2. A student says: "Length is proportional to mass, so with 400 g the length is 26 × 400 ÷ 150, which is about 69.3 cm." Explain the mistake.
    3. What mass makes the spring 30 cm long?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Separate the spring's length into its natural length and the extra length caused by the mass.

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. 36 cm. (b) The stretch is proportional to the mass, not the total length. The student also scaled up the spring's natural 20 cm, which does not depend on the mass at all. (c) 250 g.

    Find the mathematical structure

    Length = natural length + stretch = 20 cm + stretch. The stretch is proportional to the mass; the 20 cm stays the same. This is the same structure as a taxi fare with a fixed starting charge.

    Build the reasoning

    26 − 20 = 6, meaning: centimetres of stretch caused by 150 g

    6 ÷ 150 = 0.04, meaning: centimetres of stretch for each gram

    (a)

    400 × 0.04 = 16, meaning: centimetres of stretch for 400 g

    20 + 16 = 36, meaning: centimetres long

    (c) A 30 cm spring is stretched by 10 cm.

    30 − 20 = 10, meaning: centimetres of stretch needed

    10 ÷ 0.04 = 250, meaning: grams

    Check the answer

    • In (a), 400 g is 8 over 3 times 150 g, so the stretch should be 8 over 3 × 6 = 16 cm. It is.
    • The student's method predicts that with no mass the spring has length 0 cm, which is absurd: it is 20 cm long with nothing on it.

    General lesson

    When a quantity has a starting value, proportion usually applies only to the change from that start. Subtract the starting value, scale the change, then add the start back.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Error diagnosis, Transfer.

    Level 4, question 10 of 20 (problem 55 of 140)

    Question 4.10Facts you do not need

    Difficulty 2 of 4: Core

    A train is 240 m long and has 10 coaches carrying 720 passengers. The front of the train leaves Station A at 10:00 a.m. and travels at a steady 72 km/h to Station B, 18 km further along the track.

    1. At what time does the front of the train reach Station B?
    2. Which facts in the question did you not need for (a)?
    3. Which of those facts would you need to find how long the train takes to pass a signal post completely (from the moment its front reaches the post until its back passes it)? Find that time.

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    For (a), only the front of the train matters. Which facts describe the front's journey?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. 10:15 a.m. (b) The train's length, the number of coaches and the number of passengers. (c) The length: the whole train passes the post in 12 seconds.

    Understand the situation

    The question is about the front of the train moving 18 km at a steady speed. The length of the train, its coaches and its passengers do not change how long that takes.

    Build the reasoning

    (a)

    18 ÷ 72 = 1 over 4, meaning: hours for the front to travel 18 km

    A quarter of an hour is 15 minutes, so the front arrives at 10:15 a.m.

    (c) To pass the post completely, the train must move forward by its own length, 240 m. Convert the speed to metres per second:

    72,000 ÷ 3,600 = 20, meaning: metres per second

    240 ÷ 20 = 12, meaning: seconds to pass the post

    Why irrelevant facts matter

    Real problems come with many numbers. A good problem solver uses a number only when there is a reason. Here the length is irrelevant to (a) but essential to (c): whether a fact matters depends on the question being asked.

    Check the answer

    At 72 km/h the train covers 72 km in an hour, so 18 km takes a quarter of that time: 15 minutes.

    General lesson

    Do not use a number just because it is there. Ask what the question is really about, which quantities affect it, and which do not.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Missing quantity, Model selection.

    Level 4, question 11 of 20 (problem 56 of 140)

    Question 4.11Not enough to go on

    Difficulty 2 of 4: Core

    A car travels 60 km in the first hour of a journey and 40 km in the second hour.

    1. Can you work out how far it travels in 5 hours? Explain.
    2. Write two different extra facts, each of which would make the question answerable, and give the answer each fact leads to.

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Is the car's speed constant? What would you need to know about hours 3, 4 and 5?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. No. The car's speed changed between the first and second hours, so there is no single steady rate to scale. Hours 3, 4 and 5 could be fast, slow or not driven at all.
    2. For example:
    • "From the second hour on, it keeps a steady 40 km/h." Then the distance is 60 + 4 × 40 = 220 km.
    • "Its average speed for the whole 5 hours is 50 km/h." Then the distance is 5 × 50 = 250 km.

    Many other extra facts work.

    Understand the situation

    The unitary method needs a constant rate. Two different distances in two equal hours show the rate is not constant, and nothing tells us what happens next.

    Why "cannot be determined" is the correct answer

    Here are three possible journeys that all match the facts:

    Hours 3 to 5Distance in 5 hours
    steady 40 km/h60 + 40 + 3 × 40 = 220 km
    stops after hour 260 + 40 = 100 km
    steady 60 km/h60 + 40 + 3 × 60 = 280 km

    Different journeys fit the same information but give different answers. That is exactly what "cannot be determined" means.

    Build the reasoning

    For the two examples in (b):

    60 + 4 × 40 = 220, meaning: kilometres if it keeps 40 km/h after the first hour

    5 × 50 = 250, meaning: kilometres if the average speed is 50 km/h

    General lesson

    Before scaling, check that the rate is constant. If the information allows several different answers, the honest answer is "cannot be determined", together with an explanation of what is missing.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Insufficient information, Problem construction.

    Level 4, question 12 of 20 (problem 57 of 140)

    Question 4.12Packs of pens

    Difficulty 3 of 4: Challenge

    A shop sells pens singly for ₹10 each, in packs of 5 for ₹45, and in packs of 10 for ₹80. Packs cannot be split.

    1. What is the cheapest way to buy exactly 23 pens, and what does it cost?
    2. Show that buying 10 pens can cost less than buying exactly 9 pens.
    3. Is the cost of the cheapest way to buy n pens directly proportional to n? Explain using your answers.

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Packs of 10 give the lowest price per pen. Use as many as you can without going over, then look at the rest.

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. Two packs of 10 and 3 single pens, costing ₹190.
    2. Exactly 9 pens cost at least ₹85 (a pack of 5 and 4 singles), but a pack of 10 costs only ₹80.
    3. No. For example, 10 pens cost ₹80, but 23 pens cost ₹190, which is not 2.3 × ₹80 = ₹184. Even more strikingly, 10 pens cost less than 9 pens.

    Understand the situation

    The price per pen depends on how you buy: ₹10 singly, ₹9 in a pack of 5, ₹8 in a pack of 10. So the total cost is not simply "a fixed price per pen" times the number of pens.

    Build the reasoning

    (a) Per pen, the pack of 10 is cheapest (₹8), then the pack of 5 (₹9), then singles (₹10). Try the possibilities for 23 pens:

    Packs of 10Packs of 5SinglesCost
    203160 + 30 = ₹190
    12380 + 90 + 30 = ₹200
    11880 + 45 + 80 = ₹205
    043180 + 30 = ₹210
    0023₹230

    The other ways use even more single pens or smaller packs, so they cost more. The cheapest is ₹190.

    (b) Exactly 9 pens:

    45 + 4 × 10 = 85, meaning: rupees for a pack of 5 and 4 singles

    9 × 10 = 90, meaning: rupees for 9 singles

    A pack of 10 costs ₹80: you get more pens for less money.

    Check the answer

    For (a), could buying more than 23 pens be cheaper? No: every pen costs at least ₹8, however it is bought, so 24 or more pens cost at least 24 × 8 = ₹192, which is more than ₹190. So ₹190 is also the cheapest way to get at least 23 pens.

    General lesson

    Bulk prices break proportion. When prices depend on the pack, search the possibilities systematically, starting with the cheapest rate, and remember that buying a little more can sometimes cost less.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Model selection, Counterexample.

    Level 4, question 13 of 20 (problem 58 of 140)

    Question 4.13The striking clock

    Difficulty 2 of 4: Core

    A clock strikes 6 times at 6 o'clock. From the first strike to the last strike takes 30 seconds. The gaps between strikes are always equal, and the strikes themselves take no time. How long does it take, from the first strike to the last, to strike 12 times at 12 o'clock?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Six strikes: how many gaps are there between them?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    66 seconds.

    Find the mathematical structure

    The time is spent in the gaps between strikes. With n strikes there are n − 1 gaps. The time is proportional to the number of gaps, not to the number of strikes. This is the fence-post idea again, in time instead of space.

    Build the reasoning

    6 − 1 = 5, meaning: gaps between 6 strikes

    30 ÷ 5 = 6, meaning: seconds per gap

    12 − 1 = 11, meaning: gaps between 12 strikes

    11 × 6 = 66, meaning: seconds

    Check the answer

    The tempting answer is 60 seconds ("twice the strikes, twice the time"). But 12 strikes have 11 gaps, which is more than twice 5 gaps, so the time must be more than 60 seconds.

    General lesson

    Events at points in time behave like posts along a fence. Count the intervals between them, because the intervals are what take the time.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Transfer, Model selection.

    Level 4, question 14 of 20 (problem 59 of 140)

    Question 4.14Testing tables

    Difficulty 2 of 4: Core

    For each table, decide whether y is directly proportional to x, inversely proportional to x, or neither. Justify each decision with a test. If a table is neither, find a simple rule that fits every pair in it. Then use the relationship or rule to answer the extra question for that table.

    Table A: x = 2, 5, 8 and y = 7, 17.5, 28. Find y when x = 11.

    Table B: x = 3, 4, 6, 12 and y = 20, 15, 10, 5. Find y when x = 5.

    Table C: x = 1, 2, 3, 4 and y = 5, 8, 11, 14. Find y when x = 10.

    Table D: x = 1, 2, 4, 5 and y = 3, 12, 48, 75. Find y when x = 3.

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    For each table, work out y ÷ x and x × y for every pair. Which one, if either, never changes?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    • Table A: direct. y ÷ x = 3.5 every time. When x = 11, y = 38.5.
    • Table B: inverse. x × y = 60 every time. When x = 5, y = 12.
    • Table C: neither. y goes up by 3 each time x goes up by 1, but y ÷ x changes. The rule y = 3 × x + 2 fits every pair, so when x = 10, y = 32.
    • Table D: neither. y ÷ x is 3, 6, 12, 15. The rule y = 3 × x × x fits every pair, so when x = 3, y = 27.

    Find the mathematical structure

    • Direct proportion: y ÷ x is the same for every pair.
    • Inverse proportion: x × y is the same for every pair.
    • If neither stays the same, look for another pattern.

    Build the reasoning

    Table A: 7 ÷ 2 = 3.5, 17.5 ÷ 5 = 3.5, 28 ÷ 8 = 3.5.

    3.5 × 11 = 38.5, meaning: y when x = 11

    Table B: 3 × 20 = 60, 4 × 15 = 60, 6 × 10 = 60, 12 × 5 = 60.

    60 ÷ 5 = 12, meaning: y when x = 5

    Table C: y ÷ x is 5, 4, 11 over 3, 3.5, which changes. x × y is 5, 16, 33, 56, which also changes. But y increases by 3 for each step of 1 in x, starting from 5 when x = 1. So y = 3 × x + 2.

    3 × 10 + 2 = 32, meaning: y when x = 10

    Table D: y ÷ x is 3, 6, 12, 15, which changes, and x × y is 3, 24, 192, 375, which also changes. When x doubles (1 to 2, or 2 to 4), y is multiplied by 4. Each y is 3 times x × x: 3 × 1 × 1 = 3, 3 × 2 × 2 = 12, 3 × 4 × 4 = 48, 3 × 5 × 5 = 75.

    3 × 3 × 3 = 27, meaning: y when x = 3
