    A note on rules

    No table of a few pairs can prove what happens between or beyond the given values, so for all four tables the extra answers assume that the pattern continues. For Tables A and B the pattern is a constant ratio or a constant product, which is exactly what direct or inverse proportion means. For Tables C and D it is a rule we spotted that fits every pair. In a real problem, the story behind the numbers (a fixed price, a fixed distance) is what tells you whether a pattern can be trusted beyond the table.

    A warning about Table C

    Table C increases steadily, which tempts people to call it direct proportion. But if it were, then y would be 0 when x is 0. Following the pattern backwards, y would be 2 when x is 0. A steady increase with a starting value is not proportion: it is the taxi-fare pattern again.

    General lesson

    Test every pair, not just one. Direct proportion keeps the ratio fixed; inverse proportion keeps the product fixed. A steady increase is not enough for direct proportion.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Model selection, Direct application.

    Level 4, question 15 of 20 (problem 60 of 140)

    Question 4.15Riya's three claims

    Difficulty 3 of 4: Challenge

    Riya makes three claims about two quantities x and y that are never negative.

    Claim 1: "If y increases whenever x increases, then y is directly proportional to x."

    Claim 2: "If y increases whenever x increases, and y = 0 when x = 0, then y is directly proportional to x."

    Claim 3: "If y ÷ x gives the same number for every pair of values, then y is directly proportional to x."

    For each claim, decide whether it is always true. Give a counterexample with numbers for any claim that is false, and explain why any true claim is true.

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Think about a taxi fare, and about the area of a square.

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    • Claim 1 is false. Counterexample: a taxi fare of ₹50 plus ₹20 per km. 1 km costs ₹70 and 2 km cost ₹90: the fare increases, but doubling the distance does not double the fare.
    • Claim 2 is false. Counterexample: the side of a square and its area. A side of 0 gives an area of 0, and the area increases with the side, but a side of 1 cm gives 1 cm² while a side of 2 cm gives 4 cm².
    • Claim 3 is true. If y ÷ x is always the same number k, then y = k × x for every pair, which is exactly what direct proportion means.

    Why counterexamples settle it

    A claim that says "always" is destroyed by a single example where it fails. For Claim 1:

    50 + 20 × 1 = 70, meaning: rupees for 1 km

    50 + 20 × 2 = 90, meaning: rupees for 2 km, not 2 × 70 = 140

    For Claim 2:

    1 × 1 = 1, meaning: square centimetres for a side of 1 cm

    2 × 2 = 4, meaning: square centimetres for a side of 2 cm, not 2 × 1 = 2

    Why Claim 3 is true

    Suppose y ÷ x = k for every pair. Then y = k × x. If x is multiplied by any number, say 3, then y = k × (3 × x) = 3 × (k × x), so y is multiplied by 3 as well. That is the defining property of direct proportion. A constant ratio is not just evidence of direct proportion: it is direct proportion.

    General lesson

    "Increasing together" and even "starting together at zero" do not guarantee proportion. The test that works is the constant ratio, checked for every pair.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Counterexample, Proof and explanation.

    Level 4, question 16 of 20 (problem 61 of 140)

    Question 4.16Enlarging photos and boxes

    Difficulty 2 of 4: Core

    1. A photo 10 cm by 15 cm is enlarged so that each side is 3 times as long. How many times as much area does the enlarged photo have?
    2. A thin frame goes around the edge of each photo. How many times as long is the frame of the enlarged photo?
    3. A cardboard box is a cube with edges 10 cm long. A second cube has edges 20 cm long. How many times as much does the second box hold? How many times as much cardboard is needed to make it (ignoring overlaps)?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Work out the actual areas, lengths and volumes, then compare.

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. 9 times the area. (b) 3 times as long. (c) It holds 8 times as much, and needs 4 times as much cardboard.

    Find the mathematical structure

    When every length is multiplied by k:

    • lengths (such as the frame) are multiplied by k;
    • areas (such as the photo or the cardboard) are multiplied by k × k;
    • volumes (how much a box holds) are multiplied by k × k × k.

    Build the reasoning

    (a) The enlarged photo is 30 cm by 45 cm.

    10 × 15 = 150, meaning: square centimetres, original photo

    30 × 45 = 1,350, meaning: square centimetres, enlarged photo

    1,350 ÷ 150 = 9, meaning: times the area

    (b)

    2 × (10 + 15) = 50, meaning: centimetres of frame, original

    2 × (30 + 45) = 150, meaning: centimetres of frame, enlarged

    150 ÷ 50 = 3, meaning: times as long

    (c) Volume:

    10 × 10 × 10 = 1,000, meaning: cubic centimetres in the small box

    20 × 20 × 20 = 8,000, meaning: cubic centimetres in the large box

    8,000 ÷ 1,000 = 8, meaning: times as much

    Cardboard (a cube has 6 square faces):

    6 × 10 × 10 = 600, meaning: square centimetres for the small box

    6 × 20 × 20 = 2,400, meaning: square centimetres for the large box

    2,400 ÷ 600 = 4, meaning: times as much cardboard

    Develop intuition

    Nine copies of the original photo fit exactly inside the enlarged one, in 3 rows of 3. Eight small boxes fit exactly inside the large box, in 2 layers of 2 rows of 2.

    Check the answer

    In (c), the edge doubled (k = 2): the cardboard went up by 2 × 2 = 4 and the space inside by 2 × 2 × 2 = 8, matching the rule.

    General lesson

    "Twice as big" is ambiguous. Lengths, areas and volumes scale differently: by k, k × k and k × k × k. Decide which one the question is really about.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Transfer, Counterexample.

    Level 4, question 17 of 20 (problem 62 of 140)

    Question 4.17Celsius and Fahrenheit

    Difficulty 3 of 4: Challenge

    Water freezes at 0 °C, which is 32 °F, and boils at 100 °C, which is 212 °F. Between these, the Fahrenheit temperature rises by the same amount for every degree Celsius.

    1. Is the Fahrenheit temperature directly proportional to the Celsius temperature? Explain.
    2. Convert 25 °C to °F.
    3. A student converts 25 °C by saying, "100 °C is 212 °F, so 25 °C is 212 ÷ 4 = 53 °F." Find the mistake.

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    How many Fahrenheit degrees fit between freezing and boiling? How many Celsius degrees?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. No. 0 °C is 32 °F, not 0 °F. Proportional quantities are both zero together.
    2. 77 °F.
    3. The student scaled the whole 212, including the 32 that is already there at 0 °C. Only the rise above freezing should be scaled.

    Find the mathematical structure

    °F = 32 + (rise in °F above freezing). The rise above freezing is proportional to the Celsius temperature: from 0 °C to 100 °C the Fahrenheit reading rises by 212 − 32 = 180 degrees. Like the spring and the taxi, this is a "start value plus a proportional part" relationship.

    Build the reasoning

    212 − 32 = 180, meaning: Fahrenheit degrees between freezing and boiling

    180 ÷ 100 = 1.8, meaning: Fahrenheit degrees for each Celsius degree

    (b)

    25 × 1.8 = 45, meaning: Fahrenheit degrees above freezing

    32 + 45 = 77, meaning: degrees Fahrenheit

    (c) The student's method gives 53 °F, which is 24 degrees too low. Their method would also say that 0 °C is 0 °F, which contradicts the given fact that water freezes at 32 °F.

    Check the answer

    25 °C is a quarter of the way from freezing to boiling. A quarter of the way from 32 °F to 212 °F is 32 + 1 over 4 × 180 = 32 + 45 = 77 °F.

    General lesson

    If one quantity is not 0 when the other is 0, the two cannot be in direct proportion. Scale the distance from the starting point, then add the start back.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Model selection, Error diagnosis.

    Level 4, question 18 of 20 (problem 63 of 140)

    Question 4.18Numbering the pages

    Difficulty 3 of 4: Challenge

    To print page numbers on pages 1 to 9 of a book needs 9 digits in total. To number pages 1 to 99 needs 189 digits.

    1. Is the number of digits proportional to the number of pages? Explain.
    2. How many digits are needed to number pages 1 to 150?
    3. A book's page numbers use 600 digits altogether, starting from page 1. How many pages does it have?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Count separately the pages with one digit, two digits and three digits.

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. No. 9 pages need 9 digits (1 per page), but 99 pages need 189 digits (about 1.9 per page). Later pages use more digits.
    2. 342 digits. (c) 236 pages.

    Find the mathematical structure

    The "cost" in digits per page changes in stages:

    • pages 1 to 9: 1 digit each;
    • pages 10 to 99: 2 digits each;
    • pages 100 to 999: 3 digits each.

    Within one stage, the digits used by that stage's pages are proportional to how many of its pages there are. But the running total of digits from page 1 is not proportional to the number of pages.

    Build the reasoning

    Check the given total for 1 to 99. There are 90 two-digit pages (10 to 99).

    9 × 1 + 90 × 2 = 189, meaning: digits for pages 1 to 99, as stated

    (b) Pages 100 to 150 are 51 pages (150 − 100 + 1), with 3 digits each.

    189 + 51 × 3 = 342, meaning: digits for pages 1 to 150

    (c) Work backwards. The first 99 pages use 189 digits.

    600 − 189 = 411, meaning: digits left for three-digit pages

    411 ÷ 3 = 137, meaning: three-digit pages

    99 + 137 = 236, meaning: pages in the book

    Check the answer

    • (b) Counting "51 pages" from 100 to 150: there are 150 − 100 = 50 steps from page 100 to page 150, and 51 pages including both ends (the fence-post idea again).
    • (c) Pages 100 to 236 are 236 − 100 + 1 = 137 pages, which use 411 digits. Total 189 + 411 = 600.

    General lesson

    When the rate changes at known points, split the problem into stages, use proportion within each stage, and add. Working backwards through the stages answers the reverse question.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Model selection, Reverse reasoning.

    Level 4, question 19 of 20 (problem 64 of 140)

    Question 4.19Shaking hands

    Difficulty 3 of 4: Challenge

    At a meeting, every person shakes hands exactly once with every other person. With 5 people there are 10 handshakes.

    1. Neel says that with 10 people there will be 20 handshakes. Is he right? Explain.
    2. Find the correct number of handshakes for 10 people.
    3. At another meeting there were 66 handshakes. How many people were there?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Count from one person's point of view: how many hands does each person shake? Then be careful not to count any handshake twice.

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. No. Each new person shakes hands with everyone already there, so the handshakes grow much faster than the number of people. (b) 45 handshakes. (c) 12 people.

    Understand the situation

    Adding people adds more than a fixed number of handshakes, because each new person shakes hands with everyone already there. The more people there are, the more handshakes each newcomer brings.

    Find the mathematical structure

    With n people, each person shakes n − 1 hands. That counts every handshake twice (once for each of the two people), so the number of handshakes is n × (n − 1) ÷ 2. This grows much faster than n.

    Build the reasoning

    Check with 5 people:

    5 × 4 ÷ 2 = 10, meaning: handshakes, as given

    (b)

    10 × 9 ÷ 2 = 45, meaning: handshakes for 10 people

    (c) We need n × (n − 1) = 2 × 66 = 132: two consecutive whole numbers that multiply to 132. Try numbers near 11, because 11 × 11 = 121:

    12 × 11 = 132, meaning: so n = 12

    Alternative reasoning

    Add people one at a time. The 2nd person shakes 1 hand, the 3rd shakes 2, the 4th shakes 3, and so on. For 10 people:

    1 + 2 + 3 + 4 + 5 + 6 + 7 + 8 + 9 = 45, meaning: handshakes

    For (c), keep adding until you reach 66: 45 + 10 + 11 = 66, so the 11th and 12th people bring the total to 66.

    Check the answer

    Neel's 20 would mean each extra person adds just 2 handshakes. But the 10th person alone shakes hands with 9 others.

    General lesson

    Some quantities grow faster than proportionally, because each new unit interacts with all the existing ones. Test proportional claims with a small case you can count.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Counterexample, Reverse reasoning.

    Level 4, question 20 of 20 (problem 65 of 140)

    Question 4.20Tiling a floor

    Difficulty 3 of 4: Challenge

    A rectangular floor 3.2 m by 4 m is covered exactly by 320 square tiles, each with sides of 20 cm, without cutting any tile.

    1. How many square tiles with sides of 40 cm would cover the same floor?
    2. Explain why the answer to (a) is not 160.
    3. Explain why square tiles with sides of 25 cm cannot cover this floor exactly without cutting some of them.

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Doubling the side of a tile does what to its area? And for (c), how many 25 cm tiles fit along a 3.2 m edge?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. 80 tiles. (b) A tile with double the side has 4 times the area, so only a quarter as many tiles are needed, not half as many. (c) One 25 cm tile covers 625 cm², and 128,000 ÷ 625 = 204.8 is not a whole number, so no arrangement of whole tiles can cover the floor exactly. (Also, 320 ÷ 25 = 12.8, so whole tiles cannot fit exactly along the 3.2 m edge.)

    Find the mathematical structure

    The fixed total is the floor's area. Number of tiles × area of one tile = floor area, so the number of tiles is inversely proportional to the area of a tile, not to its side length. Doubling the side multiplies a tile's area by 2 × 2 = 4.

    Build the reasoning

    (a) By area.

    320 × 400 = 128,000, meaning: square centimetres of floor

    40 × 40 = 1,600, meaning: square centimetres in one large tile

    128,000 ÷ 1,600 = 80, meaning: large tiles

    By rows. Along the 320 cm edge, 320 ÷ 40 = 8 tiles fit; along the 400 cm edge, 400 ÷ 40 = 10 tiles fit.

    8 × 10 = 80, meaning: large tiles, the same answer

    (b) Each large tile covers the same area as 4 small tiles (2 rows of 2). So the number of tiles is divided by 4:

    320 ÷ 4 = 80, meaning: large tiles

    (c)

    320 ÷ 25 = 12.8, meaning: tiles of 25 cm along the 320 cm edge

    128,000 ÷ 625 = 204.8, meaning: tiles' worth of area

    Not even the area gives a whole number of tiles. Whole tiles cannot cover the floor exactly.

    Check the answer

    For the original tiles: 320 ÷ 20 = 16 and 400 ÷ 20 = 20, and 16 × 20 = 320 tiles, as stated.

    General lesson

    Inverse proportion depends on the right quantity. The number of tiles is inversely proportional to the tile's area, not its side. And real fitting needs more than area: the tiles must also fit along each edge.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Model selection, Transfer.

    Level 5, question 1 of 20 (problem 66 of 140)

    Question 5.1Painting the fence together

    Difficulty 2 of 4: Core

    Anil can paint a fence on his own in 6 hours. Bina can paint the same fence on her own in 3 hours. They paint it together, each working at their usual steady rate, without getting in each other's way.

    1. Anil says, "Together we will take 6 + 3 = 9 hours." Bina says, "We will take the average, 4.5 hours." Explain why each of them must be wrong, without finding the exact answer.
    2. How long do they actually take?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    What fraction of the fence does each person paint in one hour?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. Working together must be faster than Bina alone, so the time must be less than 3 hours. Both 9 hours and 4.5 hours are longer than 3 hours.
    2. 2 hours.

    Understand the situation

    Both people work on the same fence at the same time. Every hour, each of them paints some part of it, and those parts add together.

    Find the mathematical structure

    • A time ("6 hours for a fence") is hard to combine directly. A rate ("1 over 6 of a fence per hour") is easy to combine.
    • When two people work at the same time, the amounts they paint in each hour add up. So their rates add.
    • Time for the job = 1 job ÷ (combined rate).

    Build the reasoning

    Each person's rate.

    1 ÷ 6 = 1 over 6, meaning: of the fence painted by Anil each hour

    1 ÷ 3 = 1 over 3, meaning: of the fence painted by Bina each hour

    Combined rate. In one hour together they paint:

    1 over 6 + 1 over 3 = 1 over 2, meaning: of the fence each hour

    Time. Half the fence each hour means the whole fence takes:

    1 ÷ 1 over 2 = 2, meaning: hours

    Develop intuition

    Imagine they keep painting identical fences for 6 hours. Anil finishes 1 fence and Bina finishes 2 fences, so together they paint 3 fences in 6 hours. That is 1 fence every 2 hours.

    Check the answer

    • In 2 hours Anil paints 2 over 6 = 1 over 3 of the fence and Bina paints 2 over 3 of it. Together: 1 over 3 + 2 over 3 = 1 whole fence.
    • Why adding times fails: 9 hours would be the time if Anil painted a whole fence and then Bina painted another whole fence, one after the other. Times add only for jobs done in sequence.
    • Why averaging fails: 4.5 hours would mean Bina is slowed down by Anil's help. Help can never make a job take longer than the faster worker alone.

    General lesson

    When agents work at the same time, add their rates, never their times. A quick sense check: the combined time must be less than the fastest agent's time alone.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Direct application, Error diagnosis.

    Level 5, question 2 of 20 (problem 67 of 140)

    Question 5.2Two taps, with bounds first

    Difficulty 2 of 4: Core

    Tap A on its own fills a tank in 4 hours. Tap B on its own fills the same tank in 6 hours. Both taps flow at steady rates. The tank starts empty, and the taps are opened at the same moment.

    1. Before calculating, explain why both taps together must take more than 2 hours but less than 3 hours.
    2. Find the exact time for both taps together, in hours and minutes.

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    For (a), imagine two taps exactly like A. Then imagine two taps exactly like B.

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. Two taps like A (the faster tap) would take 4 ÷ 2 = 2 hours. Tap B is slower than A, so the real pair takes more than 2 hours. Two taps like B would take 6 ÷ 2 = 3 hours. Tap A is faster than B, so the real pair takes less than 3 hours.
    2. 2 hours 24 minutes (2 and 2 over 5 hours).

    Find the mathematical structure

    Rates add when the taps run at the same time. Tap A fills 1 over 4 of the tank per hour and tap B fills 1 over 6 per hour.

    Build the reasoning

    1 over 4 + 1 over 6 = 5 over 12, meaning: of the tank filled each hour by both taps

    1 ÷ 5 over 12 = 12 over 5, meaning: hours to fill the whole tank

    12 over 5 = 2 and 2 over 5, meaning: hours

    2 over 5 × 60 = 24, meaning: minutes

    So the taps take 2 hours 24 minutes.

    Alternative reasoning

    Use a convenient tank size. Pretend the tank holds 12 buckets (12 is the smallest number that both 4 and 6 divide into). Tap A pours 12 ÷ 4 = 3 buckets per hour, and tap B pours 12 ÷ 6 = 2 buckets per hour. Together they pour 5 buckets per hour, so 12 buckets take 12 over 5 hours. The size we pretend does not matter: any tank size gives the same time, so we choose a size that avoids fractions.

    Check the answer

    2 and 2 over 5 hours lies between 2 and 3 hours, as predicted in (a). In that time, A fills 12 over 5 ÷ 4 = 3 over 5 of the tank and B fills 12 over 5 ÷ 6 = 2 over 5. Together, 1 whole tank.

    General lesson

    Before calculating a combined time, trap it between two easy cases: the fastest agent doubled, and the slowest agent doubled. The exact answer must lie between them.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Estimation and bounds, Direct application.

    Level 5, question 3 of 20 (problem 68 of 140)

    Question 5.3An unnoticed leak

    Difficulty 2 of 4: Core

    With no leak, a pipe would fill an empty tank in 5 hours. The tank has a leak which, on its own, would empty a full tank in 20 hours. The pipe is opened when the tank is empty, and the leak goes unnoticed. How long does it take to fill the tank? Assume the pipe and the leak both work at steady rates.

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    In one hour, how much does the pipe add and how much does the leak take away?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    6 and 2 over 3 hours, which is 6 hours 40 minutes.

    Find the mathematical structure

    The pipe adds water while the leak removes it, at the same time. A leak is a negative rate: its contribution is subtracted.

    Build the reasoning

    1 ÷ 5 = 1 over 5, meaning: of the tank added by the pipe each hour

    1 ÷ 20 = 1 over 20, meaning: of the tank lost through the leak each hour

    1 over 5 − 1 over 20 = 3 over 20, meaning: of the tank gained each hour overall

    1 ÷ 3 over 20 = 20 over 3, meaning: hours to fill the tank

    20 over 3 = 6 and 2 over 3, meaning: hours

    2 over 3 of an hour is 40 minutes, so the tank fills in 6 hours 40 minutes.

    Alternative reasoning

    Pretend the tank holds 20 buckets. The pipe adds 4 buckets per hour and the leak loses 1 bucket per hour, a gain of 3 buckets per hour. 20 buckets take 20 over 3 hours.

    Check the answer

    The leak slows the filling down, so the answer must be more than 5 hours. It is. And the leak is much slower than the pipe, so the delay should be modest: an extra 1 hour 40 minutes is reasonable.

    About the assumption

    Real leaks usually flow faster when the tank is fuller, because the water pushes harder at the bottom. The steady-rate model is a simplification that makes the problem solvable, and it matters less when the leak is much slower than the pipe.

    General lesson

    Treat emptying as a negative rate. The net rate is the sum of all the rates with their signs, and the time is the job divided by the net rate.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Direct application, Assumption check.

    Level 5, question 4 of 20 (problem 69 of 140)

    Question 5.4Will the tank ever fill?

    Difficulty 2 of 4: Core

    A tank holds 600 litres and already contains 200 litres. An inlet pipe that adds water at 30 litres per minute is opened, and at the same moment a drain is opened. Describe what happens to the water level in each case, and find the time asked for if there is one.

    1. The drain removes 20 litres per minute. When is the tank full?
    2. The drain removes 30 litres per minute.
    3. The drain removes 40 litres per minute. When is the tank empty?
    4. In case (c), how fast would the inlet have to flow for the tank to be full after exactly 50 minutes?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Find the net change in the amount of water each minute. Is it positive, zero or negative?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. The level rises by 10 litres each minute; the tank is full after 40 minutes.
    2. The level never changes: it stays at 200 litres for ever, and the tank never fills.
    3. The level falls by 10 litres each minute; the tank is empty after 20 minutes and never fills.
    4. 48 litres per minute.

    Find the mathematical structure

    Net rate = inlet rate − drain rate.

    • If the net rate is positive, the tank fills.
    • If it is zero, the level stays where it is.
    • If it is negative, the level falls until the tank is empty: it never fills, however long you wait.

    Build the reasoning

    The tank needs 600 − 200 = 400 more litres to be full.

    (a)

    30 − 20 = 10, meaning: litres gained each minute

    400 ÷ 10 = 40, meaning: minutes until full

    (b)

    30 − 30 = 0, meaning: litres gained each minute

    Water arrives exactly as fast as it leaves. Dividing 400 by 0 has no answer, which matches the situation: no time is long enough.

    (c)

    30 − 40 = −10, meaning: litres per minute, so the tank loses 10 litres each minute

    200 ÷ 10 = 20, meaning: minutes until empty

    (d) To gain 400 litres in 50 minutes the net rate must be:

    400 ÷ 50 = 8, meaning: litres gained each minute

    The drain removes 40 litres per minute, so the inlet must supply 40 + 8 = 48 litres per minute.

    Check the answer

    1. After 40 minutes, the inlet has added 1,200 litres and the drain has removed 800 litres: 200 + 1,200 − 800 = 600 litres. (d) In 50 minutes, 48 × 50 = 2,400 litres in and 40 × 50 = 2,000 litres out: 200 + 400 = 600 litres.

    General lesson

    Always look at the sign of the net rate before calculating a time. A zero or negative net rate means "never", and a formula that divides by the net rate gives nonsense or no answer in those cases.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Changed conditions, Model selection.

    Level 5, question 5 of 20 (problem 70 of 140)

    Question 5.5Two trains approach

    Difficulty 2 of 4: Core

    Towns A and B are 300 km apart. At 9:00 a.m. a train leaves A towards B at a steady 70 km/h. At the same moment a train leaves B towards A at a steady 80 km/h on a parallel track.

    1. At what time do the trains meet?
    2. How far from A do they meet?
    3. How far apart are the trains at 12 noon (if both keep going)?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Each hour, how much does the gap between the trains shrink?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. 11:00 a.m. (b) 140 km from A. (c) 150 km apart (they have passed each other).

    Find the mathematical structure

    When two objects move towards each other, the gap between them shrinks by the sum of their speeds every hour. This sum is called the closing speed or relative speed. It is the "rates add" idea again: both trains eat into the same gap at the same time.

    Build the reasoning

    70 + 80 = 150, meaning: kilometres by which the gap shrinks each hour

    300 ÷ 150 = 2, meaning: hours until the gap is zero

    So they meet 2 hours after 9:00 a.m., at 11:00 a.m.

    70 × 2 = 140, meaning: kilometres travelled by the train from A

    (c) From 9:00 a.m. to 12 noon is 3 hours.

    150 × 3 = 450, meaning: kilometres travelled by the two trains together

    They only needed 300 km to meet, so after meeting they moved apart by 450 − 300 = 150 km.

    Check the answer

    The train from B travels 80 × 2 = 160 km in 2 hours, and 140 + 160 = 300 km. At 12 noon the train from A is 210 km from A and the train from B is 300 − 240 = 60 km from A, so they are 210 − 60 = 150 km apart.

    General lesson

    For objects approaching each other, add the speeds to get the rate at which the gap closes. Then the meeting time is the gap divided by that closing speed.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Direct application, Transfer.

    Level 5, question 6 of 20 (problem 71 of 140)

    Question 5.6Catching the cyclist

    Difficulty 3 of 4: Challenge

    A cyclist leaves a village at 8:00 a.m., riding at a steady 15 km/h. At 8:40 a.m. a car leaves the same village along the same road, travelling at a steady 55 km/h in the same direction.

    1. At what time does the car catch up with the cyclist?
    2. How far from the village does this happen?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    How far ahead is the cyclist when the car starts? How quickly does the car close that gap?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. 8:55 a.m. (b) 13.75 km from the village.

    Find the mathematical structure

    When two objects move in the same direction, the faster one closes the gap at the difference of their speeds. First find the head start, then divide it by that difference.

    Build the reasoning

    The head start. By 8:40 the cyclist has ridden for 40 minutes, which is 2 over 3 of an hour.

    15 × 2 over 3 = 10, meaning: kilometres ahead when the car starts

    Closing speed.

    55 − 15 = 40, meaning: kilometres by which the gap shrinks each hour

    10 ÷ 40 = 1 over 4, meaning: hours to close the gap

    A quarter of an hour is 15 minutes, so the car catches up at 8:55 a.m.

    Distance from the village. The car has driven for 1 over 4 hour:

    55 × 1 over 4 = 13.75, meaning: kilometres from the village

    Alternative reasoning

    Use a table of positions.

    TimeCyclist's distanceCar's distance
    8:4010 km0 km
    8:4511.25 km4 and 7 over 12 km
    8:5012.5 km9 and 1 over 6 km
    8:5513.75 km13.75 km

    Every 5 minutes the cyclist travels 1.25 km and the car travels 4 and 7 over 12 km, so the gap closes by 3 and 1 over 3 km every 5 minutes. A 10 km gap closes in 3 lots of 5 minutes.

    Check the answer

    The cyclist rides from 8:00 to 8:55, which is 55 minutes or 11 over 12 of an hour: 15 × 11 over 12 = 13.75 km. Both are at the same place.

    General lesson

    In a chase, the gap shrinks at the difference of the speeds. Find the gap at the moment the chase starts, then divide by the closing speed.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Direct application, Alternative solution.

    Level 5, question 7 of 20 (problem 72 of 140)

    Question 5.7Up the hill and back

    Difficulty 3 of 4: Challenge

    Priya cycles up a hill road at a steady 20 km/h and comes straight back down the same road at a steady 30 km/h.

    1. Her friend says Priya's average speed for the whole trip is 25 km/h. Is that right?
    2. Find her true average speed.
    3. Explain in words why it is less than 25 km/h.

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    The length of the road is not given. Choose a convenient length that both 20 and 30 divide into.

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. No. (b) 24 km/h. (c) She spends more time going up slowly than coming down quickly, so the slow speed counts for more of the trip's time.

    Understand the situation

    Average speed means total distance ÷ total time: the steady speed that would complete the whole trip in the same total time. It is not the average of the two speeds.

    Find the mathematical structure

    The road length is the same both ways, but the times are different. Since the length is not given, the answer must be the same whatever the length is. So we may choose a convenient length.

    Build the reasoning

    Choose the road to be 60 km long (60 is divisible by both 20 and 30).

    60 ÷ 20 = 3, meaning: hours going up

    60 ÷ 30 = 2, meaning: hours coming down

    60 + 60 = 120, meaning: kilometres in total

    3 + 2 = 5, meaning: hours in total

    120 ÷ 5 = 24, meaning: kilometres per hour on average

    Alternative reasoning

    Check that the length does not matter. Try a 6 km road: 0.3 hours up and 0.2 hours down, so 12 km in 0.5 hours, which is again 24 km/h. In general each kilometre of road takes 1 over 20 hour up and 1 over 30 hour down. So 2 km of riding (1 km up and 1 km down) takes:

    1 over 20 + 1 over 30 = 1 over 12, meaning: hours for 1 km up and 1 km down

    2 ÷ 1 over 12 = 24, meaning: kilometres per hour

    Develop intuition

    On the 60 km road, Priya rides for 3 hours at 20 km/h but only 2 hours at 30 km/h. If you average the speed over each hour of the trip, three hours "vote" for 20 and only two hours "vote" for 30: (3 × 20 + 2 × 30) ÷ 5 = 120 ÷ 5 = 24.

    Check the answer

    The average must lie between 20 and 30, and closer to 20, because more time is spent at 20 km/h. 24 is closer to 20 than 25 is.

    General lesson

    Average speed is total distance ÷ total time. Averaging the two speeds works only when equal times are spent at each speed, not equal distances.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Error diagnosis, Alternative solution.

    Level 5, question 8 of 20 (problem 73 of 140)

    Question 5.8An average that cannot be reached

    Difficulty 4 of 4: Olympiad

    A driver must cover a 2 km stretch of road. She drives the first kilometre at a steady 30 km/h. How fast must she drive the second kilometre so that her average speed for the whole 2 km is 60 km/h?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    How much time is allowed for the whole 2 km at an average of 60 km/h? How much has she already used?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    It is impossible. To average 60 km/h over 2 km she must finish in 2 minutes, but the first kilometre alone has already taken 2 minutes. However fast she drives the second kilometre, she cannot do it in no time at all.

    Understand the situation

    "Average 60 km/h over 2 km" is a statement about the total time: it fixes how long the whole trip may take. The first kilometre has used up part of that time.

    Build the reasoning

    Time allowed for the whole trip. At 60 km/h she covers 1 km every minute.

    2 ÷ 60 × 60 = 2, meaning: minutes allowed for 2 km

    Time already used. At 30 km/h she covers 1 km in 2 minutes.

    1 ÷ 30 × 60 = 2, meaning: minutes used for the first kilometre

    2 − 2 = 0, meaning: minutes left for the second kilometre

    No speed covers a kilometre in 0 minutes.

    How close can she get?

    Speed on 2nd kmTime on 2nd kmTotal timeAverage speed
    60 km/h1 min3 min40 km/h
    120 km/h0.5 min2.5 min48 km/h
    600 km/h0.1 min2.1 minabout 57.1 km/h

    The average creeps towards 60 km/h as the second speed grows, but it never reaches it.

    Develop intuition

    The time allowed for the whole 2 km is 2 minutes, and the first kilometre has already used all of it. Driving the second kilometre faster only shrinks its time towards 0 minutes, never all the way to 0, so the total time is always more than 2 minutes.

    Check the answer

    The tempting answer is 90 km/h, because (30 + 90) ÷ 2 = 60. But at 90 km/h the second kilometre takes 2 over 3 of a minute, so the total time is 2 and 2 over 3 minutes and the average speed is 2 ÷ 2 and 2 over 3 × 60 = 45 km/h.

    General lesson

    An average speed fixes the total time. When the time is already used up, no later speed can fix it. Always translate an average-speed target into a time budget.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Counterexample, Proof and explanation.

    Level 5, question 9 of 20 (problem 74 of 140)

    Question 5.9Twice as fast

    Difficulty 3 of 4: Challenge

    Asha works twice as fast as Bilal, and each of them works at a steady rate. Working together, they finish a job in 12 days. How long would each of them take to do the job alone?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Think of Asha as being worth two Bilals.

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    Asha: 18 days. Bilal: 36 days.

    Find the mathematical structure

    Asha does twice as much as Bilal in any day. So the pair works like three Bilals. Using the job's size in "Bilal-days" turns the problem into ordinary inverse proportion.

    Build the reasoning

    Asha and Bilal together are equivalent to 3 Bilals, and 3 Bilals take 12 days.

    3 × 12 = 36, meaning: Bilal-days in the job

    So Bilal alone takes 36 days. Asha works twice as fast, so she takes half as long:

    36 ÷ 2 = 18, meaning: days for Asha alone

    Alternative reasoning

    With rates. Suppose Bilal does a fraction r of the job each day; then Asha does 2 × r. Together they do 3 × r each day, which must be 1 over 12 of the job.

    1 over 12 ÷ 3 = 1 over 36, meaning: of the job done by Bilal each day

    2 × 1 over 36 = 1 over 18, meaning: of the job done by Asha each day

    Check the answer

    1 over 18 + 1 over 36 = 2 over 36 + 1 over 36 = 3 over 36 = 1 over 12, so together they do 1 over 12 of the job each day and finish in 12 days.

    General lesson

    When workers differ in speed, measure everyone in units of the slowest (or any one) worker. The problem then becomes an ordinary inverse-proportion problem.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Reverse reasoning, Alternative solution.

    Level 5, question 10 of 20 (problem 75 of 140)

    Question 5.10One worker leaves

    Difficulty 3 of 4: Challenge

    Ravi can do a job alone in 10 days. Sunita can do the same job alone in 15 days. They work together for 4 days, and then Ravi leaves. Each works at a steady rate.

    1. How much of the job is done after the first 4 days?
    2. How many more days does Sunita need to finish?
    3. How many days does the whole job take?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Split the story into two stages: together, then Sunita alone.

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. 2 over 3 of the job. (b) 5 more days. (c) 9 days in total.

    Find the mathematical structure

    • Stage 1: both work, so their rates add.
    • Stage 2: only Sunita works, at her own rate, on what is left.
    • The stages happen one after the other, so their times add.

    Build the reasoning

    Stage 1.

    1 over 10 + 1 over 15 = 1 over 6, meaning: of the job done each day together

    4 × 1 over 6 = 2 over 3, meaning: of the job done in 4 days

    Stage 2.

    1 − 2 over 3 = 1 over 3, meaning: of the job left

    1 over 3 ÷ 1 over 15 = 5, meaning: days for Sunita to finish

    Total.

    4 + 5 = 9, meaning: days altogether

    Alternative reasoning

    Pretend the job is 30 units (30 is the smallest number that 10 and 15 divide). Ravi does 3 units per day and Sunita 2 units per day. In 4 days together they do 4 × 5 = 20 units. The remaining 10 units take Sunita 10 ÷ 2 = 5 days.

    Check the answer

    Over 9 days, Sunita works all 9 days and does 9 over 15 of the job; Ravi works 4 days and does 4 over 10 of it. 9 over 15 + 4 over 10 = 3 over 5 + 2 over 5 = 1 whole job.

    General lesson

    Rates add for agents working at the same time; times add for stages that happen one after another. In staged problems, find the work done in each stage and keep track of what remains.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Changed conditions, Direct application.

    Level 5, question 11 of 20 (problem 76 of 140)

    Question 5.11Two pipes and a drain

    Difficulty 3 of 4: Challenge

    Pipe A alone can fill a tank in 12 hours, and pipe B alone can fill it in 15 hours. Drain C alone can empty the full tank in 20 hours. All three are opened when the tank is empty, and they work at steady rates. How long does the tank take to fill?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Pretend the tank holds a number of buckets that 12, 15 and 20 all divide into.

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    10 hours.

    Find the mathematical structure

    The two pipes add water and the drain removes it, all at the same time. Net rate = rate of A + rate of B − rate of C.

    Build the reasoning

    1 over 12 + 1 over 15 − 1 over 20 = 1 over 10, meaning: of the tank gained each hour

    Write the fractions with the common denominator 60 to see this: 5 over 60 + 4 over 60 − 3 over 60 = 6 over 60 = 1 over 10.

    1 ÷ 1 over 10 = 10, meaning: hours to fill the tank

    Alternative reasoning

    Convenient units. 60 is the smallest number divisible by 12, 15 and 20, so pretend the tank holds 60 buckets.

    60 ÷ 12 = 5, meaning: buckets per hour from A

    60 ÷ 15 = 4, meaning: buckets per hour from B

    60 ÷ 20 = 3, meaning: buckets per hour lost through C

    5 + 4 − 3 = 6, meaning: buckets gained per hour

    60 ÷ 6 = 10, meaning: hours

    Check the answer

    In 10 hours: A adds 10 over 12 of a tank, B adds 10 over 15, and C removes 10 over 20. 5 over 6 + 2 over 3 − 1 over 2 = 5 over 6 + 4 over 6 − 3 over 6 = 1 whole tank.

    General lesson

    With several agents, convert each into a rate (positive for filling, negative for emptying) and add them. A convenient number of units, often the least common multiple of the times, avoids fractions.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Direct application, Alternative solution.

    Level 5, question 12 of 20 (problem 77 of 140)

    Question 5.12The second pipe alone

    Difficulty 2 of 4: Core

    Two pipes working together fill an empty swimming pool in 6 hours. The first pipe alone would fill it in 10 hours. How long would the second pipe alone take? Both pipes flow at steady rates.

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    In one hour, the two pipes fill 1 over 6 of the pool. How much of that is from the first pipe?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    15 hours.

    Find the mathematical structure

    Combined rate = rate of first pipe + rate of second pipe. We know the combined rate and the first rate, so we subtract.

    Build the reasoning

    1 over 6 − 1 over 10 = 1 over 15, meaning: of the pool filled each hour by the second pipe

    1 ÷ 1 over 15 = 15, meaning: hours for the second pipe alone

    Alternative reasoning

    Pretend the pool holds 30 units. Together the pipes add 30 ÷ 6 = 5 units per hour, and the first pipe adds 30 ÷ 10 = 3 units per hour. So the second pipe adds 2 units per hour and needs 30 ÷ 2 = 15 hours.

    Check the answer

    • 1 over 10 + 1 over 15 = 3 over 30 + 2 over 30 = 5 over 30 = 1 over 6, so the two pipes together do fill the pool in 6 hours.
    • The second pipe must be slower than the pair (more than 6 hours). It is also slower than the first pipe, because together they take more than half of the first pipe's 10 hours.

    General lesson

    To find one agent's rate from a combined rate, subtract the known rates. Work in rates, then turn the final rate back into a time.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Reverse reasoning.

    Level 5, question 13 of 20 (problem 78 of 140)

    Question 5.13Overtaking on parallel tracks

    Difficulty 3 of 4: Challenge

    A train 200 m long travels at a steady 72 km/h. On a parallel track, in the same direction, a train 100 m long travels at a steady 54 km/h.

    1. How long does overtaking take, from the moment the front of the faster train reaches the back of the slower train until the back of the faster train passes the front of the slower train?
    2. If the two trains were travelling in opposite directions at the same speeds, how long would they take to pass each other completely, from the moment their fronts meet until their backs separate?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    How far must the faster train move relative to the slower one, and how fast does it gain on it?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. 60 seconds. (b) 8 and 4 over 7 seconds (about 8.6 seconds).

    Understand the situation

    During overtaking, what matters is how the faster train moves compared with the slower one. From the point of view of a passenger on the slower train, the faster train creeps forward at the difference of the speeds.

    Find the mathematical structure

    • Distance to be gained: at the start, the faster train's front is level with the slower train's back, so its own back is 200 m further behind. At the end, its back is level with the slower train's front, which is 100 m ahead of the slower train's back. Compared with the slower train, it must move 200 + 100 = 300 m: the sum of the two lengths.
    • Same direction: relative speed = difference of speeds. Opposite directions: relative speed = sum of speeds.

    Build the reasoning

    Convert the speeds to metres per second. Each km/h is 1000 over 3600 = 5 over 18 m/s.

    72 × 5 over 18 = 20, meaning: metres per second

    54 × 5 over 18 = 15, meaning: metres per second

    (a)

    20 − 15 = 5, meaning: metres per second gained by the faster train

    200 + 100 = 300, meaning: metres to be gained

    300 ÷ 5 = 60, meaning: seconds

    (b)

    20 + 15 = 35, meaning: metres per second closing speed

    300 ÷ 35 = 8 and 4 over 7, meaning: seconds

    Develop intuition

    Watch the trains from the slower train. It seems to stand still, and the faster train slides past at a gentle 5 m/s. Overtaking means sliding a whole 300 m: from "the fast train's front at the slow train's back" to "the fast train's back at the slow train's front".

    Check the answer

    In 60 seconds the faster train covers 1,200 m and the slower train covers 900 m. The difference, 300 m, is exactly the sum of their lengths.

    General lesson

    When objects with length pass each other, the distance is the sum of their lengths, and the speed is the relative speed: the difference for the same direction, the sum for opposite directions.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Direct application, Changed conditions.

    Level 5, question 14 of 20 (problem 79 of 140)

    Question 5.14Walking up the escalator

    Difficulty 3 of 4: Challenge

    Ira stands still on a moving escalator and is carried from the bottom to the top in 60 seconds. When the escalator is switched off, she walks up it in 90 seconds. How long does she take if she walks up the moving escalator at her usual pace?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    In one second, what fraction of the escalator does the machine carry her? What fraction do her legs carry her?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    36 seconds.

    Understand the situation

    When Ira walks on the moving escalator, two things move her up at the same time: the escalator and her own legs. This is a work problem in disguise, where the "job" is covering the length of the escalator.

    Find the mathematical structure

    The two contributions happen at the same time, so their rates add:

    • the escalator carries her 1 over 60 of the way each second;
    • walking carries her 1 over 90 of the way each second.

    Build the reasoning

    1 over 60 + 1 over 90 = 1 over 36, meaning: of the escalator covered each second

    1 ÷ 1 over 36 = 36, meaning: seconds

    Alternative reasoning

    Pretend the escalator has 180 steps (180 is divisible by 60 and 90). The machine moves her 3 steps per second and her legs move her 2 steps per second. Together, 5 steps per second, so 180 steps take 36 seconds.

    Check the answer

    The answer must be less than 60 seconds (faster than standing) and more than 30 seconds (two copies of the faster rate would take 60 ÷ 2 = 30 seconds). 36 seconds fits.

    General lesson

    Combined-rate thinking applies to motion too. Any two effects that act at the same time, such as an escalator and walking, or a river and rowing, add their rates.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Transfer.

    Level 5, question 15 of 20 (problem 80 of 140)

    Question 5.15A boat on a river

    Difficulty 3 of 4: Challenge

    A boat travels 24 km downstream in 2 hours, and then returns the same 24 km upstream in 3 hours. The boat's speed through the water and the river's current are both steady.

    1. Find the boat's speed in still water and the speed of the current.
    2. How long would a log, floating with the current, take to drift 24 km downstream?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Downstream, the current helps the boat; upstream, it holds it back. What are the two actual speeds?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. The boat's speed in still water is 10 km/h and the current is 2 km/h. (b) 12 hours.

    Find the mathematical structure

    • Downstream speed = boat speed + current speed.
    • Upstream speed = boat speed − current speed.

    The current adds on the way down and subtracts on the way up by the same amount.

    Build the reasoning

    24 ÷ 2 = 12, meaning: km/h downstream

    24 ÷ 3 = 8, meaning: km/h upstream

    The difference between these two speeds is twice the current, because the current is added once and subtracted once:

    (12 − 8) ÷ 2 = 2, meaning: km/h, the current

    12 − 2 = 10, meaning: km/h, the boat in still water

    (b) The log has no engine, so it moves at the speed of the current alone:

    24 ÷ 2 = 12, meaning: hours

    Develop intuition

    The boat's speed in still water lies exactly halfway between its downstream and upstream speeds: (12 + 8) ÷ 2 = 10 km/h. The current is the distance from that midpoint to either speed.

    Check the answer

    10 + 2 = 12 km/h, which covers 24 km in 2 hours. 10 − 2 = 8 km/h, which covers 24 km in 3 hours.

    General lesson

    When a steady effect (a current, a wind) helps one way and hinders the other, the average of the two speeds removes it, and half their difference measures it.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Reverse reasoning, Transfer.

    Level 5, question 16 of 20 (problem 81 of 140)

    Question 5.16Two cooks and 150 rotis

    Difficulty 2 of 4: Core

    Cook A makes 30 rotis in 10 minutes. Cook B makes 30 rotis in 15 minutes. They work together at these steady rates.

    1. How long do they take to make 150 rotis?
    2. How many of the 150 rotis does each cook make?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    How many rotis does each cook make in one minute?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. 30 minutes. (b) Cook A makes 90 rotis and Cook B makes 60.

    Find the mathematical structure

    This time the rates are counts per minute ("rotis per minute"), not fractions of a job. The idea is the same: working at the same time, their rates add.

    Build the reasoning

    30 ÷ 10 = 3, meaning: rotis per minute from Cook A

    30 ÷ 15 = 2, meaning: rotis per minute from Cook B

    3 + 2 = 5, meaning: rotis per minute together

    150 ÷ 5 = 30, meaning: minutes for 150 rotis

    (b) Both cooks work the full 30 minutes:

    3 × 30 = 90, meaning: rotis made by Cook A

    2 × 30 = 60, meaning: rotis made by Cook B

    Alternative reasoning

    Treat it as a fraction-of-a-job problem. Cook A alone would make 150 rotis in 50 minutes, and Cook B alone in 75 minutes. Together they make 1 over 50 + 1 over 75 = 3 over 150 + 2 over 150 = 5 over 150 = 1 over 30 of the order each minute, so 30 minutes. Counting rotis directly was simpler here.

    Check the answer

    90 + 60 = 150. Also, Cook A works 3 over 2 times as fast as Cook B, and 90 is 3 over 2 of 60.

    General lesson

    Rates can be counts per unit of time or fractions of a job per unit of time. Choose whichever makes the arithmetic simpler. Work shared in a fixed time is split in the ratio of the rates.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Alternative solution, Direct application.

    Level 5, question 17 of 20 (problem 82 of 140)

    Question 5.17Taking turns with the taps

    Difficulty 4 of 4: Olympiad

    Tap A alone fills a tank in 4 hours; tap B alone fills it in 6 hours. Starting with the tank empty, tap A is opened on its own for 1 hour, then tap B on its own for 1 hour, then A for 1 hour, and so on, switching every hour. Only one tap is ever open.

    1. How long does the tank take to fill?
    2. A student says: "Each 2 hours, the taps fill 1 over 4 + 1 over 6 = 5 over 12 of the tank, so the tank takes 24 over 5 hours, which is 4 hours 48 minutes." Find the flaw.
    3. If tap B were opened first instead, would the tank take the same time to fill?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Keep a running total of the fraction filled, hour by hour. Watch carefully what happens in the last hour.

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. 4 hours 40 minutes.
    2. The student assumes the tank fills at an average rate right to the end. But the last part is filled by tap A alone, which is faster than the average, so it finishes sooner.
    3. No. Starting with B, the tank takes exactly 5 hours.

    Find the mathematical structure

    The taps take turns, so in each hour only one rate applies. The pattern repeats every 2 hours, but the final stage must be calculated with the rate of whichever tap is running when the tank becomes full.

    Build the reasoning

    (a) Keep a running total.

    HourTap openFilled in that hourTotal filled
    1stA3 over 123 over 12
    2ndB2 over 125 over 12
    3rdA3 over 128 over 12
    4thB2 over 1210 over 12

    After 4 hours, 10 over 12 = 5 over 6 of the tank is full. In the 5th hour tap A is open, and only 1 over 6 of the tank remains.

    1 over 6 ÷ 1 over 4 = 2 over 3, meaning: hour for tap A to fill the last sixth

    2 over 3 of an hour is 40 minutes, so the tank is full after 4 hours 40 minutes.

    (c) Starting with B, the running totals after each hour are 2 over 12, 5 over 12, 7 over 12, 10 over 12. In the 5th hour tap B is open and must fill the last 2 over 12 = 1 over 6:

    1 over 6 ÷ 1 over 6 = 1, meaning: hour for tap B

    So the tank is full after exactly 5 hours.

    Develop intuition

    The two orders give the same total after every even number of hours, but whichever tap gets the last turn decides how quickly the final bit is filled. Starting with the faster tap gives it the last turn here.

    Check the answer

    In (a), tap A runs for 1 + 1 + 2 over 3 = 8 over 3 hours, filling 8 over 3 × 1 over 4 = 2 over 3 of the tank, and tap B runs for 2 hours, filling 2 over 6 = 1 over 3. Total: 1 tank.

    General lesson

    An average rate is safe only when the process runs in complete cycles. When the job ends part-way through a cycle, finish the calculation with the actual rate of the final stage.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Changed conditions, Error diagnosis.

    Level 5, question 18 of 20 (problem 83 of 140)

    Question 5.18Three journeys, three averages

    Difficulty 3 of 4: Challenge

    A car makes three different journeys.

    • Journey 1: 2 hours at 60 km/h, then 3 hours at 80 km/h.
    • Journey 2: 1 hour at 60 km/h, then 1 hour at 80 km/h.
    • Journey 3: 120 km at 60 km/h, then 120 km at 80 km/h.
    1. Find the average speed for each journey.
    2. For which journey does "add the two speeds and halve" give the correct average speed? Explain why it works for that journey and not the others.

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    For every journey, find the total distance and the total time.

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. Journey 1: 72 km/h. Journey 2: 70 km/h. Journey 3: 68 and 4 over 7 km/h (about 68.6 km/h).
    2. Only Journey 2, where the car spends equal times at each speed.

    Find the mathematical structure

    Average speed = total distance ÷ total time, in every case. The question is how much time is spent at each speed.

    Build the reasoning

    Journey 1.

    60 × 2 + 80 × 3 = 360, meaning: kilometres

    360 ÷ 5 = 72, meaning: km/h average

    Journey 2.

    60 + 80 = 140, meaning: kilometres in 2 hours

    140 ÷ 2 = 70, meaning: km/h average

    Journey 3.

    120 ÷ 60 + 120 ÷ 80 = 3.5, meaning: hours in total

    240 ÷ 3.5 = 68 and 4 over 7, meaning: km/h average

    Why equal times matter

    Think of the average speed as the average over every minute of the journey. In Journey 2, half the minutes are spent at 60 km/h and half at 80 km/h, so the simple average 70 km/h is right. In Journey 1, more minutes are spent at 80 km/h, so the average is pulled above 70. In Journey 3, the distances are equal, so more minutes are spent at the slower speed (2 hours against 1.5 hours), pulling the average below 70.

    Check the answer

    All three averages lie between 60 and 80 km/h, as they must. The equal-distance journey has the lowest average, which matches the hill problem: equal distances favour the slow part.

    General lesson

    "Add and halve" is correct only for equal times. For anything else, compute total distance ÷ total time.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Model selection, Comparison.

    Level 5, question 19 of 20 (problem 84 of 140)

    Question 5.19Runners on a circular track

    Difficulty 3 of 4: Challenge

    Arun and Bela start together from the same point on a 400 m circular track. Arun runs at a steady 5 m/s and Bela at a steady 3 m/s.

    1. If they run in the same direction, after how many seconds does Arun first catch up with Bela again?
    2. If they run in opposite directions, after how many seconds do they first meet?
    3. Running in opposite directions, how many times do they meet in the first 10 minutes? Do not count the start, but do count a meeting at exactly 10 minutes.

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    In (a), how much further than Bela must Arun run to catch her? In (b), how much track must they cover between them?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. 200 seconds. (b) 50 seconds. (c) 12 times.

    Find the mathematical structure

    • Same direction: Arun gains on Bela at 5 − 3 = 2 m/s. To catch her he must gain one full lap, 400 m.
    • Opposite directions: together they cover 5 + 3 = 8 m of track each second. They meet each time they have covered a full lap, 400 m, between them.

    Build the reasoning

    (a)

    5 − 3 = 2, meaning: metres per second gained by Arun

    400 ÷ 2 = 200, meaning: seconds to gain a full lap

    (b)

    5 + 3 = 8, meaning: metres per second covered between them

    400 ÷ 8 = 50, meaning: seconds until they first meet

    (c) They meet every 50 seconds. 10 minutes is 600 seconds.

    600 ÷ 50 = 12, meaning: meetings, the last one at exactly 600 seconds

    Check the answer

    • (a) In 200 seconds Arun runs 1,000 m (2.5 laps) and Bela runs 600 m (1.5 laps). Arun is exactly one lap ahead, so they are side by side.
    • (b) In 50 seconds Arun runs 250 m one way and Bela 150 m the other way: 250 + 150 = 400 m, the whole track.

    General lesson

    On a circular track, "catching up" means gaining a whole lap at the difference of the speeds, and "meeting head-on" means covering a whole lap between you at the sum of the speeds.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Transfer, Changed conditions.

    Level 5, question 20 of 20 (problem 85 of 140)

    Question 5.20When the clock hands meet

    Difficulty 4 of 4: Olympiad

    At 3:00 the minute hand of a clock points at 12 and the hour hand points at 3. Both hands turn at steady rates.

    1. How many degrees does each hand turn in one minute?
    2. At what time after 3:00 are the two hands first exactly on top of each other? Give the time to the nearest second.

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    A full turn is 360°. The minute hand makes one full turn every 60 minutes; the hour hand makes one full turn every 12 hours. Then this is a catching-up problem.

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. The minute hand turns 6° per minute; the hour hand turns 0.5° per minute.
    2. 16 and 4 over 11 minutes after 3:00, which is about 3:16:22.

    Understand the situation

    The minute hand is chasing the hour hand around the clock face. At 3:00 the hour hand is 90° ahead (a quarter of a turn), and the minute hand turns faster, so it catches up.

    Find the mathematical structure

    This is a catching-up problem with angles instead of kilometres: the gap of 90° closes at the difference of the two turning rates.

    Build the reasoning

    (a)

    360 ÷ 60 = 6, meaning: degrees per minute for the minute hand

    360 ÷ (12 × 60) = 0.5, meaning: degrees per minute for the hour hand

    (b)

    6 − 0.5 = 5.5, meaning: degrees per minute by which the gap closes

    90 ÷ 5.5 = 16 and 4 over 11, meaning: minutes after 3:00

    Convert the fraction of a minute into seconds:

    4 over 11 × 60 = 21 and 9 over 11, meaning: seconds, about 21.8 seconds

    To the nearest second, the hands meet at 3:16:22.

    Check the answer

    In 16 and 4 over 11 minutes the minute hand turns 6 × 16 and 4 over 11 = 98 and 2 over 11°, and the hour hand turns 0.5 × 16 and 4 over 11 = 8 and 2 over 11°, ending at 90° + 8 and 2 over 11° = 98 and 2 over 11° from 12. Both hands point in the same direction.

    Develop intuition

    The answer is a little after 3:15, not exactly 3:15, because while the minute hand travels to the 3, the hour hand has crept forward a little.

    General lesson

    Relative speed is not only for vehicles. Any chase, including two hands on a clock, closes the gap at the difference of the rates.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Transfer, Direct application.

    Level 6, question 1 of 20 (problem 86 of 140)

    Question 6.1Toys from the workshop

    Difficulty 2 of 4: Core

    12 workers make 360 toys in 5 days. How many toys will 18 workers make in 8 days? All the workers work at the same steady rate.

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    How many toys does one worker make in one day?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    864 toys.

    Understand the situation

    Two things change at once: the number of workers and the number of days. Both changes increase the output.

    Find the mathematical structure

    • Output depends on workers and on days. More workers make more toys; more days make more toys.
    • The natural unit is the worker-day: the work of one worker for one day. Output = (toys per worker-day) × (worker-days).
    • Assumptions: every worker is equally fast, and the rate does not change from day to day.

    Build the reasoning

    Method 1: through one worker-day.

    12 × 5 = 60, meaning: worker-days in the first situation

    360 ÷ 60 = 6, meaning: toys made in each worker-day

    18 × 8 = 144, meaning: worker-days in the new situation

    144 × 6 = 864, meaning: toys

    Method 2: one change at a time.

    • More workers, 12 → 18: output is multiplied by 18 over 12 = 3 over 2.
    • More days, 5 → 8: output is multiplied by 8 over 5.

    360 × 3 over 2 × 8 over 5 = 864, meaning: toys

    Develop intuition

    Picture a grid with one square for each worker on each day. The first situation has 12 × 5 = 60 squares and produces 360 toys, so each square produces 6 toys. The new grid has 18 × 8 = 144 squares.

    Check the answer

    Both changes push the output up, and 864 is much more than 360. Increasing the workers by half and the days by more than half should more than double the output: 864 is 2.4 times 360, and 3 over 2 × 8 over 5 = 2.4.

    General lesson

    When output depends on several quantities, either combine them into one unit (worker-days) or change one quantity at a time and multiply the factors. Both methods give the same answer.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Direct application, Alternative solution.

    Level 6, question 2 of 20 (problem 87 of 140)

    Question 6.2Machines making bolts

    Difficulty 3 of 4: Challenge

    10 identical machines working 8 hours a day produce 4,000 bolts in 5 days. How many days will 8 of these machines, working 10 hours a day, take to produce 6,000 bolts? Each machine works at a steady rate.

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    How many bolts does one machine make in one hour?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    7.5 days.

    Find the mathematical structure

    • The number of days depends on three things: the number of machines, the hours per day and the number of bolts.
    • More machines or more hours per day mean fewer days. More bolts mean more days.
    • The combined unit is the machine-hour: one machine running for one hour.

    Build the reasoning

    Method 1: machine-hours.

    10 × 8 × 5 = 400, meaning: machine-hours in the first situation

    4,000 ÷ 400 = 10, meaning: bolts per machine-hour

    6,000 ÷ 10 = 600, meaning: machine-hours needed for 6,000 bolts

    8 × 10 = 80, meaning: machine-hours available each day in the new situation

    600 ÷ 80 = 7.5, meaning: days

    Method 2: one change at a time, starting from 5 days.

    • Machines 10 → 8: fewer machines, so more days. Multiply by 10 over 8.
    • Hours per day 8 → 10: longer days, so fewer days. Multiply by 8 over 10.
    • Bolts 4,000 → 6,000: more bolts, so more days. Multiply by 6000 over 4000 = 3 over 2.

    5 × 10 over 8 × 8 over 10 × 3 over 2 = 7.5, meaning: days

    Develop intuition

    The first two changes cancel exactly: 8 machines for 10 hours a day give the same 80 machine-hours per day as 10 machines for 8 hours a day. Only the larger order matters, and 1.5 times the bolts take 1.5 times as long: 1.5 × 5 = 7.5 days.

    Check the answer

    8 machines × 10 hours × 7.5 days = 600 machine-hours, and 600 × 10 = 6,000 bolts.

    General lesson

    Predict the direction of each change before writing its factor. Factors that make the answer bigger are greater than 1; factors that make it smaller are less than 1. Combined units such as machine-hours often reveal that some changes cancel.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Direct application, Alternative solution.

    Level 6, question 3 of 20 (problem 88 of 140)

    Question 6.3Rice for a group

    Difficulty 2 of 4: Core

    15 people eat 45 kg of rice in 6 days. For how many days will 60 kg of rice last 20 people, if everyone eats the same amount each day?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    How much rice does one person eat in one day?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    6 days.

    Find the mathematical structure

    Rice eaten = (rice per person-day) × (number of people) × (number of days). One person-day is one person eating for one day.

    Build the reasoning

    15 × 6 = 90, meaning: person-days in the first situation

    45 ÷ 90 = 0.5, meaning: kilograms of rice per person-day

    60 ÷ 0.5 = 120, meaning: person-days of rice in 60 kg

    120 ÷ 20 = 6, meaning: days for 20 people

    Alternative reasoning

    One change at a time, starting from 6 days:

    • more rice (45 → 60 kg): more days, multiply by 60 over 45 = 4 over 3;
    • more people (15 → 20): fewer days, multiply by 15 over 20 = 3 over 4.

    6 × 4 over 3 × 3 over 4 = 6, meaning: days

    The two changes cancel: a third more rice is shared among a third more people.

    Check the answer

    20 people × 6 days × 0.5 kg = 60 kg.

    General lesson

    Find the amount used per person-day, then share the new supply. Opposite changes of the same size cancel.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Direct application.

    Level 6, question 4 of 20 (problem 89 of 140)

    Question 6.4Pumps and a bigger tank

    Difficulty 2 of 4: Core

    6 identical pumps, each working at a steady rate, empty a 9,000-litre tank in 5 hours. How long will 4 such pumps take to empty a 12,000-litre tank?

    Before calculating, predict whether each change (fewer pumps, more water) makes the time longer or shorter, and by what factor.

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Fewer pumps: longer or shorter? More water: longer or shorter? Write a factor for each.

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    Both changes make the time longer: fewer pumps multiply the time by 6 over 4 = 3 over 2, and more water multiplies it by 12 over 9 = 4 over 3. The time is 10 hours.

    Find the mathematical structure

    Time is inversely proportional to the number of pumps and directly proportional to the amount of water, assuming every pump removes water at the same steady rate.

    Build the reasoning

    One change at a time:

    5 × 6 over 4 = 7.5, meaning: hours for 4 pumps on the 9,000-litre tank

    7.5 × 12 over 9 = 10, meaning: hours for 4 pumps on the 12,000-litre tank

    Check with pump-hours:

    6 × 5 = 30, meaning: pump-hours to remove 9,000 litres

    9,000 ÷ 30 = 300, meaning: litres per pump-hour

    12,000 ÷ (4 × 300) = 10, meaning: hours

    Check the answer

    Both factors are greater than 1, so the answer must be more than 5 hours. It is: 5 × 3 over 2 × 4 over 3 = 5 × 2 = 10.

    General lesson

    Predicting directions first protects you from upside-down factors. If both changes make the job harder, the answer must grow.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Estimation and bounds, Direct application.

    Level 6, question 5 of 20 (problem 90 of 140)

    Question 6.5Laying a road

    Difficulty 3 of 4: Challenge

    9 workers, working 8 hours a day, lay 1.2 km of road in 10 days. How many workers, working 6 hours a day, are needed to lay 1.5 km of road in 15 days? Assume all workers work at the same steady rate.

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    How many worker-hours does one kilometre of road need?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    10 workers.

    Find the mathematical structure

    The unknown is the number of workers. The job is measured in worker-hours: one worker working for one hour. Each new worker contributes (hours per day) × (days) worker-hours.

    Build the reasoning

    9 × 8 × 10 = 720, meaning: worker-hours to lay 1.2 km

    720 ÷ 1.2 = 600, meaning: worker-hours for each kilometre

    1.5 × 600 = 900, meaning: worker-hours to lay 1.5 km

    6 × 15 = 90, meaning: hours that each new worker works

    900 ÷ 90 = 10, meaning: workers

    Alternative reasoning

    One change at a time, starting from 9 workers:

    • longer road (1.2 → 1.5 km): more workers, multiply by 1.5 over 1.2 = 5 over 4;
    • shorter working day (8 → 6 hours): more workers, multiply by 8 over 6 = 4 over 3;
    • more days (10 → 15): fewer workers, multiply by 10 over 15 = 2 over 3.

    9 × 5 over 4 × 4 over 3 × 2 over 3 = 10, meaning: workers

    Check the answer

    10 workers × 6 hours × 15 days = 900 worker-hours, which lays 900 ÷ 600 = 1.5 km.

    General lesson

    In a compound problem the unknown can be any of the quantities. Measure the whole job in a combined unit, then divide by what each unit of the unknown contributes.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Reverse reasoning, Direct application.

    Level 6, question 6 of 20 (problem 91 of 140)

    Question 6.6Cats and mice

    Difficulty 3 of 4: Challenge

    5 cats catch 5 mice in 5 minutes. How many cats are needed to catch 100 mice in 100 minutes? Assume every cat catches mice at the same steady rate, and there are always mice to catch.

    Most people's first answer is 100 cats. Find the correct answer and explain exactly what is wrong with 100.

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    How many mice does one cat catch in 5 minutes? And in 100 minutes?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    5 cats. The answer 100 scales the number of cats up with the number of mice but forgets that the cats also get 20 times as long to catch them.

    Find the mathematical structure

    Mice caught = (mice per cat-minute) × (cats) × (minutes). Two quantities change: the number of mice (×20) and the time (×20). More mice need more cats, but more time needs fewer cats.

    Build the reasoning

    Through one cat. 5 cats catch 5 mice in 5 minutes, so each cat catches 1 mouse in 5 minutes.

    100 ÷ 5 = 20, meaning: periods of 5 minutes in 100 minutes

    20 × 1 = 20, meaning: mice caught by one cat in 100 minutes

    100 ÷ 20 = 5, meaning: cats needed for 100 mice

    One change at a time, starting from 5 cats:

    • 20 times as many mice: multiply the cats by 20.
    • 20 times as long: divide the cats by 20.

    5 × 20 ÷ 20 = 5, meaning: cats

    Develop intuition

    Nothing about the situation needs more cats. The same 5 cats simply keep hunting. In every 5 minutes they catch 5 more mice, and after 20 such periods they have caught 100.

    Check the answer

    Cat-minutes: 5 cats × 5 minutes = 25 cat-minutes per 5 mice, so 5 cat-minutes per mouse. 100 mice need 500 cat-minutes, and 500 ÷ 100 minutes = 5 cats.

    General lesson

    In compound problems, every quantity that changes must be accounted for. The answer "100" responds to the change in mice but ignores the change in time.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Error diagnosis.

    Level 6, question 7 of 20 (problem 92 of 140)

    Question 6.7Tailors and trainees

    Difficulty 3 of 4: Challenge

    A school orders new uniforms. 6 experienced tailors can stitch the whole order in 15 days, or 10 trainee tailors can stitch the same order in 15 days. All the experienced tailors work at the same rate as each other, and all the trainees work at the same rate as each other.

    1. How long will 3 experienced tailors and 5 trainees take, working together?
    2. How long will 3 experienced tailors and 4 trainees take?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    6 experienced tailors do the same work in a day as 10 trainees. How many trainees is one experienced tailor worth?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. 15 days. (b) 16 and 2 over 3 days.

    Find the mathematical structure

    Because 6 experienced tailors and 10 trainees do the same work in a day, we can convert everyone into a common unit: trainee-equivalents. One experienced tailor = 10 over 6 = 5 over 3 trainees. Then the team is an ordinary number of identical workers.

    Build the reasoning

    The job in trainee-days:

    10 × 15 = 150, meaning: trainee-days of work

    (a)

    3 × 5 over 3 = 5, meaning: trainee-equivalents from 3 experienced tailors

    5 + 5 = 10, meaning: trainee-equivalents in the team

    150 ÷ 10 = 15, meaning: days

    (b)

    5 + 4 = 9, meaning: trainee-equivalents in the team

    150 ÷ 9 = 16 and 2 over 3, meaning: days

    Develop intuition

    In (a), 3 experienced tailors are half of the 6-tailor team and 5 trainees are half of the 10-trainee team. Two half-teams make one full team's worth of work, so the time is the same 15 days.

    Check the answer

    1. has one trainee fewer than (a), so it should take a little longer than 15 days. 16 and 2 over 3 days fits.

    General lesson

    When workers have different rates, convert them into one common unit before using worker-days. Use the information "A or B can do the job in the same time" to find the exchange rate between them.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Direct application, Transfer.

    Level 6, question 8 of 20 (problem 93 of 140)

    Question 6.8A taller, longer wall

    Difficulty 3 of 4: Challenge

    6 artists paint a mural covering a wall 30 m long and 4 m high in 5 days. How many days will 8 artists take to paint a mural in the same style covering a wall 40 m long and 6 m high? All the artists work at the same steady rate, and both murals need the same amount of work for each square metre of wall.

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    What measure of a wall decides how much painting it needs: its length, its height, or something built from both?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    7.5 days.

    Understand the situation

    The wall changes in two directions at once. The amount of painting depends on the area of the wall, which is length × height. This is a hidden quantity that the question never names.

    Find the mathematical structure

    Days are proportional to the area to be painted and inversely proportional to the number of artists.

    Build the reasoning

    30 × 4 = 120, meaning: square metres in the first wall

    40 × 6 = 240, meaning: square metres in the second wall

    6 × 5 = 30, meaning: artist-days for 120 m²

    30 ÷ 120 = 1 over 4, meaning: artist-day for each square metre

    240 × 1 over 4 = 60, meaning: artist-days for the second wall

    60 ÷ 8 = 7.5, meaning: days

    Alternative reasoning

    One change at a time, starting from 5 days:

    • area doubles (120 → 240 m²): multiply by 2;
    • more artists (6 → 8): multiply by 6 over 8 = 3 over 4.

    5 × 2 × 3 over 4 = 7.5, meaning: days

    Check the answer

    A common mistake is to scale by the length only (×4 over 3) or by the height only (×3 over 2). Using both gives 4 over 3 × 3 over 2 = 2, which is exactly the area factor. As a check: 8 artists for 7.5 days give 60 artist-days, and 240 m² at 1 over 4 artist-day per square metre also needs 60 artist-days.

    General lesson

    Sometimes the quantity that matters is built from two others, such as area = length × height. Identify the real measure of the work before applying proportion.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Missing quantity, Direct application.

    Level 6, question 9 of 20 (problem 94 of 140)

    Question 6.9Rice in the hostel

    Difficulty 3 of 4: Challenge
