    A hostel has enough rice to give 120 students 400 g each per day for 30 days. Before any rice is used, 30 more students arrive, and the daily ration is cut to 360 g per student.

    1. For how many complete days will the rice last?
    2. How much rice is left after the last complete day?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Work out the total amount of rice, then the amount used each day under the new arrangement.

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. 26 complete days. (b) 36 kg left, which is not enough for a 27th day.

    Find the mathematical structure

    The rice store is fixed. Daily use = (number of students) × (ration per student). Days = store ÷ daily use. Assumption: every student eats exactly the ration each day.

    Build the reasoning

    The store:

    120 × 400 × 30 = 1,440,000, meaning: grams of rice

    That is 1,440 kg.

    New daily use:

    120 + 30 = 150, meaning: students

    150 × 360 = 54,000, meaning: grams used each day, which is 54 kg

    (a)

    1,440 ÷ 54 = 26 and 2 over 3, meaning: days' worth of rice

    So the rice lasts 26 complete days.

    (b)

    26 × 54 = 1,404, meaning: kilograms used in 26 days

    1,440 − 1,404 = 36, meaning: kilograms left

    A 27th day would need 54 kg, so the 36 kg is not enough.

    Alternative reasoning

    One change at a time, starting from 30 days:

    • more students (120 → 150): multiply by 120 over 150 = 4 over 5;
    • smaller ration (400 g → 360 g): multiply by 400 over 360 = 10 over 9.

    30 × 4 over 5 × 10 over 9 = 26 and 2 over 3, meaning: days

    Check the answer

    More students and a smaller ration pull in opposite directions. More students win: the rice now lasts under 27 days instead of 30.

    General lesson

    Compound proportion gives the exact number of days' worth of supply. When only complete days count, round down and report the leftover.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Changed conditions, Direct application.

    Level 6, question 10 of 20 (problem 95 of 140)

    Question 6.10Wages for a team

    Difficulty 2 of 4: Core

    12 workers, each working 8 hours a day for 6 days, are paid ₹86,400 altogether. At the same rate of pay per hour, how much will 9 workers earn altogether for working 6 hours a day for 10 days?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    What is one worker paid for one hour?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    ₹81,000.

    Find the mathematical structure

    Pay = (pay per worker-hour) × (worker-hours). Worker-hours = workers × hours per day × days.

    Build the reasoning

    12 × 8 × 6 = 576, meaning: worker-hours in the first job

    86,400 ÷ 576 = 150, meaning: rupees per worker-hour

    9 × 6 × 10 = 540, meaning: worker-hours in the second job

    540 × 150 = 81,000, meaning: rupees

    Alternative reasoning

    One change at a time, starting from ₹86,400:

    86,400 × 9 over 12 × 6 over 8 × 10 over 6 = 81,000, meaning: rupees

    Fewer workers and shorter days lower the pay; more days raise it.

    Check the answer

    The second job has 540 worker-hours against 576, slightly fewer, so the pay should be slightly less than ₹86,400. It is.

    General lesson

    Combined units such as worker-hours work for money as well as for output: pay per worker-hour plays the role of a price per unit.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Direct application, Transfer.

    Level 6, question 11 of 20 (problem 96 of 140)

    Question 6.11A long road trip

    Difficulty 2 of 4: Core

    Driving 6 hours a day at an average speed of 50 km/h, a family completes a long road trip in 8 days. How many days of driving would the same trip take if they drove 5 hours a day at an average speed of 64 km/h? (Count part of a day's driving as a fraction of a day.)

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    What stays the same? Find it first.

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    7.5 days of driving: 7 full days, and then 2.5 hours of driving on the 8th day.

    Find the mathematical structure

    The fixed quantity is the distance of the trip. Distance per day = speed × hours per day.

    Build the reasoning

    50 × 6 = 300, meaning: kilometres per day at first

    300 × 8 = 2,400, meaning: kilometres in the trip

    64 × 5 = 320, meaning: kilometres per day with the new plan

    2,400 ÷ 320 = 7.5, meaning: days

    Alternative reasoning

    One change at a time: fewer hours per day (6 → 5) means more days, multiply by 6 over 5; faster speed (50 → 64) means fewer days, multiply by 50 over 64.

    8 × 6 over 5 × 50 over 64 = 7.5, meaning: days

    Check the answer

    The new plan covers 320 km a day instead of 300 km, a little more, so the trip should take a little less than 8 days. 7.5 days fits.

    General lesson

    Find the conserved total first (here, distance). Then each new plan is a rate per day, and the number of days is total ÷ daily rate.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Direct application.

    Level 6, question 12 of 20 (problem 97 of 140)

    Question 6.12More workers for the bridge

    Difficulty 3 of 4: Challenge

    40 workers can build a bridge in 60 days. After 20 days of work, 10 more workers join them. All the workers work at the same rate. How many days does the whole bridge take?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Measure the bridge in worker-days. How much is done in the first 20 days?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    52 days in total.

    Find the mathematical structure

    The job is fixed in worker-days. The story has two stages: 40 workers, then 50 workers. Times for stages done one after another add up.

    Build the reasoning

    40 × 60 = 2,400, meaning: worker-days for the bridge

    40 × 20 = 800, meaning: worker-days done in the first 20 days

    2,400 − 800 = 1,600, meaning: worker-days still to do

    40 + 10 = 50, meaning: workers in the second stage

    1,600 ÷ 50 = 32, meaning: days for the second stage

    20 + 32 = 52, meaning: days in total

    Alternative reasoning

    After 20 days, 1 over 3 of the bridge is built, and the remaining 2 over 3 would take the original 40 workers 40 more days. With 50 workers instead of 40, it takes 40 over 50 of that time: 4 over 5 × 40 = 32 days.

    Check the answer

    40 × 20 + 50 × 32 = 800 + 1,600 = 2,400 worker-days, the whole bridge.

    General lesson

    In staged problems, measure what is left at the moment conditions change, then apply proportion to that remainder.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Changed conditions, Direct application.

    Level 6, question 13 of 20 (problem 98 of 140)

    Question 6.13Ploughing the fields

    Difficulty 2 of 4: Core

    A farm's tractors, working 6 hours a day, plough all its fields in 18 days. For each change below, say whether the number of days goes up or down, and by what factor, when everything else stays the same.

    1. Twice as many tractors.
    2. Fields with 3 times the area.
    3. Tractors working 9 hours a day instead of 6 hours a day.
    4. New tractors that each plough 1.5 times as fast as the old ones.
    5. If all four changes happen together, how many days are needed?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    For each change ask: does it make the job bigger, or the workforce stronger?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. Down: multiply by 1 over 2. (b) Up: multiply by 3. (c) Down: multiply by 2 over 3. (d) Down: multiply by 2 over 3. (e) 12 days.

    Find the mathematical structure

    • The size of the job (area) is directly proportional to the days.
    • The strength of the workforce (number of tractors, hours per day, speed of each tractor) is inversely proportional to the days.
    • Each change multiplies the number of days by its own factor, so several changes multiply together.

    Build the reasoning

    • (a) Twice the tractors share the work: the time is halved, factor 1 over 2.
    • (b) Three times the area is three times the work: factor 3.
    • (c) Hours per day go from 6 to 9, so each day does 9 over 6 = 3 over 2 as much: factor 6 over 9 = 2 over 3.
    • (d) Each tractor does 1.5 = 3 over 2 times as much: factor 2 over 3.

    18 × 1 over 2 × 3 × 2 over 3 × 2 over 3 = 12, meaning: days

    Develop intuition

    Combine the factors in pairs: twice the tractors with three times the work gives 3 over 2 times the days; the longer days and faster tractors together divide the days by 3 over 2 × 3 over 2 = 9 over 4. Overall, 3 over 2 × 4 over 9 = 2 over 3, and 2 over 3 of 18 days is 12 days.

    Check the answer

    The workforce becomes 2 × 3 over 2 × 3 over 2 = 9 over 2 times as strong, while the job becomes 3 times as big. A stronger workforce on a job that grew less should finish sooner: 3 ÷ 9 over 2 = 2 over 3, so 12 days.

    General lesson

    In compound proportion, each change contributes its own factor and the factors multiply. Sort the quantities into "size of the job" (direct) and "strength of the workforce" (inverse).

    Thinking skills in this problem: Model selection, Estimation and bounds.

    Level 6, question 14 of 20 (problem 99 of 140)

    Question 6.14Feeding horses

    Difficulty 3 of 4: Challenge

    Feeding 25 horses for 12 days costs ₹18,000. The price of the feed then rises by one fifth. What will it cost to feed 15 horses for 32 days at the new price? Every horse eats the same amount each day.

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    What does it cost to feed one horse for one day at the old price? What does "rises by one fifth" do to that?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    ₹34,560.

    Find the mathematical structure

    Cost = (cost per horse-day) × (horses) × (days). A price rise multiplies the cost per horse-day by 1 + 1 over 5 = 6 over 5.

    Build the reasoning

    25 × 12 = 300, meaning: horse-days at first

    18,000 ÷ 300 = 60, meaning: rupees per horse-day at the old price

    60 × 6 over 5 = 72, meaning: rupees per horse-day at the new price

    15 × 32 = 480, meaning: horse-days now

    480 × 72 = 34,560, meaning: rupees

    Alternative reasoning

    One change at a time, starting from ₹18,000:

    18,000 × 15 over 25 × 32 over 12 × 6 over 5 = 34,560, meaning: rupees

    Check the answer

    Without the price rise, 480 horse-days would cost 480 × 60 = ₹28,800; adding one fifth gives 28,800 + 5,760 = ₹34,560.

    General lesson

    A percentage or fractional change in a rate is just another factor. Find the cost of one combined unit (a horse-day), adjust it, then scale.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Direct application, Changed conditions.

    Level 6, question 15 of 20 (problem 100 of 140)

    Question 6.15Packing gift boxes

    Difficulty 2 of 4: Core

    4 volunteers working for 5 hours pack 600 gift boxes. How many volunteers, packing at the same rate, are needed to pack 900 gift boxes in 3 hours?

    1. Solve it by changing one quantity at a time.
    2. Solve it again using volunteer-hours.
    3. Explain why the two methods must give the same answer.

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    For (a): to pack more boxes in the same time, do you need more or fewer volunteers? To pack them in less time?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    10 volunteers, by both methods. (c) Both methods use the same fact, that every volunteer-hour packs the same number of boxes, so they are the same calculation done in a different order.

    Build the reasoning

    (a) One change at a time. Start with 4 volunteers, 5 hours, 600 boxes.

    • 900 boxes instead of 600, still in 5 hours: 900 over 600 = 3 over 2 times as many volunteers.

    4 × 3 over 2 = 6, meaning: volunteers to pack 900 boxes in 5 hours

    • 3 hours instead of 5: less time needs more volunteers, 5 over 3 times as many.

    6 × 5 over 3 = 10, meaning: volunteers to pack 900 boxes in 3 hours

    (b) Volunteer-hours.

    4 × 5 = 20, meaning: volunteer-hours for 600 boxes

    600 ÷ 20 = 30, meaning: boxes per volunteer-hour

    900 ÷ 30 = 30, meaning: volunteer-hours for 900 boxes

    30 ÷ 3 = 10, meaning: volunteers working for 3 hours

    Why they must agree

    Method (a) computes 4 × 900 over 600 × 5 over 3. Method (b) computes (900 ÷ (600 ÷ (4 × 5))) ÷ 3. Rearranging the second expression gives exactly the same multiplications and divisions. Both rest on a single assumption: each volunteer packs 30 boxes every hour, whoever they are and however many others are packing.

    Check the answer

    10 volunteers × 3 hours × 30 boxes = 900 boxes.

    General lesson

    "One at a time" and "combined units" are two views of the same structure. Use whichever is clearer, and use the other as a check.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Alternative solution, Proof and explanation.

    Level 6, question 16 of 20 (problem 101 of 140)

    Question 6.16When the model breaks down

    Difficulty 3 of 4: Challenge

    5 painters paint 8 identical classrooms in 4 days.

    1. Assuming every painter works at the same steady rate, how many painter-days does one classroom need?
    2. A teacher predicts that 10 painters would paint the 8 classrooms in 2 days. What assumptions make this prediction reasonable?
    3. She then predicts that 160 painters could finish all 8 classrooms in one hour of an 8-hour working day. Use the model to check her arithmetic, and then explain why the prediction is unrealistic.

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    For (c), picture 20 painters squeezed into each classroom. What would actually happen?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. 2.5 painter-days per classroom.
    2. That every painter works equally fast, that painters do not get in each other's way, and that there is enough space, equipment and paint for everyone.
    3. The arithmetic is right: 20 painter-days is 160 painter-hours, and 160 painters supply 160 painter-hours in one hour. But the model has stopped matching reality: 20 painters in one room would be crowded, and wet paint needs time to dry between coats, however many painters there are.

    Build the reasoning

    (a)

    5 × 4 = 20, meaning: painter-days for 8 classrooms

    20 ÷ 8 = 2.5, meaning: painter-days per classroom

    (b)

    20 ÷ 10 = 2, meaning: days for 10 painters

    (c)

    20 × 8 = 160, meaning: painter-hours for the 8 classrooms

    160 ÷ 160 = 1, meaning: hour for 160 painters

    Where the model breaks

    The inverse model "twice the workers, half the time" assumes that the work can be shared out perfectly. Real jobs have limits:

    • Space: only a few people can work on one wall at a time.
    • Steps that must wait: a second coat cannot start until the first coat is dry. Drying time does not shrink when more painters arrive, just as the shirts in problem 4.5 dried in the same time however many were on the line.
    • Organisation: many workers need time to share out tasks and equipment.

    So the model is reasonable for 10 painters, doubtful for 40, and clearly wrong for 160.

    General lesson

    Every proportional model has a range where it works. Always ask whether the assumptions still hold at the numbers you are using.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Assumption check, Counterexample.

    Level 6, question 17 of 20 (problem 102 of 140)

    Question 6.17Seniors and juniors

    Difficulty 3 of 4: Challenge

    On a building site, 2 senior workers do as much work in a day as 3 junior workers. A team of 4 seniors and 6 juniors finishes a job in 9 days. How many juniors must work alongside the 4 seniors so that the same job is finished in 6 days? All seniors work at the same rate as each other, and so do all juniors.

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Convert the seniors into junior-equivalents.

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    12 juniors (6 more than in the first team).

    Find the mathematical structure

    Measure everyone in junior-equivalents. One senior = 3 over 2 juniors. Then the job is a fixed number of junior-days.

    Build the reasoning

    4 × 3 over 2 = 6, meaning: junior-equivalents from 4 seniors

    6 + 6 = 12, meaning: junior-equivalents in the first team

    12 × 9 = 108, meaning: junior-days in the job

    108 ÷ 6 = 18, meaning: junior-equivalents needed to finish in 6 days

    18 − 6 = 12, meaning: juniors to join the 4 seniors

    Alternative reasoning

    One change at a time. To finish in 6 days instead of 9, the team must be 9 over 6 = 3 over 2 times as strong: 12 × 3 over 2 = 18 junior-equivalents. The 4 seniors provide 6 of them, so 12 juniors are needed.

    Check the answer

    4 seniors and 12 juniors give 6 + 12 = 18 junior-equivalents, and 18 × 6 = 108 junior-days.

    General lesson

    Convert different workers into one unit before using inverse proportion. Then subtract the part of the workforce that is already fixed.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Reverse reasoning, Transfer.

    Level 6, question 18 of 20 (problem 103 of 140)

    Question 6.18A hen and a half

    Difficulty 4 of 4: Olympiad

    "A hen and a half lays an egg and a half in a day and a half. How many eggs do 6 hens lay in 6 days?"

    Assume every hen lays at the same steady rate; the halves are just a way of describing that rate.

    Many people answer 6 eggs. Find the correct answer and explain the error.

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Change one quantity at a time. What happens if you keep 1.5 hens but give them 6 days instead of 1.5 days?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    24 eggs. The answer 6 changes the hens (×4) and the eggs (×4) together but forgets that the days also change (×4).

    Find the mathematical structure

    Eggs = (eggs per hen-day) × (hens) × (days). Both more hens and more days produce more eggs, so both factors multiply.

    Build the reasoning

    One change at a time, starting from 1.5 eggs:

    • Hens 1.5 → 6: multiply the eggs by 4.

    1.5 × 4 = 6, meaning: eggs from 6 hens in a day and a half

    • Days 1.5 → 6: multiply the eggs by 4 again.

    6 × 4 = 24, meaning: eggs from 6 hens in 6 days

    Through one hen-day:

    1.5 × 1.5 = 2.25, meaning: hen-days in the given situation

    1.5 ÷ 2.25 = 2 over 3, meaning: of an egg per hen-day

    6 × 6 × 2 over 3 = 24, meaning: eggs

    Develop intuition

    The answer 6 is the number of eggs that 6 hens lay in a day and a half. Leaving the hens for 6 days instead, four times as long, gives four times as many eggs.

    Check the answer

    At 2 over 3 egg per hen-day, 1.5 hens in 1.5 days lay 2.25 × 2 over 3 = 1.5 eggs, which matches the puzzle.

    General lesson

    In compound problems, track every quantity that changes. The puzzle's odd fractions are designed to distract from the simple fact that both the hens and the days were multiplied by 4.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Error diagnosis, Direct application.

    Level 6, question 19 of 20 (problem 104 of 140)

    Question 6.19Build a problem with answer 15

    Difficulty 3 of 4: Challenge

    Invent a compound-proportion problem about workers building a wall. It must contain the number of workers, the hours they work each day, the length of wall built and the number of days. In your problem:

    • the number of workers is doubled,
    • the hours worked per day are halved,
    • the length of wall is multiplied by 3 over 2, and
    • the answer (the new number of days) is exactly 15 days.

    Solve your problem to show it works, and explain why the doubling and halving cancel each other.

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Work backwards: if only the length changed by 3 over 2, what must the original number of days have been?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    The original number of days must be 10. One example: "6 workers, working 8 hours a day, build 120 m of wall in 10 days. How many days will 12 workers, working 4 hours a day, take to build 180 m of the same wall?" The answer is 15 days.

    Designing the problem

    The new number of days = original days × (factor for workers) × (factor for hours) × (factor for length).

    • Workers doubled: days × 1 over 2.
    • Hours halved: days × 2.
    • Length × 3 over 2: days × 3 over 2.

    1 over 2 × 2 × 3 over 2 = 3 over 2, meaning: overall factor

    To get 15 days, the original must be 15 ÷ 3 over 2 = 10 days. Any starting numbers of workers, hours and metres will do, as long as the original number of days is 10.

    Solving the example

    6 × 8 × 10 = 480, meaning: worker-hours to build 120 m

    480 ÷ 120 = 4, meaning: worker-hours per metre

    180 × 4 = 720, meaning: worker-hours to build 180 m

    12 × 4 = 48, meaning: worker-hours per day in the new team

    720 ÷ 48 = 15, meaning: days

    Why doubling and halving cancel

    Twice as many workers each working half as many hours give the same number of worker-hours per day: 12 × 4 = 6 × 8 = 48. The team's daily effort does not change, so only the longer wall affects the answer.

    General lesson

    Designing a problem backwards from its answer shows how each factor works. Changes that leave the combined unit (worker-hours per day) unchanged have no effect on the answer.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Problem construction, Proof and explanation.

    Level 6, question 20 of 20 (problem 105 of 140)

    Question 6.20Upside-down factors

    Difficulty 3 of 4: Challenge

    18 workers, working 8 hours a day, dig a trench 240 m long in 10 days. A student finds how many days 12 workers, working 9 hours a day, need to dig a trench 180 m long:

    "Days = 10 × 12 over 18 × 9 over 8 × 180 over 240 = 5.625 days."

    1. Which of the student's factors are upside down? Explain how you can tell without calculating.
    2. Find the correct number of days.

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    For each change, ask: should it make the number of days bigger or smaller? Then check whether the student's factor is more than 1 or less than 1.

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. The workers factor and the hours factor are upside down. Fewer workers should mean more days, but 12 over 18 is less than 1. Longer working days should mean fewer days, but 9 over 8 is more than 1. The length factor 180 over 240 is correct: a shorter trench needs fewer days.
    2. 10 days.

    Build the reasoning

    Correct factors:

    • Workers 18 → 12: more days, factor 18 over 12 = 3 over 2.
    • Hours 8 → 9: fewer days, factor 8 over 9.
    • Length 240 → 180 m: fewer days, factor 180 over 240 = 3 over 4.

    10 × 3 over 2 × 8 over 9 × 3 over 4 = 10, meaning: days

    Check with worker-hours:

    18 × 8 × 10 = 1,440, meaning: worker-hours for 240 m

    1,440 ÷ 240 = 6, meaning: worker-hours per metre

    180 × 6 = 1,080, meaning: worker-hours for 180 m

    12 × 9 = 108, meaning: worker-hours per day in the new team

    1,080 ÷ 108 = 10, meaning: days

    Check the answer

    The changes pull in different directions: fewer workers (slower) but longer days and a shorter trench (faster). It is believable that they balance out exactly. The student's 5.625 days would mean that a smaller team finishes much faster, which should have raised suspicion.

    General lesson

    Write each factor only after deciding its direction. A factor that makes the answer bigger must be greater than 1, and one that makes it smaller must be less than 1.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Error diagnosis, Estimation and bounds.

    Level 7, question 1 of 25 (problem 106 of 140)

    Question 7.1Heaters at the research station

    Difficulty 2 of 4: Core

    A polar research station has enough fuel to run 12 identical heaters, all day and every day, for 60 days. After 15 days, some extra heaters of the same kind are switched on, and from then on the remaining fuel lasts only 30 more days. Every heater burns fuel at the same steady rate. How many extra heaters were switched on?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Measure the fuel in "heater-days": the fuel one heater burns in one day. How much is left after the first 15 days?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    6 extra heaters were switched on, so 18 heaters were running after the change.

    Understand the situation

    The story has two stages. For the first 15 days, 12 heaters burn fuel. Then more heaters are switched on, and the fuel that is left runs out after 30 more days. We must work backwards from "30 more days" to the number of heaters.

    Find the mathematical structure

    • What stays the same: the amount of fuel, measured in heater-days. The amount at the start is fixed, and so is the amount left at the moment of the change.
    • What changes: the number of heaters burning, and so how fast the fuel runs out.
    • Connection: for the fuel left after the change, (number of heaters) × (days it lasts) = heater-days left.

    Build the reasoning

    Step 1: the whole store.

    12 × 60 = 720, meaning: heater-days of fuel at the start

    Step 2: what the first stage used.

    12 × 15 = 180, meaning: heater-days burned in the first 15 days

    720 − 180 = 540, meaning: heater-days left at the change

    Step 3: work backwards from 30 days. The 540 heater-days are used up in 30 days, so each day must use:

    540 ÷ 30 = 18, meaning: heater-days per day, so 18 heaters are running

    18 − 12 = 6, meaning: extra heaters

    Develop intuition

    Think of the fuel as 720 tickets, with every heater using one ticket a day. After 15 days, 540 tickets are left. If they are all gone after 30 more days, then 18 tickets were used each day: one for each of 18 heaters.

    Check the answer

    • 12 heaters for 15 days and 18 heaters for 30 days use 180 + 540 = 720 heater-days, the whole store.
    • Direction: the fuel ran out sooner than planned (after 45 days instead of 60), so more heaters must have been running. 18 is more than 12.
    • Tempting error: using the whole store again, 720 ÷ 30 = 24 heaters. That forgets the fuel already burned in the first 15 days.

    Alternative reasoning

    Without the change, the fuel left after 15 days would have lasted the 12 heaters 60 − 15 = 45 more days. It lasted only 30 days, which is 30 over 45 = 2 over 3 as long. With a fixed amount of fuel, 2 over 3 of the time means 3 over 2 times as many heaters:

    12 × 3 over 2 = 18, meaning: heaters after the change

    General lesson

    In a story with stages, stop the clock at the moment of change and measure what is left. Inverse proportion then works in either direction: from heaters to days, or, as here, backwards from days to heaters.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Reverse reasoning, Changed conditions.

    Level 7, question 2 of 25 (problem 107 of 140)

    Question 7.2Stops along the way

    Difficulty 2 of 4: Core

    Whenever a bus is moving, it travels at 60 km/h. Because of its stops, its average speed for the whole journey is only 45 km/h.

    1. On average, for how many minutes is the bus stopped in each hour of the journey?
    2. How long does a 270 km journey take?
    3. If the stops could be cut to 6 minutes in every hour, what would the average speed become?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    In one hour of the journey the bus covers 45 km. How long does it take to cover 45 km while it is moving?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. 15 minutes in each hour. (b) 6 hours. (c) 54 km/h.

    Understand the situation

    "Average speed 45 km/h" means that over the whole journey, stops included, the bus covers 45 km for every hour that passes. The moving speed tells us how much of each hour is spent actually moving.

    Find the mathematical structure

    • Each hour of the journey splits into moving time and stopped time.
    • Distance covered in an hour comes only from the moving time, at 60 km/h. That is 1 km for every minute of moving.
    • Hidden quantity: the moving time in each hour. Once we know it, everything else follows.

    Build the reasoning

    (a) In one hour of the journey the bus covers 45 km. At 1 km per minute of moving, that needs 45 minutes of moving.

    45 ÷ 60 × 60 = 45, meaning: minutes of moving in each hour

    60 − 45 = 15, meaning: minutes stopped in each hour

    (b) Average speed is total distance ÷ total time, so total time = total distance ÷ average speed.

    270 ÷ 45 = 6, meaning: hours for the journey

    (c) With only 6 minutes of stops, the bus moves for 54 minutes of each hour, covering 54 km.

    60 − 6 = 54, meaning: minutes of moving, so 54 km, in each hour

    Develop intuition

    Picture each hour as a strip of 60 boxes, one for each minute. In 45 of the boxes the bus moves 1 km; in the other 15 it stands still. The average speed simply counts the kilometres in the strip.

    Check the answer

    • (b) In 6 hours the bus is stopped for 6 × 15 = 90 minutes, so it moves for 4.5 hours, and 4.5 × 60 = 270 km.
    • The average speed must be less than the moving speed, because stops add time but no distance. Both 45 km/h and 54 km/h are less than 60 km/h.

    General lesson

    An average speed hides the stops inside it. Split the time into moving time and stopped time: the distance comes only from the moving time.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Missing quantity, Changed conditions.

    Level 7, question 3 of 25 (problem 108 of 140)

    Question 7.3Behind schedule in the tunnel

    Difficulty 3 of 4: Challenge

    A company promises to dig a metro tunnel in 40 days, using 30 workers. All 30 workers dig every day, each at the same steady rate. After 25 days, only 2 over 5 of the tunnel has been dug. How many more workers, digging at the same rate, must join so that the tunnel is finished on time?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Do not trust the company's plan; trust what has actually happened. How many worker-days did 2 over 5 of the tunnel take?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    45 more workers must join, making 75 workers for the last 15 days.

    Understand the situation

    The company planned 30 workers for 40 days, but after 25 days the tunnel is less than half dug. The plan was wrong about the size of the job. What has actually happened tells us the true size.

    Find the mathematical structure

    • What stays the same: the amount of digging the tunnel really needs, measured in worker-days.
    • What the evidence says: 30 workers for 25 days dug 2 over 5 of the tunnel. That fixes the size of the whole job.
    • What remains: 3 over 5 of the tunnel, to be dug in the 40 − 25 = 15 days that are left.

    Build the reasoning

    Step 1: the work already done.

    30 × 25 = 750, meaning: worker-days spent so far

    Step 2: the true size of the job. 750 worker-days dug two fifths, so one fifth needs half as much.

    750 ÷ 2 = 375, meaning: worker-days for 1 over 5 of the tunnel

    5 × 375 = 1,875, meaning: worker-days for the whole tunnel

    Step 3: what is left.

    3 × 375 = 1,125, meaning: worker-days for the remaining 3 over 5

    Step 4: workers needed in the last 15 days.

    1,125 ÷ 15 = 75, meaning: workers needed

    75 − 30 = 45, meaning: extra workers

    Check the answer

    • 30 workers for 25 days and 75 workers for 15 days give 750 + 1,125 = 1,875 worker-days, the whole tunnel.
    • Tempting error: trusting the plan. The plan assumed 30 × 40 = 1,200 worker-days in all, leaving 1,200 − 750 = 450, so it seems that 450 ÷ 15 = 30 workers are enough and nobody extra is needed. But 750 worker-days produced only 2 over 5 of the tunnel, so the plan was simply wrong.
    • Direction: the 3 over 5 still to dig is more than the 2 over 5 already dug, and there is less time left (15 days instead of 25), so far more than 30 workers are needed.

    Alternative reasoning

    Compare the two parts directly. 2 over 5 of the tunnel took 30 workers 25 days, so 1 over 5 would take them 12.5 days, and the remaining 3 over 5 would take them 3 × 12.5 = 37.5 days. To do it in 15 days instead, the team must be bigger by the factor:

    37.5 ÷ 15 = 2.5, meaning: times as many workers

    30 × 2.5 = 75, meaning: workers, as before

    General lesson

    When the evidence contradicts a plan, believe the evidence. Use what has actually been achieved to measure the true size of a job, then plan the rest from there.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Reverse reasoning, Error diagnosis.

    Level 7, question 4 of 25 (problem 109 of 140)

    Question 7.4Volunteers who leave early

    Difficulty 3 of 4: Challenge

    24 volunteers, each working 8 hours a day, would finish clearing a park in exactly 20 days. Everyone works at the same steady rate. After 8 days of work, 8 of the volunteers leave.

    1. How many hours a day must the remaining volunteers work to finish on time?
    2. The organisers decide that nobody should work more than 10 hours a day. If the remaining volunteers work 10 hours a day from day 9 onwards, on which day is the work finished, and how many working days later than planned is that?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Measure the job in volunteer-hours. How many are left after the first 8 days?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. 12 hours a day.
    2. The 16 volunteers need 14.4 days instead of 12, so the work ends 4 hours into day 23 instead of at the end of day 20. It is 2.4 working days late (2 days and 4 working hours).

    Understand the situation

    The plan would have worked, but a third of the team leaves after 8 days. The work that remains must now be shared by fewer people. They can make up for it by working longer each day, or by taking more days.

    Find the mathematical structure

    • What stays the same: the work still to be done, measured in volunteer-hours (one volunteer working for one hour).
    • Work supplied per day = (number of volunteers) × (hours per day).
    • Days needed = work left ÷ work supplied per day.

    Build the reasoning

    Step 1: the work left after 8 days. The full team would have needed 20 − 8 = 12 more days.

    24 × 8 × 12 = 2,304, meaning: volunteer-hours of work left

    Step 2: the people who remain.

    24 − 8 = 16, meaning: volunteers

    (a) The 16 volunteers have 12 days to finish.

    2,304 ÷ 12 = 192, meaning: volunteer-hours needed each day

    192 ÷ 16 = 12, meaning: hours a day for each volunteer

    (b) At 10 hours a day, the team supplies:

    16 × 10 = 160, meaning: volunteer-hours each day

    2,304 ÷ 160 = 14.4, meaning: days needed

    14.4 − 12 = 2.4, meaning: days late

    Counting from the start: days 9 to 22 are 14 full days, and 0.4 of a 10-hour day is 4 hours, so the work ends 4 hours into day 23.

    Develop intuition

    Losing a third of the team (8 of 24) means each remaining person must do 3 over 2 times as much each day, and 3 over 2 of 8 hours is 12 hours. Capping the day at 10 hours supplies only 10 over 12 = 5 over 6 of what is needed each day, so the remaining work takes 6 over 5 times as long: 6 over 5 of 12 days is 14.4 days.

    Check the answer

    • (a) 16 volunteers × 12 hours × 12 days: 192 × 12 = 2,304 volunteer-hours, the work that was left.
    • (b) 14 full days supply 14 × 160 = 2,240 volunteer-hours, leaving 64, which the 16 volunteers finish in 64 ÷ 16 = 4 hours on day 23.

    Is 12 hours a day realistic?

    The calculation assumes every volunteer works as fast in the 12th hour as in the 1st. Real people tire, and very long days usually lower the work done per hour. A plan that needs 12-hour days for 12 days in a row is risky, which is probably why the organisers set a 10-hour limit. The model gives the arithmetic; judgement decides whether the arithmetic can be trusted.

    General lesson

    When a team shrinks part-way through, measure the work left in a combined unit such as volunteer-hours. Then any way of finishing (more hours per day, or more days) is a division of that fixed amount.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Changed conditions, Assumption check.

    Level 7, question 5 of 25 (problem 110 of 140)

    Question 7.5Two readings of a tank

    Difficulty 3 of 4: Challenge

    A tap fills a tank at a steady rate, and the tank was empty when the tap was turned on. At 10:00 a.m. the tank is 1 over 4 full, and at 10:40 a.m. it is 2 over 3 full.

    1. At what time will the tank be full?
    2. At what time was the tap turned on?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    What fraction of the tank fills in those 40 minutes? Twelfths make both fractions easy to compare.

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. 11:12 a.m. (b) 9:36 a.m.

    Understand the situation

    We are given two snapshots of the water level, and neither is at the start or at the end. We must find the rate from the change between the snapshots, then work forwards to "full" and backwards to "empty".

    Find the mathematical structure

    • What stays the same: the rate of filling, so equal fractions of the tank take equal times.
    • A convenient unit: 1 over 4 = 3 over 12 and 2 over 3 = 8 over 12, so twelfths of the tank turn every amount into a whole number.

    Build the reasoning

    Step 1: the change between the snapshots.

    2 over 3 − 1 over 4 = 5 over 12, meaning: of the tank filled between 10:00 and 10:40

    Step 2: the bridge of one twelfth. Five twelfths took 40 minutes.

    40 ÷ 5 = 8, meaning: minutes to fill 1 over 12 of the tank

    (a) At 10:40 the tank still needs 1 over 3 = 4 over 12.

    4 × 8 = 32, meaning: minutes after 10:40, which is 11:12 a.m.

    (b) At 10:00 the tank held 1 over 4 = 3 over 12, which took:

    3 × 8 = 24, meaning: minutes, so the tap was turned on at 9:36 a.m.

    Develop intuition

    Draw the tank as a column of 12 equal bands. Each band takes 8 minutes to fill. At 10:00, 3 bands are full; at 10:40, 8 bands are full. Counting bands forwards and backwards gives every time you need.

    Check the answer

    • From 9:36 a.m. to 11:12 a.m. is 96 minutes, and 12 bands at 8 minutes each also make 96 minutes.
    • At 10:00, 24 minutes after the start, 24 over 96 = 1 over 4 of the tank is full, as stated.

    General lesson

    A steady rate can be found from any two snapshots: divide the change in amount by the change in time. Then you can run the clock forwards or backwards.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Reverse reasoning, Direct application.

    Level 7, question 6 of 25 (problem 111 of 140)

    Question 7.6Two pipes, two start times

    Difficulty 3 of 4: Challenge

    Pipe A alone fills a tank in 12 hours, and pipe B alone fills it in 18 hours. Both pipes flow at steady rates, and the tank starts empty.

    1. Pipe A is opened at 6:00 a.m. and pipe B at 8:00 a.m., and then both stay open. At what time is the tank full?
    2. Pipe A is again opened at 6:00 a.m. The owner wants the tank to be full by 12 noon. By what time would pipe B have to be opened? Is that possible if neither pipe may be opened before 6:00 a.m.?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Pretend the tank holds 36 buckets. How many buckets does each pipe pour in one hour?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. 2:00 p.m.
    2. B would have to be opened by 3:00 a.m., three hours before A. If neither pipe may be opened before 6:00 a.m., the plan is impossible: even with both pipes opened at 6:00 a.m., the tank is not full until 1:12 p.m.

    Understand the situation

    The pipes do not start together. In (a), only A works for the first two hours, and after that both work. In (b) we know when the tank must be full and must work backwards to a starting time for B.

    Find the mathematical structure

    • Pretend the tank holds 36 buckets, because 36 is the smallest number that both 12 and 18 divide. A pours 36 ÷ 12 = 3 buckets per hour and B pours 36 ÷ 18 = 2 buckets per hour.
    • When both are open, their rates add: 5 buckets per hour.
    • The water poured in each stage adds up to the whole tank.

    Build the reasoning

    (a) From 6:00 to 8:00 only A is open.

    2 × 3 = 6, meaning: buckets poured by 8:00 a.m.

    36 − 6 = 30, meaning: buckets still needed

    30 ÷ 5 = 6, meaning: hours with both pipes open

    Six hours after 8:00 a.m. is 2:00 p.m.

    (b) From 6:00 a.m. to 12 noon, A pours for 6 hours.

    6 × 3 = 18, meaning: buckets from A by noon

    36 − 18 = 18, meaning: buckets that B must pour by noon

    18 ÷ 2 = 9, meaning: hours that B must run before noon

    Nine hours before noon is 3:00 a.m.

    The best possible without breaking the rule is to open both pipes at 6:00 a.m.:

    36 ÷ 5 = 7.2, meaning: hours with both pipes open from the start

    7.2 hours is 7 hours 12 minutes, so the tank would be full at 1:12 p.m., which is after noon.

    Check the answer

    • (a) A runs 8 hours (6:00 a.m. to 2:00 p.m.) and pours 8 × 3 = 24 buckets; B runs 6 hours and pours 6 × 2 = 12 buckets. 24 + 12 = 36.
    • (b) B from 3:00 a.m. pours 9 × 2 = 18 buckets and A pours 18 buckets by noon, 36 in all.

    Develop intuition

    Working backwards from a deadline can give an answer that breaks a rule of the story, here a start time earlier than the 6:00 a.m. rule allows. An impossible result like this is not a mistake in the arithmetic. It is the mathematics telling you that the plan cannot work.

    General lesson

    For pipes that start at different times, add up the water from each stage. When you work backwards from a deadline, check that the answer obeys every condition of the story.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Reverse reasoning, Changed conditions.

    Level 7, question 7 of 25 (problem 112 of 140)

    Question 7.7The lamp post and the platform

    Difficulty 3 of 4: Challenge

    A metro train moves at a steady speed. Its front passes a lamp post, and 10 seconds later its back passes the same lamp post. The same train crosses a 300 m platform completely in 25 seconds, from the moment its front reaches the start of the platform until its back leaves the far end. Find the train's speed, in kilometres per hour, and its length.

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    In each case, how far does the front of the train travel? Compare the two distances and the two times.

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    The train travels at 72 km/h (20 m/s) and is 200 m long.

    Understand the situation

    We know neither the speed nor the length. Each observation on its own links them, but does not separate them. Putting the two observations together does.

    Find the mathematical structure

    • Passing the post: the front moves forward by exactly one train length. That takes 10 seconds.
    • Crossing the platform: the front moves forward by one train length plus 300 m. That takes 25 seconds.
    • Hidden relationship: the second movement is the first one with an extra 300 m added. So the extra 15 seconds are spent covering exactly the extra 300 m.

    Build the reasoning

    25 − 10 = 15, meaning: extra seconds, caused only by the 300 m platform

    300 ÷ 15 = 20, meaning: metres per second

    20 × 3.6 = 72, meaning: kilometres per hour

    In 10 seconds the train moves one length:

    20 × 10 = 200, meaning: metres, the length of the train

    Develop intuition

    Imagine a dot painted on the front of the train. To pass the post, the dot travels one train length. To cross the platform, the dot travels the whole platform and then one more train length, so that the back of the train can clear the end.

    Check the answer

    Crossing the platform, the front moves 200 + 300 = 500 m, and at 20 m/s that takes 500 ÷ 20 = 25 seconds, as stated.

    Why one observation is not enough

    "The back passes the post 10 seconds after the front" fits a 100 m train at 10 m/s, a 200 m train at 20 m/s, a 300 m train at 30 m/s, and so on. Only the second observation picks out one of these. When a problem has two unknowns, look for two separate facts about them.

    General lesson

    When two situations differ in only one way, the difference between their results is caused by that one change. Subtracting removes the unknown that both share, here the train's length.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Model selection, Reverse reasoning.

    Level 7, question 8 of 25 (problem 113 of 140)

    Question 7.8Climb high, sleep low

    Difficulty 3 of 4: Challenge

    Mountaineers set out from base camp to reach a summit 3,000 m higher. Every day they climb 600 m higher, and every evening they come back down 200 m to sleep at a lower, safer camp. (Real mountaineers often do this: "climb high, sleep low" helps the body get used to the thin air.) They leave base camp on the morning of day 1.

    1. On which day do they first reach the summit?
    2. Sonia says: "They gain 400 m in each day and night, and 3,000 ÷ 400 = 7.5, so they reach the summit on day 8." Explain her mistake.
    3. On which day would they reach a summit 3,100 m above base camp?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Make a table of their height each morning and the highest point they reach each day.

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. Day 7.
    2. Sonia makes them come back down 200 m after every day, including the last one. But on the day they reach the summit, they get there before that evening's descent, so the descent cannot stop them.
    3. Day 8.

    Understand the situation

    Each 24 hours has two parts: a climb of 600 m and a descent of 200 m. The question is when they first reach the summit, and that happens during a day of climbing, before that evening's descent.

    Find the mathematical structure

    • Each full day and night gives a net gain of 600 − 200 = 400 m.
    • But the summit can be reached during the climbing part of a day. So the useful question is: "On which morning are they within 600 m of the summit?"
    • Each morning they are 400 m higher than the morning before.

    Build the reasoning

    DayHeight in the morningHighest point that day
    10 m600 m
    2400 m1,000 m
    3800 m1,400 m
    41,200 m1,800 m
    51,600 m2,200 m
    62,000 m2,600 m
    72,400 m3,000 m

    On the morning of day 7 they are at 2,400 m, and 3,000 − 2,400 = 600 m is exactly one day's climb. They reach the summit at the end of day 7.

    Without the table: they reach the summit on the first day that starts within 600 m of the top.

    3,000 − 600 = 2,400, meaning: metres they must reach by a morning

    2,400 ÷ 400 = 6, meaning: full days and nights needed first

    So the summit is reached on day 6 + 1 = 7.

    (c) For 3,100 m they must start a day at or above 3,100 − 600 = 2,500 m. Their morning heights go up in steps of 400 m: 2,400 m on day 7 is not enough, but 2,800 m on day 8 is. They reach the summit during day 8.

    Check the answer

    For 3,100 m: day 7 ends at 3,000 m, still 100 m short, so that evening they come back down to 2,800 m. On day 8 they climb from 2,800 m to 3,100 m after only 300 m of climbing.

    Why Sonia's method fails

    Sonia's method treats the climb as a steady rise of 400 m per day. That would be right if they lost height a little at a time all through the day. In fact they climb the full 600 m first and come down only in the evening. On the summit day the descent comes too late to matter, so the final stage is a full day's 600 m climb. When a process goes forward and then back, the last stage must be worked out separately.

    General lesson

    When progress goes "forward, then back", the target can be reached before the step back. Work out the last stage separately instead of dividing the whole distance by the net rate.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Error diagnosis, Changed conditions.

    Level 7, question 9 of 25 (problem 114 of 140)

    Question 7.9Cars waiting for the ferry

    Difficulty 3 of 4: Challenge

    50 cars are waiting on one bank of a river to cross on a ferry that carries at most 8 cars at a time. Every crossing, in either direction, takes 10 minutes, including loading and unloading. The ferry starts on the cars' side, takes as many cars as it can on each trip, and comes straight back for more until every car is across.

    1. How long is it from the start of loading the first cars until the last car has been unloaded on the far bank?
    2. How much time would be saved if the ferry could carry 10 cars at a time?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Count crossings, not cars. How many crossings carry cars? How many empty trips back are there? Does the ferry need to come back after the last load?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. 130 minutes (2 hours 10 minutes): 7 crossings with cars and 6 empty crossings back.
    2. With 10 cars at a time it takes 90 minutes, a saving of 40 minutes.

    Understand the situation

    The time is not spent on each car but on each crossing. The ferry takes cars across and then comes back empty to fetch more, except after the very last load.

    Find the mathematical structure

    • Hidden unit: the crossing. Time = 10 minutes × (number of crossings).
    • Loaded crossings: enough trips to carry 50 cars, 8 at a time. A part-full trip still takes a full crossing.
    • Empty crossings: one fewer than the loaded crossings, because nobody needs the ferry to come back after the last car is across.

    Build the reasoning

    (a)

    50 ÷ 8 = 6.25, meaning: loads of cars

    Six trips carry only 48 cars, so a 7th trip is needed for the last 2 cars: 7 loaded crossings.

    7 − 1 = 6, meaning: empty crossings back

    7 + 6 = 13, meaning: crossings in all

    13 × 10 = 130, meaning: minutes

    (b) With 10 cars per trip:

    50 ÷ 10 = 5, meaning: loaded crossings

    5 + 4 = 9, meaning: crossings, including 4 empty ones

    9 × 10 = 90, meaning: minutes

    130 − 90 = 40, meaning: minutes saved

    Develop intuition

    Write the journey as a line of trips: load 1 across, back, load 2 across, back, and so on, ending with load 7 across. The loaded trips are like fence posts and the empty returns are like the gaps between them, so there is one fewer of them.

    Check the answer

    Three tempting wrong answers:

    • 6.25 loads at 20 minutes per round trip gives 125 minutes, but the ferry cannot make a quarter of a trip.
    • 7 round trips at 20 minutes each gives 140 minutes, but that counts a return after the last load that nobody needs.
    • 50 ÷ 8 × 10 = 62.5 minutes forgets the empty returns altogether.

    General lesson

    Find what the time is really spent on. Here it is crossings, which come in whole numbers, and the final return is not needed. Count the pattern carefully, like posts and gaps.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Direct application, Changed conditions.

    Level 7, question 10 of 25 (problem 115 of 140)

    Question 7.10The shrinking cleaning team

    Difficulty 3 of 4: Challenge

    A team of 10 cleaners, all working at the same steady rate, would need exactly 4 days to clean a stadium. They start together, but at the end of each day one cleaner leaves: 10 cleaners work on day 1, 9 on day 2, 8 on day 3, and so on.

    1. On which day is the cleaning finished?
    2. A smaller stadium needs exactly 4 days of work from 6 cleaners. A team of 6 starts it, and again one cleaner leaves at the end of each day. Is this cleaning ever finished? Explain.

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Measure each job in cleaner-days, then keep a running total of the work done, day by day.

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. It is finished at the end of day 5.
    2. No. The job needs 24 cleaner-days, but the shrinking team can supply only 6 + 5 + 4 + 3 + 2 + 1 = 21 cleaner-days before nobody is left. 1 over 8 of the job is never done.

    Understand the situation

    The team gets smaller every day, so the work done each day keeps falling. We cannot divide the job by a fixed daily amount; we must add up the actual work, day by day.

    Find the mathematical structure

    • Fixed: the size of each job, in cleaner-days.
    • Changing: the work done each day, which falls by one cleaner-day every day.
    • The job is finished on the first day when the running total reaches the size of the job, if that ever happens.

    Build the reasoning

    (a)

    10 × 4 = 40, meaning: cleaner-days for the stadium

    DayCleaners at workRunning total of work
    11010
    2919
    3827
    4734
    5640

    The total reaches exactly 40 at the end of day 5.

    (b)

    6 × 4 = 24, meaning: cleaner-days for the smaller stadium

    6 + 5 + 4 + 3 + 2 + 1 = 21, meaning: cleaner-days the shrinking team can ever supply

    24 − 21 = 3, meaning: cleaner-days that are never done

    3 ÷ 24 = 1 over 8, meaning: of the job left undone

    After day 6 there are no cleaners left, so the job can never be finished.

    Check the answer

    A tempting shortcut that fails. "In (a) the team averages 8 cleaners, so it needs 40 ÷ 8 = 5 days." It happens to work, but only because the average over days 1 to 5 is exactly 8. Try the same shortcut on (b): the average of 6, 5, 4, 3, 2 and 1 cleaners is 3.5, and 24 ÷ 3.5 is about 6.9, which suggests the job is finished on day 7. But on day 7 there are no cleaners at all. The average hides the fact that the team runs out.

    Develop intuition

    Picture the work as a staircase of blocks: 10 blocks on day 1, 9 on day 2, and so on, each step one block lower. A staircase that gets lower every day has a largest possible number of blocks, and some jobs need more blocks than that.

    General lesson

    When the rate changes from stage to stage, add up the work stage by stage. Ask whether a job can be finished at all before asking when.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Changed conditions, Counterexample.

    Level 7, question 11 of 25 (problem 116 of 140)

    Question 7.11Leaflets and minutes

    Difficulty 3 of 4: Challenge

    A print shop uses the same machine for every order. An order of 200 leaflets takes 25 minutes, and an order of 500 leaflets takes 55 minutes.

    1. Show that the time is not directly proportional to the number of leaflets.
    2. Find a simple model that fits both orders, and explain what each part of it could mean in the shop.
    3. Use your model to predict how long an order of 800 leaflets takes.
    4. A customer predicts that 800 leaflets will take 4 × 25 = 100 minutes. Explain why this prediction is too long.

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    How much extra time do the extra 300 leaflets take? Then see how much of the 25 minutes is left over.

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. If the time were proportional, 500 leaflets would take 500 over 200 × 25 = 62.5 minutes, not 55.
    2. Time = 5 minutes of setting up + 1 minute for every 10 leaflets. The 5 minutes could be spent preparing the machine, loading the design and paper, and making a test print; after that the machine prints 10 leaflets per minute.
    3. 85 minutes.
    4. The customer multiplies the 5 minutes of setting up by 4 as well, but setting up happens only once per order. That adds 15 minutes too many.

    Understand the situation

    The two orders do not fit direct proportion. The data are telling us that part of the time does not grow with the number of leaflets.

    Find the mathematical structure

    • Suppose the time has two parts: a fixed part that every order needs, and a part proportional to the number of leaflets.
    • Comparing the two orders removes the fixed part, because each order includes it exactly once. The difference between them then gives the printing rate.

    Build the reasoning

    (a)

    25 ÷ 200 = 0.125, meaning: minutes per leaflet for the small order

    55 ÷ 500 = 0.11, meaning: minutes per leaflet for the large order

    The time per leaflet is not the same, so the time is not directly proportional to the number of leaflets.

    (b) Compare the two orders:

    500 − 200 = 300, meaning: extra leaflets

    55 − 25 = 30, meaning: extra minutes

    300 ÷ 30 = 10, meaning: leaflets printed per minute

    At 10 leaflets per minute, printing 200 leaflets takes 20 minutes, so:

    25 − 200 ÷ 10 = 5, meaning: minutes of fixed setting up

    (c)

    5 + 800 ÷ 10 = 85, meaning: minutes for 800 leaflets

    Check the answer

    The model gives 5 + 200 ÷ 10 = 25 minutes and 5 + 500 ÷ 10 = 55 minutes, matching both orders.

    An honest warning

    A fixed part plus a proportional part can always be made to fit two orders, so these two orders do not prove that the model is right. A third order would test it: the model predicts that 1,000 leaflets take 105 minutes. A good modeller says what the model assumes and how it could be checked.

    General lesson

    When data do not fit proportion, try "fixed part + proportional part". The difference between two cases removes the fixed part and reveals the rate. Then scale only the part that really grows.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Model selection, Error diagnosis.

    Level 7, question 12 of 25 (problem 117 of 140)

    Question 7.12With and against the wind

    Difficulty 3 of 4: Challenge

    Meena cycles from her house to the market with a steady wind behind her, and the trip takes 20 minutes. She cycles straight back along the same road against the same wind, and that takes 30 minutes. She pedals equally hard both ways, and the wind adds to her speed on the way there exactly as much as it takes away on the way back.

    1. Can you work out the distance from her house to the market? Explain what you can and cannot work out from this information.
    2. You are now told that with no wind at all, Meena cycles at 15 km/h. Find the distance, and the amount by which the wind changes her speed.

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    The road is the same both ways. What do the two times tell you about her two speeds?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. No, the distance cannot be determined. We can find only comparisons: her speed with the wind is 3 over 2 times her speed against it, the wind changes her speed by 1 over 5 of her no-wind speed, and with no wind each trip would take 24 minutes.
    2. The distance is 6 km, and the wind changes her speed by 3 km/h (18 km/h with the wind, 12 km/h against it).

    Understand the situation

    We know two times but no speed and no distance. Because the distance is the same both ways, the times can tell us how the two speeds compare, but not how big they are.

    Find the mathematical structure

    • For a fixed distance, speed and time are in inverse proportion. Times of 20 and 30 minutes mean the speeds are in the ratio 30 : 20 = 3 : 2.
    • Call the speed with the wind 3 parts and the speed against it 2 parts. Her no-wind speed is exactly halfway between them, at 2.5 parts, and the wind's effect is 0.5 of a part.
    • Nothing yet tells us how big one part is. That is the missing quantity.

    Build the reasoning

    (a) Many different journeys fit the facts. For example:

    One partWith windAgainst windDistance (20 min with the wind)Time back
    4 km/h12 km/h8 km/h4 km30 min
    6 km/h18 km/h12 km/h6 km30 min
    8 km/h24 km/h16 km/h8 km30 min

    Every row matches both times but gives a different distance, so the distance cannot be determined. What every row shares is the ratio: the wind's effect (0.5 of a part) is always 1 over 5 of the no-wind speed (2.5 parts). Every row also gives the same time for a trip with no wind, because the same distance is covered at 2.5 parts instead of 3:

    20 × 3 ÷ 2.5 = 24, meaning: minutes each way with no wind

    (b) The no-wind speed is 2.5 parts, and it is 15 km/h.

    15 ÷ 2.5 = 6, meaning: km/h in one part

    3 × 6 = 18, meaning: km/h with the wind

    2 × 6 = 12, meaning: km/h against the wind

    18 − 15 = 3, meaning: km/h added or taken away by the wind

    18 × 20 over 60 = 6, meaning: kilometres to the market

    Check the answer

    Going back at 12 km/h, 6 km takes 6 over 12 = 1 over 2 hour, which is 30 minutes, as stated.

    General lesson

    Two times for the same distance give only the ratio of the speeds. To turn a ratio into actual speeds or distances, you need one actual measurement. Saying exactly what is missing is part of a complete answer.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Insufficient information, Missing quantity.

    Level 7, question 13 of 25 (problem 118 of 140)

    Question 7.13Three pairs of gardeners

    Difficulty 3 of 4: Challenge

    Three gardeners each weed at their own steady rate. Asha and Bilal together can weed a large garden in 10 days. Bilal and Chetan together take 12 days, and Asha and Chetan together take 15 days.

    1. How long would all three take, working together?
    2. How long would each of them take, working alone?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Pretend the garden has 120 beds. How many beds does each pair weed in a day? What happens if you add the three pairs together?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. 8 days.
    2. Asha: 24 days. Bilal: 17 and 1 over 7 days (about 17.14 days). Chetan: 40 days.

    Understand the situation

    We are never told what one gardener does alone, only what pairs do. Each gardener belongs to exactly two of the three pairs. That is the hidden relationship to use.

    Find the mathematical structure

    • Rates add for people working at the same time. If we add the three pairs, every gardener is counted twice, so we will have to halve.
    • Pretend the garden has 120 beds. 120 is divisible by 10, 12 and 15, and it is twice their smallest common multiple, 60, so the rates stay whole numbers even after halving.

    Build the reasoning

    The pairs' rates:

    120 ÷ 10 = 12, meaning: beds per day for Asha and Bilal

    120 ÷ 12 = 10, meaning: beds per day for Bilal and Chetan

    120 ÷ 15 = 8, meaning: beds per day for Asha and Chetan

    Add the pairs. This counts each gardener twice:

    12 + 10 + 8 = 30, meaning: beds per day for two of each gardener

    30 ÷ 2 = 15, meaning: beds per day for the three gardeners together

    (a)

    120 ÷ 15 = 8, meaning: days for all three

    (b) Take a pair away from the team of three to find the person who is left.

    15 − 10 = 5, meaning: beds per day for Asha (all three, minus Bilal and Chetan)

    15 − 8 = 7, meaning: beds per day for Bilal

    15 − 12 = 3, meaning: beds per day for Chetan

    120 ÷ 5 = 24, meaning: days for Asha alone

    120 ÷ 7 = 17 and 1 over 7, meaning: days for Bilal alone

    120 ÷ 3 = 40, meaning: days for Chetan alone

    Check the answer

    • Asha + Bilal = 5 + 7 = 12 beds per day; Bilal + Chetan = 7 + 3 = 10; Asha + Chetan = 5 + 3 = 8. All three pairs match.
    • All three together (8 days) are faster than the fastest pair (10 days), as they must be.

    Alternative reasoning

    With fractions of the garden. The pairs weed 1 over 10, 1 over 12 and 1 over 15 of the garden each day.

    1 over 10 + 1 over 12 + 1 over 15 = 1 over 4, meaning: of the garden per day, for two of each gardener

    1 over 4 ÷ 2 = 1 over 8, meaning: of the garden per day for all three, so 8 days

    1 over 8 − 1 over 12 = 1 over 24, meaning: of the garden per day for Asha (all three, minus Bilal and Chetan), so 24 days

    The 120-bed garden gives the same results while keeping every rate a whole number.

    General lesson

    When information comes in overlapping groups, add the groups and notice how many times each member has been counted. Then subtract to isolate each member.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Alternative solution, Reverse reasoning.

    Level 7, question 14 of 25 (problem 119 of 140)

    Question 7.14Hire now or hire later?

    Difficulty 3 of 4: Challenge

    A job needs exactly 600 worker-days of work (for example, 20 workers for 30 days), and it must be finished within 25 days. 20 workers are already on the job. The manager can hire extra workers, who work at the same rate as everyone else, in one of two ways:

    • Plan P: 5 extra workers from day 1.
    • Plan Q: 10 extra workers from day 11, after the first 10 days.

    Everyone keeps working until the job is finished.

    1. How long does each plan take? Do both meet the deadline?
    2. Every worker is paid the same amount for a day's work. Suppose workers are paid for exactly the time they work, so that a third of a day's work earns a third of a day's pay. Which plan costs less in wages?
    3. Explain your answer to (b) without calculating.
    4. Now suppose that anyone who works for any part of a day must be paid for the whole day. Which plan costs less now?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    For Plan Q, how much of the job is done in the first 10 days? For (b), what exactly does a wage bill pay for?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. Plan P takes 24 days; Plan Q takes 23 and 1 over 3 days (about 23.33 days). Both meet the deadline, and Plan Q finishes first.
    2. Neither. Both plans pay for exactly 600 worker-days.
    3. The job needs 600 worker-days however the work is scheduled, and the wages pay for exactly those worker-days.
    4. Plan P: it pays for 600 worker-days, while Plan Q pays for 620.

    Understand the situation

    Two plans share out the same fixed job in different ways. We compare when they finish and what they cost.

    Find the mathematical structure

    • Fixed: the job, 600 worker-days.
    • Plan P has one stage (25 workers). Plan Q has two stages (20 workers, then 30 workers).
    • In (b), the wage bill matches the worker-days actually worked. In (d), any part of a day is paid as a whole day.

    Build the reasoning

    (a) Plan P.

    600 ÷ 25 = 24, meaning: days with 25 workers

    Plan Q.

    20 × 10 = 200, meaning: worker-days done in the first 10 days

    600 − 200 = 400, meaning: worker-days left

    400 ÷ 30 = 13 and 1 over 3, meaning: days with 30 workers

    10 + 13 and 1 over 3 = 23 and 1 over 3, meaning: days in all

    Plan Q finishes 2 over 3 of a day sooner, a third of the way through day 24.

    (b) Plan P pays 25 workers for 24 days, which is 600 worker-days. Plan Q pays for 200 worker-days in its first stage and 30 × 13 and 1 over 3 = 400 worker-days in its second: 600 again.

    (d) Plan P is unchanged, because it ends exactly at the end of day 24: 600 worker-days. In Plan Q the second stage runs into day 24, so its 30 workers are paid for 14 whole days:

    200 + 30 × 14 = 620, meaning: worker-days paid in Plan Q

    Why (b) needs no calculation

    Every worker-day of work is paid for once, and the job contains 600 worker-days of work whatever the plan. Hiring earlier or later changes when the work is done, not how much work there is. So if pay matches work exactly, every plan that finishes the job costs the same. Only an extra rule, such as paying for whole days, can make one plan more expensive than another.

    Check the answer

    • Plan Q: 200 + 400 = 600 worker-days, exactly the job.
    • In (d), Plan Q pays each of its 30 workers for 2 over 3 of a day that nobody works. That is 30 × 2 over 3, which is 20 worker-days, exactly the difference between 620 and 600.

    General lesson

    A fixed amount of work costs a fixed amount of wages, however it is scheduled, as long as pay matches work exactly. Whole-number rules, such as paying for whole days, are what make the schedule matter.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Comparison, Changed conditions.

    Level 7, question 15 of 25 (problem 120 of 140)

    Question 7.15Sharing a taxi fairly

    Difficulty 3 of 4: Challenge

    Three friends share a taxi from the station. The fare is ₹30 for every kilometre the taxi travels, with no fixed charge. Ira gets out after 10 km, Jai after 20 km, and Kiran rides the whole 30 km, so the meter shows ₹900 at the end.

    Kiran suggests: "Let's pay in proportion to the distance each of us rode, 10 : 20 : 30."

    Ira suggests: "Let's split the cost of each stretch of the ride equally among the people who were in the taxi during that stretch."

    1. Work out what each friend pays under each suggestion, and check that each suggestion collects exactly ₹900.
    2. Which suggestion do you think is fairer? Give a reason based on what each friend actually used.

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Split the ride into three 10 km stretches. Who is in the taxi during each stretch, and what does each stretch cost?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. Each friend pays:
    FriendKiran's suggestionIra's suggestion
    Ira₹150₹100
    Jai₹300₹250
    Kiran₹450₹550
    Total₹900₹900
    1. Many people find Ira's suggestion fairer, because the meter charges for kilometres travelled by the taxi, not for each passenger. While three people share the taxi they share its cost, and for the last 10 km Kiran has the taxi to himself, so he pays for all of that stretch. A clearly argued case for Kiran's suggestion (it is simpler, and everyone pays the same amount for each kilometre ridden) is also a reasonable answer.

    Understand the situation

    The fare depends only on how far the taxi travels. Three people use the taxi for different distances, and they must share one bill. The question does not give a rule for sharing, so part of the task is to judge which model matches the situation.

    Find the mathematical structure

    • Kiran's model: share the cost in proportion to person-kilometres (one person riding one kilometre). Everyone pays the same price per kilometre ridden.
    • Ira's model: share the cost stretch by stretch. The cost of each stretch is split among the people in the taxi at the time.
    • Both models collect the whole fare. They differ in what they treat as the thing being paid for.

    Build the reasoning

    Kiran's suggestion. Together the friends ride 10 + 20 + 30 = 60 person-kilometres.

    900 ÷ 60 = 15, meaning: rupees per person-kilometre

    10 × 15 = 150, meaning: rupees for Ira

    20 × 15 = 300, meaning: rupees for Jai

    30 × 15 = 450, meaning: rupees for Kiran

    Ira's suggestion. Each 10 km stretch costs 10 × ₹30 = ₹300.

    300 ÷ 3 = 100, meaning: rupees each for the first stretch (all three in the taxi)

    300 ÷ 2 = 150, meaning: rupees each for the second stretch (Jai and Kiran)

    So Ira pays ₹100, Jai pays 100 + 150 = ₹250, and Kiran pays 100 + 150 + 300 = ₹550, because he rides the last stretch alone.

    100 + 250 + 550 = 900, meaning: rupees collected

    Weighing the two models

    • Under Kiran's plan, Kiran pays only ₹450 for a ride that would cost ₹900 on his own. For the last 10 km he is the only passenger, yet the other two help to pay for a stretch they were not even in the taxi for.
    • Under Ira's plan, each person pays only for stretches when they were in the taxi, and shares each stretch with whoever was there.
    • Under both plans, everyone pays less than a taxi of their own would cost (₹300 for Ira, ₹600 for Jai, ₹900 for Kiran), so sharing helps everybody either way.

    Notice that each friend suggested the plan that charges them less. Mathematics cannot decide what is "fair", but it can show exactly what each rule charges for, so the argument can be about the rule instead of the arithmetic.

    General lesson

    Before sharing a cost, decide what the cost is really for, here kilometres travelled by the taxi. Different models of the same situation can all add up correctly yet share the cost differently, so the choice of model matters.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Model selection, Comparison.

    Level 7, question 16 of 25 (problem 121 of 140)

    Question 7.16The clock that runs fast

    Difficulty 3 of 4: Challenge

    A wall clock runs fast: whenever 60 real minutes pass, its hands move as if 63 minutes had passed. It is set to the correct time at 8:00 a.m.

    1. That afternoon, the clock shows 3:00. What is the correct time?
    2. Ravi answers (a) like this: "From 8:00 to 3:00 is 7 hours, so the clock has gained 7 × 3 = 21 minutes, and the correct time is 2:39 p.m." Explain why Ravi is slightly wrong.
    3. If nobody adjusts the clock, when will it next show exactly the correct time? (Its face does not show a.m. or p.m.)

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Ravi's 7 hours are hours on the clock, not real hours. How many real minutes match 63 clock-minutes?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. 2:40 p.m.
    2. Ravi assumes that 7 real hours have passed. In fact the clock's 7 hours took only 6 hours 40 minutes of real time, and in that time the clock gains 20 minutes, not 21.
    3. At 8:00 a.m., exactly 10 days later, when the clock has gained a full 12 hours.

    Understand the situation

    There are two kinds of minutes here: real minutes and clock-minutes (the amount the hands move). The clock squeezes 63 clock-minutes into every 60 real minutes. The clock's reading tells us how many clock-minutes have passed, but we want the real time.

    Find the mathematical structure

    • Constant: the exchange rate between the two kinds of minutes, 63 clock-minutes for every 60 real minutes.
    • So clock-minutes and real minutes are in direct proportion, but we must cross the bridge in the right direction: from clock-minutes to real minutes.

    Build the reasoning

    (a) From 8:00 a.m. to 3:00 p.m. on the clock is 7 clock-hours.

    7 × 60 = 420, meaning: clock-minutes shown

    420 ÷ 63 = 20 over 3, meaning: lots of 63 clock-minutes

    20 over 3 × 60 = 400, meaning: real minutes that have passed

    400 real minutes is 6 hours 40 minutes, and 6 hours 40 minutes after 8:00 a.m. is 2:40 p.m.

    (b) In 6 hours 40 minutes, which is 20 over 3 real hours, the clock gains 3 minutes in each real hour:

    20 over 3 × 3 = 20, meaning: minutes gained

    Adding 20 minutes to 2:40 p.m. gives 3:00 p.m., which matches the clock. Ravi's 21 minutes would be right only if 7 real hours had passed.

    (c) A 12-hour clock face looks correct again once the clock has gained exactly 12 hours.

    12 × 60 = 720, meaning: minutes the clock must gain

    720 ÷ 3 = 240, meaning: real hours needed

    240 ÷ 24 = 10, meaning: days

    After exactly 10 days the real time is 8:00 a.m. again. The clock is then exactly 12 hours ahead, so its hands also point to 8:00.

    Develop intuition

    Think of the clock as a runner who is a little faster than real time. Ravi measures the race by how far the runner went, which is slightly too far. For the real time, we must ask how long the runner took.

    Check the answer

    • Direction: the clock runs fast, so the correct time must be earlier than 3:00 p.m. 2:40 p.m. is earlier.
    • Size: the clock gains about 3 minutes in each of about 7 hours, so the error should be about 20 minutes. It is exactly 20.

    General lesson

    When two clocks, scales or units disagree, write down the exchange rate between them and convert in the right direction. Here "3 minutes per hour" means per real hour, because that is how the rate was defined.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Error diagnosis, Transfer.

    Level 7, question 17 of 25 (problem 122 of 140)

    Question 7.17One pipe, then the other

    Difficulty 4 of 4: Olympiad

    With pipes A and B both open, an empty tank fills in 12 minutes. One day the tank is empty again, and pipe A is opened on its own for 8 minutes and then closed. Pipe B is then opened on its own, and the tank becomes full after B has run for 18 minutes. Each pipe always flows at its own steady rate. How long would each pipe take to fill the empty tank on its own?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Can you rewrite "A for 8 minutes, then B for 18 minutes" as "both together for some minutes, then B alone for the rest"?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    Pipe A alone takes 20 minutes, and pipe B alone takes 30 minutes.

    Understand the situation

    We know what the two pipes do together, and what a mixture of A on its own and B on its own does. We need each pipe's own rate.

    Find the mathematical structure

    • The order in which the pipes run does not change how much water they deliver. So "A for 8 minutes, then B for 18 minutes" delivers the same water as "A and B together for 8 minutes, then B alone for 10 more minutes".
    • We know exactly what 8 minutes together delivers, because together the pipes fill the tank in 12 minutes.

    Build the reasoning

    Step 1: regroup the running times. Split B's 18 minutes into 8 + 10, and pair 8 minutes of B with the 8 minutes of A.

    18 − 8 = 10, meaning: minutes of B left over after pairing
