    Step 2: what the paired minutes delivered. Together, the pipes fill 1 over 12 of the tank each minute.

    8 × 1 over 12 = 2 over 3, meaning: of the tank from the 8 paired minutes

    Step 3: what B's extra 10 minutes delivered.

    1 − 2 over 3 = 1 over 3, meaning: of the tank from B alone in 10 minutes

    10 × 3 = 30, meaning: minutes for B to fill the whole tank alone

    Step 4: pipe A. A's rate is the combined rate minus B's rate.

    1 over 12 − 1 over 30 = 1 over 20, meaning: of the tank per minute from A

    So A alone takes 20 minutes.

    Check the answer

    • A for 8 minutes fills 8 over 20 = 2 over 5 of the tank, and B for 18 minutes fills 18 over 30 = 3 over 5. Together, 2 over 5 + 3 over 5 = 1 whole tank.
    • Together the pipes fill 1 over 20 + 1 over 30 = 3 over 60 + 2 over 60 = 5 over 60 = 1 over 12 of the tank per minute, so 12 minutes, as given.

    Alternative reasoning

    A convenient tank size. Pretend the tank holds 60 buckets, so together the pipes pour 60 ÷ 12 = 5 buckets per minute. If A pours some number of buckets per minute, B pours the rest of the 5. Testing whole numbers: if A pours 3 buckets per minute, then B pours 2, and 8 minutes of A plus 18 minutes of B give 24 + 36 = 60 buckets, exactly a full tank. So A takes 60 ÷ 3 = 20 minutes and B takes 60 ÷ 2 = 30 minutes. The regrouping method finds this without any guessing.

    General lesson

    Amounts of work done at different times can be regrouped. Pair up equal times of A and B to use what you know about them together; the leftover time shows one agent on its own.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Alternative solution, Reverse reasoning.

    Level 7, question 18 of 25 (problem 123 of 140)

    Question 7.18How full was the tank?

    Difficulty 4 of 4: Olympiad

    A tank is partly full of water. Three separate experiments are carried out, each starting from this same level:

    • With only inlet pipe A open, the tank becomes full in 3 hours.
    • With only drain B open, the tank becomes empty in 2 hours.
    • With both A and B open, the tank becomes empty in 6 hours.

    A and B always work at their own steady rates.

    1. What fraction of the tank was full at the start?
    2. How long would pipe A take to fill the tank from empty, and how long would drain B take to empty a full tank?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Compare the second and third experiments. In 6 hours, how much water would B remove on its own? So how much must A have added in those 6 hours?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. The tank was half full (1 over 2).
    2. A fills an empty tank in 6 hours, and B empties a full tank in 4 hours.

    Understand the situation

    No amount of water is given, only times. We must find the starting amount from the way the three experiments are related.

    Find the mathematical structure

    Call the water in the tank at the start one load. We measure everything in loads first, and connect loads to the whole tank at the end.

    • B removes one load in 2 hours.
    • A, in 3 hours, fills exactly the empty space above the starting level.
    • A and B together remove one load in 6 hours.

    Build the reasoning

    Step 1: what B does in 6 hours. B removes one load every 2 hours.

    6 ÷ 2 = 3, meaning: loads that B removes in 6 hours

    Step 2: what A adds in those 6 hours. With both open, only one load disappears in 6 hours, so A must have put back the other two.

    3 − 1 = 2, meaning: loads added by A in 6 hours

    2 ÷ 6 × 3 = 1, meaning: load added by A in 3 hours

    Step 3: connect loads to the tank. In the first experiment A, in 3 hours, fills the empty space above the starting level. We have just found that A adds one load in 3 hours. So the empty space is also one load: the water and the empty space are equal.

    1 + 1 = 2, meaning: loads in the full tank, so the tank started half full

    (b) A adds one load, which is half the tank, in 3 hours.

    2 × 3 = 6, meaning: hours for A to fill an empty tank

    B removes half the tank in 2 hours.

    2 × 2 = 4, meaning: hours for B to empty a full tank

    Check the answer

    In fractions of the tank, A adds 1 over 6 per hour and B removes 1 over 4 per hour.

    • A alone from half full: 1 over 2 ÷ 1 over 6 = 3 hours to fill the tank.
    • B alone from half full: 1 over 2 ÷ 1 over 4 = 2 hours to empty it.
    • Both together: the level falls by 1 over 4 − 1 over 6 = 1 over 12 of the tank each hour, so half a tank empties in 1 over 2 ÷ 1 over 12 = 6 hours.

    All three experiments match.

    Develop intuition

    The key step is choosing the unit. Measuring in "loads" (the starting water) instead of "tanks" makes the second and third clues simple, and comparing them shows what A does by itself.

    General lesson

    When no amount is given, choose a convenient unknown amount as your unit, express every clue in that unit, and let the clues reveal how it compares with the whole.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Reverse reasoning, Missing quantity.

    Level 7, question 19 of 25 (problem 124 of 140)

    Question 7.19Two candles

    Difficulty 4 of 4: Olympiad

    Two new candles have the same length but different thicknesses. The thin one would burn away completely in 3 hours, and the thick one in 4 hours, each getting shorter at its own steady rate. They are lit at the same moment.

    1. After how long is the thick candle exactly twice as long as the thin one?
    2. The length of the candles is not given. Does that matter? Explain.

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Choose a convenient length, such as 12 cm. How many centimetres does each candle lose per hour? Then try a different length.

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. After 2 and 2 over 5 hours (2.4 hours), which is 2 hours 24 minutes.
    2. No. Each hour, each candle loses the same fraction of its starting length (1 over 3 for the thin one, 1 over 4 for the thick one), so at any moment the two remaining lengths are fixed fractions of the same starting length. When we compare them, the starting length cancels out, so any length gives the same answer.

    Understand the situation

    The thin candle burns away faster. At the start the candles are equal; after 3 hours the thin one is gone while a quarter of the thick one is left. Somewhere in between, the thick candle must be exactly twice as long as the thin one.

    Find the mathematical structure

    • Each candle shrinks at a steady rate, so the length burned is proportional to the time. The length remaining is not proportional to the time: it is the starting length minus the length burned.
    • Choose a length that makes both rates whole numbers: 12 cm, because 12 is divisible by both 3 and 4.

    Build the reasoning

    With 12 cm candles, the thin candle loses 12 ÷ 3 = 4 cm per hour and the thick candle loses 12 ÷ 4 = 3 cm per hour.

    TimeThin candleThick candleThick ÷ thin
    0 h12 cm12 cm1
    1 h8 cm9 cm9 over 8
    2 h4 cm6 cm3 over 2
    3 h0 cm3 cmthe thin one is gone

    The answer lies between 2 and 3 hours. At 2 hours the thick candle (6 cm) is 2 cm short of twice the thin one (2 × 4 = 8 cm). After that, each hour the thick candle loses 3 cm, while "twice the thin candle" loses 2 × 4 = 8 cm. So the 2 cm shortfall closes by 8 − 3 = 5 cm per hour.

    2 ÷ 5 = 0.4, meaning: hours after the 2-hour mark

    2 + 0.4 = 2.4, meaning: hours, which is 2 hours 24 minutes

    Check the answer

    At 2.4 hours the thin candle is 12 − 4 × 2.4 = 2.4 cm long and the thick candle is 12 − 3 × 2.4 = 4.8 cm long, and 4.8 is exactly twice 2.4.

    Why the length does not matter

    Try 24 cm candles instead. Every length in the table doubles: the thin candle loses 8 cm per hour and the thick one 6 cm per hour. At 2.4 hours they are 4.8 cm and 9.6 cm long, and 9.6 is still exactly twice 4.8. Doubling, tripling or halving the starting length multiplies both remaining lengths by the same number, so the comparison between them does not change.

    In general, after any time the thin candle has the fraction 1 − (time ÷ 3) of its length left, and the thick one has 1 − (time ÷ 4) of the same length left. The question compares these two fractions, so the actual length plays no part.

    General lesson

    A quantity that seems to be missing may cancel out. Test this by trying one convenient value and then another. If the answer does not change, the quantity was never needed, and you can often explain why.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Missing quantity, Proof and explanation.

    Level 7, question 20 of 25 (problem 125 of 140)

    Question 7.20Eight loaves, eight coins

    Difficulty 4 of 4: Olympiad

    Two travellers stop for a meal. Arun has 5 loaves of bread and Bela has 3 loaves, all the same size. A hungry stranger asks to join them. The three of them share all 8 loaves equally, and the stranger then pays 8 coins for the bread he ate.

    Arun says, "I brought 5 loaves and you brought 3, so I get 5 coins and you get 3."

    How should the 8 coins be shared, so that each traveller is paid for the bread that he or she gave to the stranger? Is Arun's split fair?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    How much bread does each person eat? So how much of their own bread does each traveller give to the stranger?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    Arun should get 7 coins and Bela 1 coin. Arun's 5 : 3 split is not fair: it pays the travellers in proportion to the bread they brought, including the bread they ate themselves. Surprisingly, it is unfair to Arun himself.

    Understand the situation

    The stranger pays only for the bread he ate, not for all 8 loaves. Arun and Bela also ate some of their own bread. Each traveller should be paid only for the part of their bread that went to the stranger.

    Find the mathematical structure

    • Constant: the price the stranger pays for each loaf he ate.
    • Hidden quantity: how much bread each traveller gave to the stranger, which is their own loaves minus what they ate themselves.
    • Payment is directly proportional to the bread given, not to the bread brought.

    Build the reasoning

    Step 1: everyone's share.

    8 ÷ 3 = 8 over 3, meaning: loaves eaten by each person, which is 2 and 2 over 3 loaves

    Step 2: what each traveller gave.

    5 − 8 over 3 = 7 over 3, meaning: loaves Arun gave to the stranger

    3 − 8 over 3 = 1 over 3, meaning: loaf Bela gave to the stranger

    7 over 3 + 1 over 3 = 8 over 3, meaning: loaves, exactly what the stranger ate

    Step 3: the price of a loaf.

    8 ÷ 8 over 3 = 3, meaning: coins for each loaf the stranger ate

    Step 4: payment.

    7 over 3 × 3 = 7, meaning: coins for Arun

    1 over 3 × 3 = 1, meaning: coin for Bela

    Develop intuition

    Cut every loaf into thirds. There are 24 thirds, and each person eats 8 of them. Arun brought 15 thirds, ate 8 and gave 7 to the stranger. Bela brought 9 thirds, ate 8 and gave only 1. The stranger's 8 thirds came 7 from Arun and 1 from Bela, so the coins split 7 and 1.

    Check the answer

    • 7 + 1 = 8 coins, all paid out.
    • Under Arun's split, Bela would receive 3 coins, the price of a whole loaf, although she gave the stranger only a third of a loaf. Arun would receive 5 coins for the 2 and 1 over 3 loaves he gave, when they are worth 7 coins.

    General lesson

    In fair-sharing problems, find exactly what each person contributed to the thing being paid for. Here it is the bread given away, not the bread brought, and that changes 5 : 3 into 7 : 1.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Model selection, Error diagnosis.

    Level 7, question 21 of 25 (problem 126 of 140)

    Question 7.21After they meet

    Difficulty 4 of 4: Olympiad

    Asha sets off from town P towards town Q, and at the same moment Bilal sets off from Q towards P along the same road. Each walks at a steady speed. They meet, pass each other and carry on. After the meeting, Asha takes 4 more hours to reach Q, and Bilal takes 9 more hours to reach P.

    1. How long after setting off did they meet?
    2. Who walks faster, and how many times as fast?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Call the meeting point M. Asha walks from M to Q in 4 hours, but Bilal walked that same stretch before the meeting. How long did that take him?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. They met 6 hours after setting off.
    2. Asha walks faster, 3 over 2 times as fast as Bilal (1.5 times as fast).

    Understand the situation

    Neither a speed nor a distance is given, only times. The hidden relationship is that each stretch of road is walked by both people, at different speeds.

    Find the mathematical structure

    Call the meeting point M, and call the time from setting off until the meeting the meeting time.

    • Stretch MQ: Bilal walked it before the meeting, taking the meeting time. Asha walks it after the meeting, taking 4 hours.
    • Stretch PM: Asha walked it before the meeting, taking the meeting time. Bilal walks it after the meeting, taking 9 hours.
    • On the same stretch, speed and time are in inverse proportion, so each stretch tells us how Asha's speed compares with Bilal's.

    Build the reasoning

    From stretch MQ: Asha needs 4 hours where Bilal needs the meeting time, so Asha's speed ÷ Bilal's speed = (meeting time) ÷ 4.

    From stretch PM: Asha needs the meeting time where Bilal needs 9 hours, so Asha's speed ÷ Bilal's speed = 9 ÷ (meeting time).

    Both describe the same comparison, so (meeting time) ÷ 4 = 9 ÷ (meeting time). Multiplying both sides by 4 and by the meeting time gives: (meeting time) × (meeting time) = 4 × 9 = 36.

    6 × 6 = 36, meaning: so the meeting time is 6 hours

    (b)

    6 ÷ 4 = 3 over 2, meaning: Asha's speed compared with Bilal's

    As a check, the other stretch gives 9 ÷ 6, which is also 3 over 2.

    Check the answer

    Try actual speeds: Bilal at 2 km/h and Asha at 3 km/h. In 6 hours Asha walks 18 km and Bilal 12 km, so the road is 30 km long and M is 18 km from P. After the meeting, Asha has 12 km left at 3 km/h, which takes 4 hours. Bilal has 18 km left at 2 km/h, which takes 9 hours. Both match.

    Develop intuition

    The meeting time sits "in the middle" of 4 hours and 9 hours in a multiplying sense: 4 is to 6 as 6 is to 9, since each step multiplies by 3 over 2. To find such a middle number, ask which number multiplied by itself gives 4 × 9.

    General lesson

    When the same distance is covered by two people, the ratio of their times is the ratio of their speeds turned upside down. Writing the same ratio in two different ways is a powerful way to create an equation.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Proof and explanation, Reverse reasoning.

    Level 7, question 22 of 25 (problem 127 of 140)

    Question 7.22Meeting twice on the road

    Difficulty 4 of 4: Olympiad

    Two walkers start at the same moment from the two ends, A and B, of a straight road 12 km long, and walk towards each other, each at a steady speed. They first meet 5 km from A. Each walker carries on to the far end of the road, turns round immediately, and walks back. Where do they meet for the second time?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    At the first meeting, how many road-lengths have the two walkers covered between them? How many have they covered by the second meeting?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    They meet for the second time 3 km from B (that is, 9 km from A).

    Understand the situation

    Neither speed is given, and the second meeting happens after both walkers have turned round. It looks as though we need the speeds. The key is to measure distances in road-lengths.

    Find the mathematical structure

    • First meeting: together the walkers have covered exactly one road-length (12 km), and the walker from A has covered 5 km of it.
    • Second meeting: each walker has reached the far end (two road-lengths between them) and then they have walked towards each other until they meet (one more road-length between them). Together they have covered three road-lengths.
    • Their speeds are steady, so the distance each walker covers is proportional to the time. Three times the combined distance means three times the time, so each walker has walked three times their first-meeting distance.

    Build the reasoning

    3 × 12 = 36, meaning: kilometres covered by the two walkers together by the second meeting

    3 × 5 = 15, meaning: kilometres walked by the walker from A

    The walker from A walks 12 km to B, then 15 − 12 = 3 km back towards A. So the second meeting is 3 km from B.

    Check the answer

    Try actual speeds that fit the first meeting, such as 5 km/h for the walker from A and 7 km/h for the walker from B. Together they close the 12 km gap in 12 ÷ 12 = 1 hour, meeting 5 km from A. The walker from B reaches A after 12 over 7 hours (about 1.7 hours) and turns; the walker from A reaches B after 12 over 5 hours (2.4 hours) and turns. Together they walk 12 km each hour, so they have covered 36 km after 3 hours. By then the walker from A has gone 15 km (3 km back from B) and the walker from B has gone 21 km (9 km back from A). Both are at the same point, 3 km from B.

    When does the "three road-lengths" idea work?

    The argument needs both walkers to turn round before they meet again, so that the second meeting is head-on. Here both turns happen before the 3-hour mark (at about 1.7 hours and 2.4 hours), so it is safe. If one walker were more than twice as fast as the other, they could catch the slower walker from behind before the slower one reached the end, and the argument would have to change. Here the first meeting shows that the speeds are in the ratio 5 : 7 (5 km and 7 km in the same time), and 7 is less than twice 5, so the shortcut is safe. Checking that the conditions of a shortcut hold is part of using it properly.

    General lesson

    When speeds are steady, distances are proportional to times, so you can reason about times by counting distances in a convenient unit, here the length of the road.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Proof and explanation, Transfer.

    Level 7, question 23 of 25 (problem 128 of 140)

    Question 7.23The hat in the river

    Difficulty 4 of 4: Olympiad

    A rower is rowing upstream on a river when her hat falls into the water and begins to float downstream with the current. She does not notice for 10 minutes. Then she turns round at once, rows downstream, and picks up her hat 1 km downstream from the place where it fell in. Through the water, she always rows at the same steady speed, and the current is steady. What is the speed of the current?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Imagine watching everything from a raft that floats with the current, right next to the hat. From the raft, does the hat move? How long does the rower take to come back to it?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    The current flows at 3 km/h.

    Understand the situation

    The rower's speed is not given, which seems to make the problem impossible. The real question is whether her speed matters at all.

    Find the mathematical structure

    • Choose a different point of view: watch everything from the water itself, as if you were floating along with the current. Seen this way, the hat does not move at all, and the rower moves away from it and back towards it at her own rowing speed, which is the same both ways.
    • So, seen from the water, she rows away for 10 minutes and must row back for exactly 10 minutes.
    • Meanwhile, seen from the bank, the hat drifts with the current for the whole time.

    Build the reasoning

    Step 1: how long the hat is in the water.

    10 + 10 = 20, meaning: minutes from falling in to being picked up

    Step 2: the speed of the current. The hat drifts with the current, and in those 20 minutes it travels 1 km.

    60 ÷ 20 = 3, meaning: lots of 20 minutes in an hour

    3 × 1 = 3, meaning: kilometres per hour, the speed of the current

    Check the answer

    Test with a rowing speed of 5 km/h through the water. Upstream she moves at 5 − 3 = 2 km/h past the bank, so in 10 minutes she goes 1 over 3 km upstream while the hat drifts 0.5 km downstream: they are 5 over 6 km apart. Rowing downstream she moves at 5 + 3 = 8 km/h and the hat at 3 km/h, so she closes the gap at 5 km/h and needs 5 over 6 ÷ 5 = 1 over 6 hour, which is 10 minutes. The hat has drifted for 20 minutes in all, which is 1 km. With a rowing speed of 10 km/h the gap and the closing speed both grow, and the return still takes exactly 10 minutes.

    Alternative reasoning

    Why the rowing speed cancels, seen from the bank. While she rows upstream, she moves one way at (her speed − current) and the hat moves the other way at the current, so the gap between them grows at her rowing speed. While she rows back, she moves at (her speed + current) and the hat at the current in the same direction, so the gap shrinks at her rowing speed. The gap grows and shrinks at the same rate, so the time back equals the time away.

    General lesson

    Choosing the right point of view can make a hard problem easy. Watching from the moving water removes the current from the rower's motion altogether, and with it the need to know her speed.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Model selection, Alternative solution.

    Level 7, question 24 of 25 (problem 129 of 140)

    Question 7.24Counting steps on the escalator

    Difficulty 4 of 4: Olympiad

    An escalator moves upwards at a steady speed. Asha walks up it while it is moving, one stair at a time, and counts 20 steps from the bottom to the top. Her brother Dev walks up the same moving escalator twice as fast as Asha (he takes 2 steps in the time she takes 1), and he counts 32 steps. Every step either of them takes moves them up exactly one stair. How many stairs would Asha have to climb if the escalator were switched off?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Measure time in "Asha-steps", the time Asha takes for one step. How long does each person's ride last in these units? The escalator lifts its riders the same number of stairs in each unit.

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    80 stairs.

    Understand the situation

    Each person rises the full height of the escalator. Part of that rise comes from their own steps, and the rest comes from the escalator carrying them. Dev walks faster, so he spends less time on the escalator and gets less help from it.

    Find the mathematical structure

    • Unit of time: one "Asha-step", the time Asha takes for one step.
    • Asha's ride lasts 20 units. Dev takes 2 steps per unit, so his 32 steps take 16 units.
    • Constant: the escalator lifts its riders by the same number of stairs in every unit of time. This is the escalator's rate.
    • For each person: their own steps + (escalator's rate × time on the escalator) = total stairs.

    Build the reasoning

    Step 1: compare the two rides. Asha rides 4 units longer than Dev but takes 12 fewer steps. Both reach the same top, so in those 4 extra units the escalator lifts Asha the 12 stairs she did not walk.

    20 − 16 = 4, meaning: extra units of time on the escalator for Asha

    32 − 20 = 12, meaning: stairs that Dev walked and Asha did not

    12 ÷ 4 = 3, meaning: stairs the escalator lifts in each unit

    Step 2: total stairs. Asha walks 20 stairs and is lifted for 20 units.

    20 + 20 × 3 = 80, meaning: stairs from bottom to top

    Check the answer

    For Dev: 32 steps of his own, and 16 units at 3 stairs per unit give 48 more. 32 + 48 = 80, the same total.

    A common mistake

    It is tempting to think "Dev walks twice as fast, so he takes twice as many steps". But he takes 32 steps, not 40. Walking faster shortens his ride, so the escalator has less time to help him. He must take more steps than Asha, but fewer than twice as many.

    General lesson

    When two effects act together, such as walking and being carried, write each person's total as the sum of the two parts. Comparing two such sums cancels what they share and reveals the hidden rate.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Model selection, Transfer.

    Level 7, question 25 of 25 (problem 130 of 140)

    Question 7.25Grass that keeps growing

    Difficulty 4 of 4: Olympiad

    In a field, the grass grows at the same steady rate every day, and every cow eats the same amount of grass each day. Starting from the same amount of grass, 10 cows would eat the field bare (including all the new growth) in 20 days, while 15 cows would eat it bare in 10 days.

    1. How long would 25 cows take to eat the field bare?
    2. What is the largest number of cows that could graze on this field for ever, without ever running out of grass?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Measure grass in "cow-days": the grass one cow eats in one day. How much grass did the 10 cows eat altogether, and how much did the 15 cows eat? Why are these different?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. 5 days.
    2. 5 cows. Five cows eat exactly as much grass as grows each day, so the field never runs out; with a sixth cow it would eventually run out.

    Understand the situation

    The amount of grass is not fixed: it keeps growing while the cows eat. So the inverse-proportion idea "cows × days = a fixed amount" cannot be used directly. Indeed, 10 × 20 = 200 and 15 × 10 = 150 are different, which shows that the total is not fixed.

    Find the mathematical structure

    Split the grass into two parts:

    • the grass already there at the start, a fixed amount, and
    • the grass that grows each day, a steady flow.

    The cows eat both. Measure everything in cow-days of grass.

    Build the reasoning

    Step 1: the total grass eaten in each case.

    10 × 20 = 200, meaning: cow-days of grass eaten by the 10 cows

    15 × 10 = 150, meaning: cow-days of grass eaten by the 15 cows

    Step 2: the daily growth. Both cases start with the same grass. The first case lasted 10 days longer, and the extra grass eaten must be the grass that grew during those extra days.

    200 − 150 = 50, meaning: cow-days of grass that grew in 10 days

    50 ÷ 10 = 5, meaning: cow-days of grass that grow each day

    Step 3: the starting grass.

    200 − 20 × 5 = 100, meaning: cow-days of grass at the start

    (a) Each day, 25 cows eat 25 cow-days of grass while 5 grow back, so the store shrinks by 20 each day.

    25 − 5 = 20, meaning: cow-days lost from the store each day

    100 ÷ 20 = 5, meaning: days

    (b) If the herd eats no more than grows, the store never shrinks. Five cows eat exactly the 5 cow-days that grow each day.

    Check the answer

    • 15 cows: the store shrinks by 15 − 5 = 10 cow-days each day, so 100 cow-days last 100 ÷ 10 = 10 days, as given.
    • 25 cows for 5 days eat 125 cow-days: the 100 at the start plus the 5 × 5 = 25 that grew.
    • With 6 cows the store shrinks by 1 cow-day each day and lasts 100 days, so 6 cows cannot graze for ever.

    Develop intuition

    Think of the field as a money box that starts with ₹100 and receives ₹5 each day, while each cow takes out ₹1 a day. With 25 cows you lose ₹20 a day, and the box is empty after 5 days. With 5 cows you take out exactly what comes in, and the ₹100 is never touched.

    Why "cows × days" fails here

    With inverse proportion you would say that 10 cows × 20 days = 200 cow-days, so 25 cows take 200 ÷ 25 = 8 days. But the second piece of data, 15 cows for 10 days, would then also have to give 200, and it gives 150. When two pieces of data disagree with a model, the model is wrong, not the data.

    General lesson

    When a resource grows while it is used, split it into a starting stock and a steady inflow. Two different situations give two facts, which are exactly enough to find both parts.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Model selection, Changed conditions.

    Level 8, question 1 of 10 (problem 131 of 140)

    Question 8.1Rent for the pasture

    Difficulty 3 of 4: Challenge

    Three farmers rent a pasture together for one season, for ₹6,500. Asha grazes 10 cows there for 4 months, Bilal grazes 12 cows for 5 months, and Chetan grazes 30 goats for 3 months. A goat eats a third as much grass as a cow. They agree to share the rent in proportion to the amount of grass their animals eat.

    1. How much should each farmer pay?
    2. Chetan has the most animals. Explain why he pays the least.

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Invent a unit for grass eaten: the grass one cow eats in one month. How many of these units does each farmer use?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. Asha pays ₹2,000, Bilal ₹3,000 and Chetan ₹1,500.
    2. The grass eaten depends on the kind of animal and on the number of months, not only on the number of animals. Chetan's 30 goats eat as much as 10 cows, and only for 3 months, which is the least grass of the three farmers.

    Understand the situation

    The rent pays for grass. Three things affect how much grass a farmer's animals eat: how many animals there are, what kind they are, and for how long they graze. We need one unit that combines all three.

    Find the mathematical structure

    • Combined unit: the cow-month, the grass one cow eats in one month.
    • Grass eaten = (number of animals) × (months) × (how much one animal eats compared with a cow).
    • A goat counts as 1 over 3 of a cow.
    • The rent is shared in direct proportion to cow-months.

    Build the reasoning

    10 × 4 = 40, meaning: cow-months for Asha

    12 × 5 = 60, meaning: cow-months for Bilal

    30 × 3 × 1 over 3 = 30, meaning: cow-months for Chetan, since 30 goats eat like 10 cows

    40 + 60 + 30 = 130, meaning: cow-months in all

    6,500 ÷ 130 = 50, meaning: rupees for each cow-month

    40 × 50 = 2,000, meaning: rupees for Asha

    60 × 50 = 3,000, meaning: rupees for Bilal

    30 × 50 = 1,500, meaning: rupees for Chetan

    Check the answer

    • 2,000 + 3,000 + 1,500 = 6,500, the whole rent.
    • Tempting error: sharing by the number of animals, 10 : 12 : 30. That would charge Chetan ₹3,750, more than half the rent, for the smallest amount of grass.

    General lesson

    When a cost depends on several quantities, combine them into one unit that measures what is really being paid for (here, cow-months). Then share in proportion to that unit.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Transfer, Error diagnosis.

    Level 8, question 2 of 10 (problem 132 of 140)

    Question 8.2Food on the mountain

    Difficulty 3 of 4: Challenge

    An expedition of 12 climbers sets out with enough food for 15 days, with each climber eating the same full ration every day. After 5 days, 4 of the climbers turn back. They take with them exactly enough food for their 2-day journey down at full rations, and the rest of the food stays with the other 8 climbers. Fresh supplies are waiting at base camp, so the 8 climbers do not need to keep food for their own journey down.

    1. For how many more days can the 8 climbers continue at full rations?
    2. The 8 climbers decide that they must stay on the mountain for 16 more days. To what fraction of a full ration must they cut their daily food?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Measure the food in climber-days. Follow it through each stage: what is eaten, what is taken away, and what is left.

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. 14 more days.
    2. They must eat 7 over 8 of a full ration each day.

    Understand the situation

    The food passes through three stages: all 12 climbers eat for 5 days; then 4 climbers leave and take some food with them; then 8 climbers share what is left.

    Find the mathematical structure

    • Unit: the climber-day, the food one climber eats in one day at full ration.
    • The food at the start is fixed, and each stage removes some of it.
    • In (b) the size of the daily ration changes too, so the food needed = climbers × days × (fraction of a full ration).

    Build the reasoning

    12 × 15 = 180, meaning: climber-days of food at the start

    12 × 5 = 60, meaning: climber-days eaten in the first 5 days

    4 × 2 = 8, meaning: climber-days taken by the climbers going down

    180 − 60 − 8 = 112, meaning: climber-days left for the 8 climbers

    (a)

    112 ÷ 8 = 14, meaning: days at full rations

    (b) At full rations, 16 days would need:

    8 × 16 = 128, meaning: climber-days

    Only 112 are available, so each daily ration must be cut to:

    112 ÷ 128 = 7 over 8, meaning: of a full ration

    Check the answer

    • Tempting errors: forgetting the food taken down gives 120 ÷ 8 = 15 days; forgetting the 5 days already eaten gives 172 ÷ 8 = 21.5 days.
    • (b) Eating 7 over 8 of a ration for 16 days, the 8 climbers use 8 × 16 × 7 over 8 climber-days, which is 112, exactly what is left.

    General lesson

    Follow a resource through every stage of the story, recording what is used, what is taken away and what remains. The final stage is then an ordinary inverse- or compound-proportion problem.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Changed conditions, Error diagnosis.

    Level 8, question 3 of 10 (problem 133 of 140)

    Question 8.3Pipes of different widths

    Difficulty 3 of 4: Challenge

    A round pipe 2 cm across fills a tank in 48 minutes. Assume that water always flows along a pipe at the same speed, whatever the pipe's width, so the amount of water a pipe delivers each minute is proportional to the area of its round opening.

    1. How long would two of these 2 cm pipes take, working together?
    2. How long would one pipe 4 cm across take on its own?
    3. A student says: "A 4 cm pipe is as wide as two 2 cm pipes side by side, so it fills the tank in the same time as two 2 cm pipes." Explain the mistake.
    4. How many 2 cm pipes, working together, deliver water as fast as one pipe 6 cm across?
    5. A 2 cm pipe and a 4 cm pipe are used together. How long do they take to fill the tank?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    When a circle is enlarged so that its width doubles, what happens to its area?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. 24 minutes. (b) 12 minutes.
    2. Doubling the width makes the opening's area 4 times as large, not 2 times, so a 4 cm pipe delivers as much as four 2 cm pipes.
    3. 9 pipes.
    4. 9 and 3 over 5 minutes, which is 9 minutes 36 seconds.

    Understand the situation

    Two ideas from earlier levels meet here. Pipes working together add their rates, and the rate of a single pipe depends on the area of its opening, which grows faster than its width.

    Find the mathematical structure

    • Combined rates: two identical pipes deliver twice as much water each minute, so they take half the time.
    • Area scaling: if every length of a shape is multiplied by some number, its area is multiplied by that number times itself. A circle twice as wide has 2 × 2 = 4 times the area; one three times as wide has 3 × 3 = 9 times the area.
    • Inverse proportion: the tank is fixed, so time = 48 minutes ÷ (how many times as fast as one 2 cm pipe).

    Build the reasoning

    (a) Two pipes deliver twice as fast.

    48 ÷ 2 = 24, meaning: minutes

    (b) A 4 cm pipe is twice as wide as a 2 cm pipe, so its opening has 4 times the area and it delivers 4 times as fast.

    48 ÷ 4 = 12, meaning: minutes

    (d) A 6 cm pipe is 3 times as wide, so its opening has 9 times the area. It delivers as fast as 9 pipes of 2 cm.

    (e) Counting in "2 cm pipes' worth", the pair delivers 1 + 4 = 5 times as fast as one 2 cm pipe.

    48 ÷ 5 = 9 and 3 over 5, meaning: minutes

    3 over 5 × 60 = 36, meaning: seconds

    The student's mistake in (c)

    Two 2 cm pipes side by side take up a strip 4 cm wide but only 2 cm tall. A 4 cm pipe is 4 cm wide and 4 cm tall. Think of squares instead of circles: a 4 cm square holds exactly four 2 cm squares, in 2 rows of 2. Circles enlarge in exactly the same way, so a 4 cm opening has the same area as four 2 cm openings, not two (even though four small circles would not fit neatly inside the big one).

    Check the answer

    • (b) is faster than (a), which makes sense: one 4 cm pipe is worth four 2 cm pipes, which is more than two.
    • (e) 9.6 minutes lies between 12 minutes (the 4 cm pipe alone) and 48 ÷ 8 = 6 minutes (two 4 cm pipes), as it must, because a 2 cm pipe helps but is weaker than a 4 cm pipe.

    General lesson

    Before scaling, ask which quantity actually controls the rate. Here it is the area of the opening, which grows like width × width. Then combine the rates as usual.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Model selection, Error diagnosis.

    Level 8, question 4 of 10 (problem 134 of 140)

    Question 8.4The water-filter jug

    Difficulty 3 of 4: Challenge

    A family can buy bottled drinking water at ₹20 per litre, or buy a filter jug for ₹1,200. The jug needs a filter cartridge costing ₹450, and each cartridge cleans exactly 150 litres. A new cartridge must be fitted before the first litre is cleaned and whenever the old one is used up. Cartridges can only be bought whole.

    1. What is the smallest whole number of litres for which using the jug costs less than buying bottles?
    2. Tara reasons: "With the jug, each litre costs ₹450 ÷ 150 = ₹3, so each litre saves ₹17 compared with bottles. The jug pays for itself after 1,200 ÷ 17 litres, which is about 70.6 litres, so the jug is cheaper from 71 litres." Show that Tara's conclusion is wrong, and explain which step of her reasoning fails.
    3. By the time the family has drunk 1,000 litres, how much has the jug saved compared with bottles?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    What does the jug option cost altogether if the family drinks only 1 litre? 70 litres? 100 litres?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. 83 litres. For anything up to 150 litres the jug option costs ₹1,650, and bottles cost more than that from 83 litres onwards (83 × ₹20 = ₹1,660).
    2. At 71 litres the jug option costs ₹1,650 but bottles cost only ₹1,420, so bottles are still cheaper. Tara treats the cartridge as costing ₹3 for each litre used, but the first cartridge costs the full ₹450 as soon as the jug is used at all.
    3. The jug has saved ₹15,650 (₹4,350 against ₹20,000).

    Understand the situation

    Bottled water is a pure rate: ₹20 for every litre. The jug has a fixed cost (the jug itself) plus a cost that rises in steps of ₹450, one step for each new cartridge. Comparing them means comparing a straight-line cost with a staircase.

    Find the mathematical structure

    • Bottles: cost = 20 × (litres). This is directly proportional.
    • Jug: cost = 1,200 + 450 × (number of cartridges), where the number of cartridges is litres ÷ 150, rounded up.
    • The cartridge cost is a staircase, not a steady ₹3 per litre. It works out at ₹3 per litre only over complete cartridges.

    Build the reasoning

    (a) For any amount from 1 to 150 litres the family needs exactly one cartridge.

    1,200 + 450 = 1,650, meaning: rupees for the jug option, for up to 150 litres

    Bottles cost more than ₹1,650 when:

    1,650 ÷ 20 = 82.5, meaning: litres

    82 × 20 = 1,640, meaning: rupees for 82 litres of bottles, still cheaper than the jug

    83 × 20 = 1,660, meaning: rupees for 83 litres of bottles, now more expensive than the jug

    At 150 litres, bottles cost 150 × ₹20 = ₹3,000 against the jug's ₹1,650, a lead of ₹1,350 for the jug. After that, each new cartridge adds ₹450 at once, while each further 150 litres of bottles adds another ₹3,000. So no single cartridge can wipe out the jug's lead, and the lead keeps growing.

    (b) Test Tara's conclusion at 71 litres:

    71 × 20 = 1,420, meaning: rupees for 71 litres of bottles

    The jug option costs ₹1,650 for any amount up to 150 litres, so at 71 litres the jug is ₹230 more expensive. The step that fails is "each litre costs ₹3": the family must pay ₹450 for the first cartridge even if they clean only one litre.

    (c) 1,000 litres need 1,000 ÷ 150 = 6 and 2 over 3 cartridges' worth of cleaning, so 7 whole cartridges.

    1,200 + 7 × 450 = 4,350, meaning: rupees for the jug option

    1,000 × 20 = 20,000, meaning: rupees for bottles

    20,000 − 4,350 = 15,650, meaning: rupees saved

    Develop intuition

    Imagine both costs drawn on a graph. Bottles make a straight line from ₹0, rising ₹20 for every litre. The jug starts at ₹1,650 for the first litre and stays flat until 150 litres, then jumps up by ₹450, stays flat again, and so on. The straight line crosses the first flat step at 82.5 litres, and after that the steps never catch up.

    Check the answer

    • At 82 litres bottles cost ₹1,640, less than ₹1,650; at 83 litres they cost ₹1,660, more. So 83 is the smallest whole number of litres.
    • (c) 7 cartridges clean up to 1,050 litres, while 6 would clean only 900, so 7 is right.

    General lesson

    A cost that is really paid per pack behaves like a staircase. Spreading it into a smooth rate per unit can give wrong answers, especially near the start. Model the pack the way it is really bought.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Error diagnosis, Model selection.

    Level 8, question 5 of 10 (problem 135 of 140)

    Question 8.5More people, more time?

    Difficulty 3 of 4: Challenge

    For each situation, decide how the time taken depends on the number of people: directly proportional, inversely proportional, not affected, or something else. State the assumption you are making each time.

    1. People painting a long fence together.
    2. A photographer taking a separate portrait of each person, one after another, 2 minutes per portrait.
    3. People at a table, each eating a bowl of soup at the same time.
    4. One teacher marking one exam paper for each student, 10 minutes per paper.
    5. Everyone at a party shaking hands with everyone else, one handshake at a time, each handshake taking 3 seconds. Find the time for 10 people and for 20 people.
    6. Invent your own situation in which doubling the number of people doubles the time, and another in which doubling the number of people leaves the time unchanged.

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    In each case ask: is a fixed job being shared, does each person bring their own piece of work, or does everything happen at once?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    • (a) Inversely proportional, assuming equally fast painters who do not get in each other's way.
    • (b) Directly proportional: 2 minutes for each person, one after another.
    • (c) Not affected: everyone eats at the same time (assuming there is enough soup and room at the table).
    • (d) Directly proportional: 10 minutes for each student's paper, because one teacher marks them one after another.
    • (e) Something else: the time grows faster than the number of people. 10 people need 135 seconds and 20 people need 570 seconds.
    • (f) For example: doubling the guests doubles the time to hand out name badges one by one; doubling the runners in a race does not change how long the race takes.

    Understand the situation

    The same two quantities, the number of people and the time, can be connected in completely different ways. The connection depends on what the time is spent on.

    Find the mathematical structure

    • If a fixed job is shared, more people means less time: inverse proportion.
    • If each person adds the same amount of work for a single worker to do, more people means more time: direct proportion.
    • If everything happens at once, the number of people does not matter.
    • If each person's work depends on how many others there are, as with handshakes, the time grows faster than the number of people.

    Build the reasoning

    (e) With 10 people, each shakes hands with the 9 others, and each handshake involves two people, so it would be counted twice.

    10 × 9 ÷ 2 = 45, meaning: handshakes for 10 people

    45 × 3 = 135, meaning: seconds

    20 × 19 ÷ 2 = 190, meaning: handshakes for 20 people

    190 × 3 = 570, meaning: seconds

    570 ÷ 135 = 38 over 9, meaning: times as long, which is more than 4 times

    Doubling the number of people more than quadruples the time, so this is neither direct nor inverse proportion.

    The assumptions matter

    • (a) assumes the painting can be shared without crowding. A hundred painters on one short fence would get in each other's way.
    • (b) and (d) assume one photographer or one teacher working at a steady pace. If a second teacher shared the marking in (d), the job would become a shared job, and the time would depend on the number of teachers as well.
    • (c) assumes enough bowls and chairs. With only 4 chairs, people would eat in batches, like the shirts drying on a short line in problem 4.5.

    General lesson

    Never decide a relationship from the names of the quantities. Ask what the time is spent on: a shared job, separate pieces of work, events that happen at once, or interactions between people.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Model selection, Problem construction.

    Level 8, question 6 of 10 (problem 136 of 140)

    Question 8.6The pigeon between the cyclists

    Difficulty 3 of 4: Challenge

    Two cyclists, Ravi and Sita, are 30 km apart. At the same moment they start riding towards each other, Ravi at a steady 10 km/h and Sita at a steady 20 km/h. At that moment a pigeon flies from Ravi's bicycle towards Sita's at a steady 50 km/h. When it reaches Sita's bicycle it turns round instantly and flies back to Ravi's, and so on, back and forth, until the two bicycles meet.

    1. How far does the pigeon fly on its first trip, from Ravi's bicycle to Sita's?
    2. How far does the pigeon fly altogether?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Instead of following the pigeon trip by trip, ask a different question: for how long does the pigeon fly?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. 21 and 3 over 7 km (about 21.4 km).
    2. 50 km.

    Understand the situation

    The pigeon makes more and more trips, each shorter than the one before, as the bicycles close in. Adding up all the trips one by one would be very hard. But the pigeon flies at one steady speed for as long as the bicycles are apart.

    Find the mathematical structure

    • The bridge is time, not distance. The pigeon's total distance = its speed × the time it flies.
    • The pigeon flies until the bicycles meet, and the bicycles close their gap at 10 + 20 = 30 km/h.
    • For part (a): the pigeon and Sita's bicycle approach each other, so the gap between them closes at 50 + 20 = 70 km/h.

    Build the reasoning

    (a)

    50 + 20 = 70, meaning: km/h at which the pigeon and Sita's bicycle close in

    30 ÷ 70 = 3 over 7, meaning: hours for the first trip

    50 × 3 over 7 = 21 and 3 over 7, meaning: kilometres flown on the first trip

    (b)

    10 + 20 = 30, meaning: km/h at which the bicycles close their gap

    30 ÷ 30 = 1, meaning: hour until they meet

    50 × 1 = 50, meaning: kilometres flown by the pigeon

    Check the answer

    • Bounds: the first trip alone is about 21.4 km, so the total must be more than that. The pigeon cannot fly for more than 1 hour, so the total cannot be more than 50 km. The answer is exactly 50 km, at the top of that range, because the pigeon never stops.
    • Units: km/h × hours = km.

    Why the trip-by-trip method is hard

    After the first trip the bicycles are 17 and 1 over 7 km apart, and the second trip is shorter again. The trips get shorter and shorter, and there are infinitely many of them. Their total is still 50 km, but adding them needs much more advanced mathematics. Choosing the right bridge, time instead of distance, avoids all of that.

    General lesson

    When a process is complicated in one quantity (distance), look for another quantity (time) in which it is simple. The cleverest step in a problem is often choosing what to calculate.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Alternative solution, Estimation and bounds.

    Level 8, question 7 of 10 (problem 137 of 140)

    Question 8.7The bakery's bottleneck

    Difficulty 4 of 4: Olympiad

    In a bakery, every loaf must first be shaped by a baker and then baked in an oven. Each baker can shape at most 10 loaves per hour, and each oven can bake at most 15 loaves per hour. Assume that shaping and baking can overlap, so that over a 4-hour shift the only limits are these hourly amounts.

    1. How many loaves can 6 bakers and 3 ovens produce in a 4-hour shift?
    2. The manager adds 4 more bakers, making 10 bakers with the same 3 ovens. How many loaves can they produce in a shift now? Why is the answer not 10 over 6 times the answer to (a)?
    3. With 6 bakers, how many ovens are worth having? Explain.
    4. What is the smallest number of bakers, and the smallest number of ovens, that can produce 300 loaves in a 4-hour shift?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Work out what the bakers could manage if the ovens were unlimited, and what the ovens could manage if the bakers were unlimited. Which limit is reached first?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. 180 loaves. The ovens set the limit; the bakers could shape 240.
    2. Still 180 loaves. The ovens were already the limit, so the extra bakers only wait for oven space. Scaling by 10 over 6 assumes the output depends only on the number of bakers, which is false here.
    3. 4 ovens. 6 bakers shape 60 loaves an hour, and 4 ovens bake exactly 60 an hour. A 5th oven would have nothing extra to bake.
    4. 8 bakers and 5 ovens.

    Understand the situation

    Every loaf passes through two stages, and each stage has its own capacity. The bakery can only go as fast as its slower stage, just as a bottle can only pour as fast as its narrow neck allows.

    Find the mathematical structure

    • Shaping capacity = 10 × (bakers) × (hours). Baking capacity = 15 × (ovens) × (hours).
    • Output = the smaller of the two capacities. The stage with the smaller capacity is called the bottleneck.
    • Adding to the stage that is not the bottleneck changes nothing. Proportional reasoning works only for the bottleneck stage.

    Build the reasoning

    (a)

    6 × 10 × 4 = 240, meaning: loaves the bakers could shape

    3 × 15 × 4 = 180, meaning: loaves the ovens could bake

    The smaller number wins: 180 loaves.

    (b)

    10 × 10 × 4 = 400, meaning: loaves 10 bakers could shape

    The ovens can still bake only 180, so the output stays at 180. Scaling by 10 over 6 would predict 300 loaves, which the ovens cannot bake.

    (c)

    6 × 10 = 60, meaning: loaves shaped per hour by 6 bakers

    60 ÷ 15 = 4, meaning: ovens needed to bake them all

    (d)

    300 ÷ 4 = 75, meaning: loaves needed per hour

    75 ÷ 15 = 5, meaning: ovens

    75 ÷ 10 = 7.5, meaning: bakers, so 8 bakers are needed

    Check the answer

    1. 8 bakers can shape 8 × 10 × 4 = 320 loaves and 5 ovens can bake 5 × 15 × 4 = 300, so the output is 300. With only 7 bakers, just 7 × 10 × 4 = 280 loaves could be shaped; with only 4 ovens, just 4 × 15 × 4 = 240 could be baked. Both minimums are needed.

    General lesson

    When a process has several stages, the slowest stage sets the pace. Before scaling one quantity, check that it is the one actually limiting the result.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Model selection, Changed conditions.

    Level 8, question 8 of 10 (problem 138 of 140)

    Question 8.8Does the wind cancel out?

    Difficulty 4 of 4: Olympiad

    A small plane flies from town R to town S, 600 km away, and straight back. In still air it flies at 300 km/h. Ignore the time spent taking off, landing and turning.

    1. How long does the round trip take with no wind?
    2. On another day a steady 60 km/h wind blows from S towards R the whole time. Pilot Arjun says, "The wind slows us down on the way out but speeds us up by the same amount on the way back, so the round trip takes the same time as on a calm day." Is he right? Find the round-trip time.
    3. Find the average speed for the round trip in (b).
    4. Is there any steady wind blowing along the route from S towards R that would make the round trip quicker than on a calm day? Explain.

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Find the time for each half of the trip separately. Which half takes longer, and how does each half compare with a calm day?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. 4 hours.
    2. No. The round trip takes 4 and 1 over 6 hours, which is 4 hours 10 minutes: 2.5 hours out against the wind and 1 hour 40 minutes back with it.
    3. 288 km/h.
    4. No. Any such wind makes the round trip longer. The plane spends more time flying slowly than flying fast, so the time lost going out is always more than the time saved coming back.

    Understand the situation

    The wind changes the plane's speed over the ground by the same 60 km/h each way. Arjun thinks the two effects cancel. But the trip is measured in time, and the slow half of the trip lasts longer than the fast half.

    Find the mathematical structure

    • Out, against the wind: speed over the ground = 300 − 60 = 240 km/h.
    • Back, with the wind: speed over the ground = 300 + 60 = 360 km/h.
    • Each half is the same 600 km. For each half, time = distance ÷ speed, and the two times add.
    • This is the "equal distances at two speeds" situation from Level 5, so the average speed is below the simple average of the two speeds.

    Build the reasoning

    (a)

    1,200 ÷ 300 = 4, meaning: hours for the round trip in still air

    (b)

    600 ÷ 240 = 2.5, meaning: hours out, against the wind

    600 ÷ 360 = 5 over 3, meaning: hours back, which is 1 hour 40 minutes

    2.5 + 5 over 3 = 25 over 6, meaning: hours, which is 4 hours 10 minutes

    (c)

    1,200 ÷ 25 over 6 = 288, meaning: km/h on average

    Why no wind can help in (d)

    On a calm day each half takes 2 hours. Compare different winds:

    WindTime outTime backRound trip
    none2 h2 h4 h
    30 km/habout 2.22 habout 1.82 habout 4.04 h
    60 km/h2.5 habout 1.67 habout 4.17 h
    150 km/h4 habout 1.33 habout 5.33 h
    300 km/hnever arrivesnot reachedimpossible

    On the way out the plane loses time, and on the way back it saves some. But on the way out it is slow for longer, so the wind works against it for longer than it works for it, and the loss always beats the saving. With the 60 km/h wind, the plane loses 30 minutes going out and saves only 20 minutes coming back.

    Check the answer

    The average speed, 288 km/h, is less than 300 km/h, which agrees with the longer time: 1,200 km at 288 km/h takes 1200 over 288 = 25 over 6 hours.

    General lesson

    Equal and opposite changes in speed do not give equal and opposite changes in time. When the distances are fixed, work with times, and expect the slower part of a trip to count for more.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Counterexample, Comparison.

    Level 8, question 9 of 10 (problem 139 of 140)

    Question 8.9How much can a helper help?

    Difficulty 4 of 4: Olympiad

    Two people work together on a job, each at their own steady rate. Working alone, the faster person would take a hours and the slower person b hours, so a is less than or equal to b.

    1. Explain why, together, they must take less than a hours.
    2. Explain why, together, they must take at least a ÷ 2 hours. When do they take exactly a ÷ 2 hours?
    3. Use (a) and (b) to say, without exact calculation, between which two numbers the time must lie when a = 6 and b = 10. Then find the exact time.

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Compare the pair with two simpler teams: the faster person alone, and two copies of the faster person.

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. The slower person adds some work every hour, so together they do more each hour than the faster person alone, and they finish in less than a hours.
    2. The slower person does at most as much each hour as the faster person, so together they do at most twice the faster person's hourly work, and they need at least half of a hours. They take exactly a ÷ 2 hours when both work equally fast, that is, when b = a.
    3. At least 3 hours and less than 6 hours. The exact time is 3 and 3 over 4 hours (3.75 hours), which is 3 hours 45 minutes.

    Understand the situation

    We are asked to prove facts that hold for every possible pair of workers, not just to work out one example. The argument must work whatever the numbers a and b are.

    Find the mathematical structure

    • Each person's work per hour is a rate: the faster person does 1 over a of the job each hour and the slower person does 1 over b. Since b is at least a, the slower person's rate is at most the faster person's rate.
    • Rates add when people work together.
    • Compare with two simpler teams: the faster person alone (time a), and two copies of the faster person (time a ÷ 2). The real pair must lie between them.

    Build the reasoning

    (a) The pair's hourly work is the faster person's work plus the slower person's work. The slower person's work is more than zero, so the pair does more each hour than the faster person alone. More work each hour on the same job means less time: less than a hours.

    (b) The slower person does no more each hour than the faster person, so the pair does no more each hour than two copies of the faster person. Two copies of the faster person would finish in a ÷ 2 hours, so the pair cannot finish sooner than that. The pair matches two copies of the faster person exactly only when the slower person is just as fast, that is, when b = a.

    (c) With a = 6, the time is less than 6 hours and at least 6 ÷ 2 = 3 hours. For the exact time, use a job of 30 units, because 30 is divisible by 6 and by 10:

    30 ÷ 6 = 5, meaning: units per hour for the faster person

    30 ÷ 10 = 3, meaning: units per hour for the slower person

    30 ÷ (5 + 3) = 3.75, meaning: hours together, which is 3 hours 45 minutes

    Check the answer

    3.75 hours lies between 3 and 6 hours, as (a) and (b) require. It is near the lower end because the slower person (10 hours) is not very much slower than the faster one (6 hours).

    Why this matters

    These two facts give a quick test for any combined-work answer. If two pipes that take 6 and 10 hours alone are claimed to take 2 hours together, the claim must be wrong, because 2 is less than 6 ÷ 2 = 3. If they are claimed to take 8 hours together, that must be wrong too, because 8 is more than 6.

    General lesson

    A proof shows that something must be true in every case, not just in the cases you tried. Comparing a situation with simpler situations on either side of it is a powerful way to trap an answer between bounds.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Proof and explanation, Estimation and bounds.

    Level 8, question 10 of 10 (problem 140 of 140)

    Question 8.10The queue at the ticket counters

    Difficulty 4 of 4: Olympiad

    At a railway station, people join the queue for tickets at a steady rate, and every ticket counter serves people at the same steady rate. When the counters open in the morning, a queue is already waiting. On a day when 3 counters are opened, the queue disappears in 30 minutes. On a day when 4 counters are opened, it disappears in 20 minutes. Both days start with the same queue and have the same arrival rate.

    1. How long would it take for the queue to disappear if 6 counters were opened?
    2. With only 2 counters, would the queue ever disappear? If so, when?
    3. What would happen with just 1 counter?
    4. What is the smallest number of counters that would clear the queue in 10 minutes or less?

    Not marked yet.

    Stuck? Show a nudge (it does not give the answer)

    A nudge for this problem

    Invent a unit: the number of people one counter serves in one minute. How many such units are served on each day? Why are the two totals different?

    Reveal answer and explanationHide answer and explanation

    Final answer

    1. 12 minutes.
    2. Yes, after 60 minutes.
    3. With 1 counter the queue never gets shorter: people arrive exactly as fast as one counter serves them, so the queue stays at its starting length.
    4. 7 counters. They take exactly 10 minutes, while 6 counters would take 12 minutes.

    Understand the situation

    The queue is not a fixed amount of work: new people keep arriving while the counters work. That makes this the same kind of situation as a field whose grass keeps growing while it is eaten.

    Find the mathematical structure

    • Unit: a counter-minute of people, the number of people one counter serves in one minute.
    • Split the people into the queue already waiting (a fixed amount) and the people arriving each minute (a steady flow).
    • On each day: people served = people waiting at the start + people who arrive before the queue disappears.

    Build the reasoning

    Step 1: people served on each day, in counter-minutes.

    3 × 30 = 90, meaning: counter-minutes on the 3-counter day

    4 × 20 = 80, meaning: counter-minutes on the 4-counter day

    Step 2: the arrival rate. The 3-counter day lasted 10 minutes longer and served 10 counter-minutes more. These extra people are exactly the ones who arrived during the extra 10 minutes.

    90 − 80 = 10, meaning: counter-minutes of people arriving in 10 minutes

    10 ÷ 10 = 1, meaning: counter-minute of people arriving each minute

    So people arrive exactly as fast as one counter can serve them.

    Step 3: the starting queue.

    90 − 30 × 1 = 60, meaning: counter-minutes of people waiting at the start

    (a) With 6 counters, the queue shrinks by 6 − 1 = 5 counter-minutes each minute.

    60 ÷ 5 = 12, meaning: minutes

    (b)

    60 ÷ (2 − 1) = 60, meaning: minutes with 2 counters

    (c) With 1 counter, the queue shrinks by 1 − 1 = 0 each minute, so it never gets shorter.

    (d) To clear 60 counter-minutes of people in 10 minutes, the queue must shrink by at least 6 each minute. So the counters must serve at least 6 + 1 = 7 counter-minutes each minute: 7 counters.

    Check the answer

    • 4 counters: the queue shrinks by 3 each minute, and 60 ÷ 3 = 20 minutes, as given.
    • 3 counters: the queue shrinks by 2 each minute, and 60 ÷ 2 = 30 minutes, as given.
    • Tempting error: "3 counters × 30 minutes = 90 counter-minutes, so 6 counters take 15 minutes." That treats the queue as fixed; the 4-counter day (80 counter-minutes, not 90) shows that it is not.

    General lesson

    A familiar model can hide in an unfamiliar story. A queue with steady arrivals behaves exactly like grass that keeps growing: a starting stock plus a steady inflow, found from two different situations.

    Thinking skills in this problem: Transfer, Model selection.

    Questions that often unlock a problem

    • What stays the same in this situation, and what changes?
    • What is the right “one” to work with: one item, one hour, one worker, one worker-hour, one gap?
    • How are the quantities connected? If one doubles, what happens to the other, and why?
    • Does the relationship really hold here, or is there a fixed part, a hidden count, or a limit?
    • What am I assuming (constant speed, equal workers, no breaks)? Is that stated?
    • Can I try a smaller or simpler version with friendly numbers first?
    • Before calculating: should the answer be bigger or smaller than the number I started with?

    Your progress

    Your marks are your own record: this page cannot see your written working, so it never decides whether an answer is right. “Answer opened” is recorded separately and never counts as completed.

    Quality report

    This page records what was checked, how it was checked and what remains uncertain. It keeps checks that were actually carried out separate from intentions.

    Problem count

    LevelProblemsDifferent perspectives used

    Total14015

    Every level has exactly the number of problems in the brief (15, 15, 15, 20, 20, 20, 25 and 10), making 140 in all. The build script refuses to produce this page unless these counts are exact and every problem has a complete solution.

    No multiple-choice questions

    There are zero multiple-choice questions. No problem offers answer options, and the page has no feature for picking options. The build script also scans every problem statement for multiple-choice patterns (lettered options, "which of the following", "choose one" and similar) and stops if it finds one.

    Thinking perspectives

    All 12 of the 12 perspectives in the brief are used, together with three further kinds of task (proof and explanation, insufficient information, and assumption checks): 15 kinds of task in all. Most problems train two or three of them.

    PerspectiveProblems using it

    Difficulty across the 140 problems: Foundation 8, Core 57, Challenge 58, Olympiad 17.

    Audit A: mathematical checks that were actually run

    • Independent recomputation of every problem. For all 140 problems, a separate program recomputes the key results from the numbers in the question, using exact fractions, and wherever practical by a different route from the written solution: step-by-step simulation (tanks, ferries, climbers, queues, shrinking teams), brute-force search over whole numbers (unknown rates, cheapest purchases, smallest numbers of workers, ovens or counters) or solving the conditions directly. 366 stated results were compared with these computations, and all of them agree.
    • Every line of displayed working. All 686 working lines were evaluated exactly, including every "=", "<" and ">", and every "≈" was checked against the rounding shown. None was skipped.
    • Arithmetic inside sentences. 220 further calculations written inside sentences were found automatically and evaluated.
    • Final answers. Each checked value was also looked for in the visible final answer, so the answer the student sees is the value that was verified.

    Audit B: wording and assumptions

    Every problem was read as a strict examiner would read it. Is every needed fact stated? Are steady rates and equal efficiencies stated wherever the model needs them? Are whole-number and rounding rules clear? Are the start and end of each process clear? Would another reasonable reading give a different answer? Where information is genuinely missing, the missing information is the point of the question, and the solution says exactly what is missing (for example 4.11 and 7.12).

    Independent blind solve

    Two separate solvers, each a fresh AI model that had not seen the course being written, received the 140 problem statements only: no nudges, answers or solutions. One took Levels 1 to 4 and the other Levels 5 to 8. Each solved every problem independently, using exact arithmetic, and reported its answers and every wording problem it noticed.

    • Final answers: their answers agreed with the course's answers for all 140 problems.
    • Wording: they flagged one serious problem (2.8(c), where the answer depended on whether pieces of ribbon can be joined) and about 30 smaller points where a careful student could read a question in another way, for example which bicycle was "the first" in 8.6, or whether "within 10 minutes" in 8.10 includes exactly 10 minutes. Every point was corrected or deliberately kept with a reason.
    • Second pass on the explanations: the same solvers then read all 140 written solutions and re-checked every number in them. They found no wrong final answers, but they found 7 errors inside explanations (correct answers with a false or confusing statement on the way) and about 30 places where an explanation could be clearer. All of these were corrected. They also found one display fault (stray asterisks would have appeared in 5.9), which led to a new build check: the page is not built if any markup characters would show on screen.
    • After all corrections, every automatic check in Audit A was run again on the final text, and all passed.

    Interface checks

    An automated test opened this file in a real browser engine (Chromium) and checked 73 behaviours, all of which passed on the final version. Among them:

    • all 140 answers are closed when the page loads, when a level opens, when a problem is chosen and after returning to a problem; every answer opens on a click and closes on a second click;
    • the closed answers cannot be found by the browser's find-in-page search, so searching for a word cannot open an answer by accident;
    • opening an answer is recorded, but it never marks a problem as attempted or completed; "attempted", "completed" and "answer opened" are counted separately, and "completed before opening the answer" counts only problems marked complete before their answer was opened;
    • marks and typed working survive a page reload; "Reset" asks for confirmation, "Keep my progress" changes nothing, and "Clear progress" clears everything;
    • the page makes no network requests at all and shows no script errors;
    • at phone width (375 pixels) nothing scrolls sideways, the level menu opens and closes, and the Escape key closes it;
    • with JavaScript switched off, all 140 problems are still listed and every answer is still closed;
    • in the worked solutions, the explanation of each calculation sits clearly beside it on a wide screen (lined up down each block of working) and below it on a phone.

    The screenshots taken by this test were also inspected by eye, in light and dark colours and at desktop and phone widths.

    Defects found and corrected

    Found by the author's own review, before the independent audit

    • 2.15: "3 cups every 8 seconds" could mean a machine filling 3 cups at once, which changes part (b) from 112 cups to 111. The machine now fills cups one at a time.
    • 1.13(c): asked how much paint to add, which has infinitely many answers. It now asks for the least amount.
    • 2.6: the cheetah's speed was not stated to be steady, and an early draft had an impossibly long sprint.
    • 4.3: stacking pieces would allow fewer cuts. Each cut now goes through one piece only.
    • 4.4: in the first draft the wrong method happened to give the right answer, so the trap was invisible. The numbers were changed.
    • 4.5, 4.7, 4.14: missing conditions were added (no time to swap shirts; toppings spread equally thickly; a fourth pair for Table D, with a note that answers beyond a table assume the pattern continues).
    • 7.6: the first draft said the tank was empty at 6:00 a.m. in both parts, which contradicted the 3:00 a.m. answer to part (b). Rewritten.
    • 7.22: the "three road-lengths" shortcut works only when neither walker is more than twice as fast as the other. The solution now checks this condition.
    • Level 7 titles that hinted at the method were renamed (for example "Reading the tank backwards" became "Two readings of a tank").

    Found by the independent blind solve (wording)

    • 2.8(c), serious: "How much more ribbon does she need?" had two defensible answers, 0.5 m and 0.7 m. It is now two questions: (c) how long one ribbon must be (10.5 m), and (d) the shortest separate piece (0.7 m), with an explanation of why the leftover scrap cannot help.
    • 3.5: "can buy exactly 18" could mean "can afford 18 but not 19". It now says the money is exactly enough, with nothing left over.
    • 3.10(h): days need not be whole numbers, so "count part of a day as a fraction of a day" was added.
    • 4.8: ages in whole years allow a gap other than 4 years. The sister is now born exactly 4 years earlier.
    • 4.10: 9 coaches in a 120 m train is unrealistic. It is now 10 coaches in a 240 m train (new answer: 12 seconds).
    • 6.8: painting 4 m² per person per day is unrealistically slow, so the painters became artists painting a mural.
    • 7.8: climbers sliding 200 m in their sleep was unrealistic. The story now uses the real practice of "climb high, sleep low".
    • Clarified: 6.11 (part-days of driving), 6.13 (the 6-hour working day stated at the start), 6.17 ("work alongside the 4 seniors"), 7.4 (how lateness is counted), 7.12 (neutral wording that no longer hints that the answer is "no"), 7.14 (equal daily wages), 7.16 (a clock face without a.m. or p.m.), 8.2 (food for the climbers' own descent), 8.6 (the cyclists are named; the bee became a pigeon, which really can fly at 50 km/h) and 8.10 ("in 10 minutes or less").
    • Smaller wording fixes: 1.12, 1.13, 2.3, 2.7, 2.9, 3.12, 4.1, 4.2, 4.6, 4.15, 4.19, 5.2, 5.3, 5.4, 5.12, 6.4 and 6.16.

    Found by the independent review of the written solutions

    • 1.7: two example situations broke the problem's own condition that 4 pens still cost ₹60. They were replaced.
    • 3.10: an explanation contradicted the corrected question. Rewritten.
    • 3.11: the "intuition" gave a reason that is false (the same reasoning would wrongly apply to direct proportion). Replaced by the correct reason: the product is fixed, so the factor is turned upside down.
    • 3.13: a ratio was turned the wrong way round in the check. Corrected.
    • 4.14: a note wrongly claimed that Tables A and B need no assumption beyond the data. Corrected.
    • 5.13: overtaking was described with the wrong start and end positions. Corrected, with the 300 m explained step by step.
    • 7.13: the solution claimed that its working used whole numbers when some rates were 2.5 and 7.5. It was reworked with a 120-bed garden, so every rate really is whole.
    • About 30 explanations were made clearer (for example 1.9, 2.2, 4.12, 4.20, 5.8, 6.8, 7.19 and 8.4), and a display fault in 5.9 was fixed.

    Found by a final examiner read of all 140 problems (after the independent reviews)

    Every problem and solution was read once more, start to finish, with each answer worked out again by hand. No wrong answers were found. Four wording points were corrected:

    • 6.7: the problem gave "men" and "women" different working rates, which builds a stereotype into the mathematics. It now uses experienced tailors and trainee tailors, with the same numbers and the same answers.
    • Level 6 introduction: the example "one man = two boys" was replaced by a neutral example about large and small pumps.
    • Level 5 introduction: "times never add" now says that times do add for stages done one after another. The quick check now says the combined time is no shorter than the fastest time divided by the number of workers, because it equals that value when everyone is equally fast.
    • 2.8(c): "how long would it have needed to be to make exactly 30 bows" allowed any length from 10.5 m up to just under 10.85 m. It now asks for the shortest length, 10.5 m.

    Found by the final visual check of the page

    • Working lines in every solution: the page had no layout rule for a calculation and its explanation, so they ran together on screen ("222 ÷ 6 = 37rupees for one notebook"). The calculation and its meaning are now set side by side in two aligned columns on wide screens and one below the other on phones. Four new browser checks guard against this and against the next fault.
    • Side menu: on a large screen its panel stopped short of the bottom of the window. It now fills the full height.

    Limitations that remain

    • A computer can check arithmetic, but it cannot prove that a model fits a story. Model choices and wording were checked by careful reading and by an independent solver, not by a machine.
    • The independent solver was a separate AI model working without the answers, not a human mathematician. Its agreement makes errors much less likely but cannot rule them out.
    • Some questions ask for judgement (for example "Which suggestion is fairer?" in 7.15) or for invention (the problem-construction tasks). Their explanations give one well-reasoned answer and say when other answers are acceptable; no checker can mark every possible answer.
    • Every model is an idealisation: steady rates, equally fast workers, leaks that do not slow down as a tank empties. Each problem states the idealisation it needs, and several problems discuss when such models stop being realistic (for example 5.3, 6.16 and 7.4).
    • The seven-level teaching guide mentioned in the brief was not included with it (its placeholder was empty). The course was therefore built directly from the brief's own specification, not checked against that guide.

    Clear your progress?

    This removes every “attempted” and “completed” mark and the record of which answers you opened, for all 140 problems. It cannot be undone.
